Dimer models on astroidal zig-zag graphs
Tomas Berggren, Alexei Borodin, Terrence George

Abstract
On a finite weighted graph, the dimer model is a probability measure on its dimer covers, also known as perfect matchings, that assigns to any cover a probability proportional to the product of the weights of its edges. For planar bipartite graphs, dimer correlations are encoded by the inverse of the so-called Kasteleyn matrix, which is a version of the adjacency matrix for the graph. The inverse Kasteleyn matrix for a large graph, typically taken as a finite domain in a periodic graph, is known explicitly only for a handful of examples. In all previously known examples, the Newton polygon – a convex lattice polygon that classifies periodic graphs up to local moves – is either a triangle or a quadrilateral. Our main results are the following. For any (minimal) periodic planar bipartite graph, we construct an ( n − 3 ) (n-3) -dimensional family of finite subgraphs for which we obtain an explicit inverse Kasteleyn matrix; here n n is the number of sides of the Newton polygon. Their boundaries are formed by zig-zag paths and their overall shape is reminiscent of an astroid; we call them astroidal zig-zag graphs (AZ graphs). If the Newton polygon is the unit square then the corresponding AZ graph is the celebrated Aztec diamond with its size as the parameter. Our inverse Kasteleyn matrices are given by a double contour integral on the corresponding spectral curve for any Fock weighting of the graph. This includes, in particular, all periodic weightings. For periodic weightings, we asymptotically analyze the resulting inverse Kasteleyn matrices. We establish a phase separation in large AZ graphs into asymptotically frozen, rough (liquid), and smooth (gaseous) regions, and obtain an explicit parametrization of the ‘arctic curve’. We also compute the deterministic limit of the height function, known as the limit shape, and prove the convergence of the local dimer correlations to the translation-invariant Gibbs measure of the slope predicted by the limit shape.



1
Introduction

The dimer model is a statistical mechanical model that studies random perfect matchings or dimer covers of a large graph. Via Thurston’s height function
[
108
]
, random perfect matchings on planar bipartite graphs may be viewed as random discrete surfaces with prescribed boundary conditions. Classical examples include lozenge tilings and domino tilings, which have been studied extensively from the points of view of combinatorics, probability and integrable systems; see Kenyon’s lectures
[
84
]
and Gorin’s book
[
64
]
for an introduction.

A fundamental result in the subject is the variational principle, proved for domino tilings by Cohn–Kenyon–Propp
[
38
]
and generalized by Kenyon–Okounkov–Sheffield
[
79
]
and Kuchumov
[
86
]
. For a periodic planar bipartite graph with periodic edge weights, and for suitable exhaustions by finite subgraphs, the associated random height functions concentrate, after scaling, near a deterministic
limit shape
characterized as the minimizer of an explicit surface tension functional. This can also be viewed as a variant of the Wulff construction
[
110
]
; see also Nienhuis
[
93
]
, Dobrushin–Kotecký–Shlosman
[
46
]
, and Cerf–Kenyon
[
30
]
.

The limit shape is highly sensitive to the choice of finite subgraphs, or, equivalently, on the boundary conditions. For example, the limit shape of a growing sequence of rectangles on the square lattice is constant, whereas for sequences of Aztec diamonds it develops distinct phases separated by a curve known as the
arctic curve
, as shown by Jockusch–Propp–Shor
[
71
]
. This raises a natural problem: how does one systematically choose sequences of finite graphs that exhibit nontrivial phase transitions?

The general variational theory is not accompanied by an equally general supply of concrete models with this behavior. Outside a small number of classical examples—lozenge tilings, domino tilings, and the Schur process/rail-yard graph models of Okounkov–Reshetikhin
[
96
]
and Boutillier–Bouttier–Chapuy–Corteel–Ramassamy
[
24
]
—there are no theoretical results or any simulations. The first main contribution of this paper is to identify natural finite subgraphs called
astroidal zig-zag graphs
(or
AZ graphs
for short) of an arbitrary (minimal) periodic planar bipartite graph exhibiting nontrivial phase transitions.

Once the limit shape has been identified, the next step is to study local and global fluctuations around it.
In the smooth part of the limit shape,
it is conjectured—and proved for the hexagonal lattice with uniform weights by Aggarwal
[
6
]
(see also Gorin
[
63
]
)—that the local statistics are governed by the translation-invariant ergodic Gibbs measure of Kenyon–Okounkov–Sheffield
[
79
]
whose slope matches that of the limit shape. It is also of considerable interest to study the local fluctuations near non-smooth points, such as regular points of the arctic curve as in Johansson
[
74
]
, Chhita–Dauvergne–Finn
[
33
]
, Huang
[
67
]
and Aggarwal–Huang
[
4
]
; turning points as in Johansson–Nordenstam
[
72
]
, Okounkov–Reshetikhin
[
94
]
, Aggarwal–Gorin
[
3
]
and Berggren–Bradinoff
[
13
]
; cusps as in Tracy–Widom
[
109
]
, Okounkov–Reshetikhin
[
95
]
, Huang–Yang–Zhang
[
66
]
; and at other special points as discussed in Astala–Duse–Prause–Zhong
[
8
]
. Likewise, understanding the global fluctuations around the limit shape remains an important open problem; see Kenyon
[
83
]
and Kenyon–Okounkov
[
81
]
. Kasteleyn theory
[
75
,
107
,
76
]
provides a powerful tool for tackling these problems through the inverse Kasteleyn matrix, which, by a result of Kenyon
[
82
]
, encodes all probabilistic information about the dimer model.

Explicit formulas for the inverse Kasteleyn matrix (equivalently, for the correlation kernel of the associated nonintersecting lattice path model) are, however, known only in certain special cases, including:

•

domino tilings of the Aztec diamond: the uniform case by Helfgott
[
65
]
and Johansson
[
74
]
;
q^{\mathrm{vol}}
and other one-periodic weightings due to Chhita–Young
[
36
]
and Chhita–Johansson–Young
[
34
]
; the two-periodic and, more generally, doubly periodic models studied by Chhita–Young
[
36
]
, Chhita–Johansson
[
35
]
, Duits–Kuijlaars
[
48
]
, Berggren–Duits
[
14
]
, Berggren
[
17
]
, Borodin–Duits
[
20
]
, Berggren–Borodin
[
11
]
, and Boutillier–de Tilière
[
29
]
; the double Aztec diamond of Adler–Chhita–Johansson–van Moerbeke
[
1
]
; and the split Aztec diamond of Shea
[
105
]
.

•

lozenge tilings of hexagonal and polygonal regions: the uniform hexagon by Johansson
[
73
]
;
q^{\mathrm{vol}}
-weighted hexagons studied by Borodin–Gorin–Rains
[
22
]
; polygonal domains studied by Petrov
[
100
]
; holey hexagons studied by Gilmore
[
61
]
; non-convex polygons studied by Adler–Johansson–van Moerbeke
[
2
]
; and periodic hexagons studied by Charlier–Duits–Kuijlaars–Lenells
[
31
]
and Kuijlaars
[
87
]
.

•

more general graph families related to the Schur processes of Okounkov–Reshetikhin
[
96
]
, rail-yard graphs of Boutillier–Bouttier–Chapuy–Corteel–Ramassamy
[
24
]
, and tower graphs by Borodin–Ferrari
[
21
]
and Nicoletti
[
92
]
.

The complexity of a periodic bipartite graph is captured by an invariant called the Newton polygon. All the cases listed above correspond to Newton polygons that are triangles or quadrilaterals.
By contrast, AZ graphs exist for arbitrary Newton polygons and, to the best of our knowledge, provide the first analysis of finite subgraphs beyond triangles and quadrilaterals.

The second main contribution of the present paper is a formula for the inverse Kasteleyn matrix for AZ graphs, generalizing the recent Aztec diamond formula of Boutillier–de Tilière
[
29
]
(which is a streamlined version of the formula obtained in Berggren–Borodin
[
11
]
). This formula allows for a detailed asymptotic analysis similar to that performed in Berggren–Borodin
[
11
]
for the Aztec diamond. In particular, we obtain an explicit parameterization of the limit shape, prove that local statistics are governed by the ergodic Gibbs measures classified in Kenyon–Okounkov–Sheffield
[
79
]
, and establish geometric properties of the arctic curve analogous to Kenyon–Okounkov
[
81
]
, Berggren–Borodin
[
11
]
, and Bobenko–Bobenko
[
19
]
. In this sense, AZ graphs provide a general new class of dimer models whose large-scale behavior can be described with a level of detail previously available only in a few special examples.

Although we do not currently have an exact sampling algorithm for random dimer covers on general AZ graphs, we show in the appendix that certain AZ graphs can be sampled via a tropical limit of the Aztec diamond. This yields concrete simulations that can be compared with our theoretical predictions.

As we were writing this paper, we learned that Boutillier–de Tilière–Deb
[
28
]
have independent work that derives a formula for the inverse Kasteleyn matrix for Speyer’s “crosses and wrenches” graphs
[
106
]
. We expect that our class of graphs overlaps with theirs but neither class includes the other.

:
A pentagonal lattice polygon
N
(left), a minimal graph
{\mathsf{G}}
with Newton polygon
N
together with a fundamental domain (middle), and a choice of isoradial weights (modulo gauge) where the angles are chosen evenly spaced around the unit circle (right). Here
\phi=(1+\sqrt{5})/2
denotes the golden ratio. See Figure

below for an illustration of the isoradial property.

Let us now describe our results in more detail. Let
{\mathsf{G}}
be a minimal
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-periodic planar bipartite graph, and let

\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}:={\mathsf{G}}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}

be the associated torus graph. We denote the black vertices, white vertices and faces of
{\mathsf{G}}
by
B({\mathsf{G}}),W({\mathsf{G}})
and
F({\mathsf{G}})
respectively. A
zig-zag path
of
{\mathsf{G}}
or
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}
is a path that turns maximally right at white vertices and maximally left at black vertices. Every zig-zag path of
{\mathsf{G}}
is a lift of a zig-zag path in
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}
. We write
\bm{\alpha}
for the set of zig-zag paths of
{\mathsf{G}}
.

The homology classes of the zig-zag paths of
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}
determine a convex lattice polygon
N
, called the
Newton polygon
of
{\mathsf{G}}
; see Section
2.2.3
. By a theorem of Goncharov–Kenyon, minimal periodic planar bipartite graphs are classified, up to certain local moves, by their Newton polygon. These local moves do not affect the statistical-mechanical properties of the model. Figure
1
shows an example of a minimal graph for a convex lattice pentagon.

Let
V(N)
and
E(N)
denote the vertices and edges of
N
, respectively, where by an edge we mean a side of
N
rather than a primitive edge vector. For each
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
, let
e(\alpha)\in E(N)
be the edge of
N
corresponding to the homology class of
\alpha
. For each edge
e\in E(N)
, define

\bm{\alpha}_{e}:=\{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}:e(\alpha)=e\}.

We say that the zig-zag paths in
\bm{\alpha}_{e}
are
parallel to

e
.

1.1
Astroidal zig-zag graphs

Figure 2
:
Two natural finite domains for the square lattice are the square and the Aztec diamond, together with their associated bipartite graphs. Only the Aztec diamond gives rise to a limit shape with a nontrivial phase transition. The images were generated using Leonid Petrov’s domino tiling simulator; see
https://lpetrov.cc/simulations/
.

Fix a convex lattice polygon
N
, and let
{\mathsf{G}}
be a graph with Newton polygon
N
. Our first main contribution is the definition of a natural class of finite subgraphs of
{\mathsf{G}}
that give rise to limit shapes with nontrivial phase transitions. To motivate this definition, consider the square lattice. Two natural families of finite domains are squares and Aztec diamonds. As illustrated in Figure
2
, only the Aztec diamond produces a limit shape exhibiting a phase transition. Our goal is therefore to isolate the features of the Aztec diamond responsible for this phenomenon.

{e(\alpha)}
{e(\beta)}
{e(\gamma)}
{e(\delta)}
\beta
\delta
\alpha
\gamma

Figure 3
:
The square Newton polygon
N
(left) and the Aztec diamond (right). The cyclic order of zig-zag paths along the boundary of the Aztec diamond is opposite to the cyclic order of the corresponding edges of
N
. The edges of
N
are colored according to whether the black or white vertices are outside.

First of all, we observe that the boundary of the Aztec diamond is formed by zig-zag paths whose cyclic order along the boundary is opposite to the cyclic order of the corresponding edges of
N
(see Figure
3
). This condition alone, however, is not sufficient: one can also construct rectangular regions satisfying it, but such regions do not admit dimer covers. This leads to a second requirement, which we call
admissibility
. These observations motivate the definition of the class of AZ graphs.

e_{5}

Figure 4
:
The construction of an AZ graph for the graph
{\mathsf{G}}
in Figure
1
. We choose zig-zag paths forming the boundary
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
(middle), and obtain
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
as the enclosed subgraph (right). The dashed polygon is an astroidal domain that
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
approximates.

An AZ graph is constructed by choosing, for each edge
e\in E(N)
, a zig-zag path
\beta_{e}
parallel to
e
, such that these boundary zig-zag paths appear in the cyclic order opposite to that of the corresponding edges of
N
. We write

\bm{\beta}:=(\beta_{e})_{e\in E(N)}

for the resulting collection of boundary zig-zag paths. They cut out a finite region of
{\mathsf{G}}
, and the resulting subgraph
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}\subset{\mathsf{G}}
is the portion of
{\mathsf{G}}
enclosed by the corresponding zig-zag boundary
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
. Each boundary zig-zag path
\beta_{e}
also determines a color of the edge
e
, according to whether the black or white vertices lie outside
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
. We now give an informal definition of AZ graphs; see Definition
3.2
for the precise definition.

Definition 1.1
.

The graph
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
is called an
astroidal zig-zag graph
, or
AZ graph
, if its zig-zag boundary is simple and it is
admissible
, meaning that for any face, the sum of the distances to
\beta_{e}
over black edges
e
is equal to the sum of the distances to
\beta_{e}
over white edges
e
.

Here the distances are measured using the
discrete Abel map

\bm{d}:W({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup F({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup B({\mathsf{G}})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{\bm{\alpha}},

which assigns to each vertex or face an integer linear combination of zig-zag paths recording which zig-zag paths we cross as we move from a fixed reference face
\mathsf{f}_{0}
to that vertex or face. In Lemma
3.13
, we show that every AZ graph has exactly four convex vertices, connected by boundary arcs that may contain concave vertices. Thus, these graphs resemble the astroid curve, which explains the term “astroidal,” while “zig-zag” refers to the fact that the boundary is formed by zig-zag paths.

In Proposition
3.10
and Corollary
4.4
, we show that among finite subgraphs whose boundary consists of zig-zag paths, one for each edge of the Newton polygon, in opposite cyclic order, the ones that admit dimer covers are precisely the AZ graphs. Thus admissibility is not an auxiliary technical condition, but exactly the condition for the graph to carry a nontrivial dimer model.

AZ graphs form a
(\#E(N)-3)
-parameter family. Thus, when
N
is a triangle, no AZ graphs exist. This can be seen directly: any finite subgraph bounded by three zig-zag paths, one in each direction, has unequal numbers of black and white vertices. When
N
is the unit square, the Aztec diamond is, up to size and local moves, the unique AZ graph.

1.2
Formula for the inverse Kasteleyn matrix

We next turn to the dimer model itself. The periodic graph
{\mathsf{G}}
is equipped with Fock’s dimer model, built from the spectral data of an M-curve
\Sigma
called the
spectral curve
, a point
t
in the Jacobian
\operatorname{Jac}(\Sigma)
, and an
angle function

\nu
assigning to each zig-zag path a point in a distinguished component
A_{0}
of the real part
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
.
As is customary, we identify a zig-zag path with the corresponding angle in
A_{0}
. We write

\Sigma(\mathbb{R})=A_{0}\sqcup\left(\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{g}A_{j}\right),

and denote by
\Sigma_{+}
the closure of one of the two connected components of
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
. We also write
\theta
for the Riemann theta function on
\Sigma
and
E
for the prime form. We recall the necessary background on M-curves, the Abel map, the theta function, the prime form and Fock’s dimer model in Sections
2.3
–
2.4
. For the purposes of the introduction, it is sufficient to know that out of this geometric data, one can construct a Kasteleyn matrix
\mathsf{K}
called
Fock’s Kasteleyn matrix
. Moreover, Fock
[
53
]
showed that this construction realizes all periodic weights on
{\mathsf{G}}
(see also Boutillier–Cimasoni–de Tilière
[
27
]
and George–Goncharov–Kenyon
[
58
]
).

We now describe the whole-plane inverse Kasteleyn matrices introduced in Boutillier–Cimasoni–de Tilière
[
27
, Section 3.5]
, which will be used both in the definition of
\mathsf{K}^{-1}
for finite AZ graphs and in the description of local statistics. For a black vertex
\mathsf{b}
and a white vertex
\mathsf{w}
of
{\mathsf{G}}
, there is an associated meromorphic form
g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
on
\Sigma
, lying in the kernel/cokernel of
\mathsf{K}
. For
\zeta\in\Sigma_{+}\setminus\bm{\alpha}
, set

\mathsf{A}^{\zeta}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}:=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C^{\zeta}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}},

where
C^{\zeta}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
is a contour from
\sigma(\zeta)
to
\zeta
, with
\sigma:\Sigma\to\Sigma
the antiholomorphic involution of the M-curve; see Section
2.4.5
.
Then, for each
\zeta
,
\mathsf{A}^{\zeta}
is an inverse of Fock’s Kasteleyn matrix on the full graph
{\mathsf{G}}
.

For
\zeta\in A_{0}\setminus\bm{\alpha}
, the matrix
\mathsf{A}^{\zeta}
depends only on the connected component of
A_{0}\setminus\bm{\alpha}
containing
\zeta
. These components are naturally indexed by the vertices
u\in V(N)
, and we denote the corresponding whole-plane inverse by
\mathsf{A}^{u}
.

We now state the main theorem of the paper which gives an explicit formula for
\mathsf{K}^{-1}
for AZ graphs. Since the dimer model is determinantal, this formula gives complete access to all local correlations on
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
.
The boundary conditions determine a divisor
\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}
, defined by recording, via the discrete Abel map, the signed distances from the reference face
\mathsf{f}_{0}
to the zig-zag paths forming the boundary of
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
. For a black vertex
\mathsf{b}
and a white vertex
\mathsf{w}
of
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
, we define

\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}(\zeta,\eta):=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\frac{1}{E(\zeta,\eta)}\frac{\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}+\eta-\zeta)}{\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}})}\frac{g_{{\mathsf{f}_{0}},\mathsf{w}}(\eta)\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\eta)}{g_{{\mathsf{f}_{0}},\mathsf{b}}(\zeta)\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\zeta)},

where

\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\zeta):=\prod_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}E(\alpha,\zeta)^{(\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}})_{\alpha}}

is the product of prime forms associated with the boundary divisor
\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}
. The key point is that admissibility is precisely the condition under which this kernel is globally well-defined on
\Sigma\times\Sigma
.

Remark 1.2
.

While we use the formalism of Fock’s dimer model to state the results in full generality, our results are already new and interesting in the case of genus-zero spectral curves. From the probabilistic point of view, this case is the familiar critical/isoradial dimer model; see Kenyon
[
78
]
and Kenyon–Okounkov
[
80
, Section 5]
. The genus-zero case is especially concrete: the theta functions are identically equal to
1
and the prime form
E(\alpha,\zeta)
can be written as
\zeta-\alpha
, so the kernel reduces to an explicit rational function. For example, choosing the angles to be evenly spaced around the unit circle for the pentagonal Newton polygon in Figure
1
(left) yields the isoradial weights shown in Figure
1
(right).

For each variable, the zeros and poles of
\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
partition the boundary of
N
into four intervals, alternating between zeros and poles. To each of
\mathsf{b}
and
\mathsf{w}
, we assign one of the two pole intervals, denoted

I_{\mathsf{b}}=[\ell_{\mathsf{b}},r_{\mathsf{b}}],\qquad I_{\mathsf{w}}=[\ell_{\mathsf{w}},r_{\mathsf{w}}].

We then let
C(I_{\mathsf{b}})
and
C(I_{\mathsf{w}})
be small counterclockwise-oriented contours around the corresponding angles on
\Sigma
.

The inverse Kasteleyn matrix is then defined by

\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}=\iint_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{b}})}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}+\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell_{\mathsf{b}}\in I_{\mathsf{w}}\}}\mathsf{A}^{\ell_{\mathsf{b}}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{r_{\mathsf{b}}\in I_{\mathsf{w}}\}}\mathsf{A}^{r_{\mathsf{b}}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}},

(1.1)

where
C(I_{\mathsf{w}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{b}})
denotes the product contour
C(I_{\mathsf{b}})\times C(I_{\mathsf{w}})
with the convention that whenever both contours contain small circles around a common angle
\alpha
, the circle in
C(I_{\mathsf{w}})
has smaller radius than the one in
C(I_{\mathsf{b}})
. This formula has the same general structure as many correlation kernels appearing in the dimer and integrable-probability literature: a double contour integral with a single-integral correction term whose integrand is the residue along the diagonal
\zeta=\eta
.

Theorem 1.3

(cf. Theorem
4.3
)
.

The matrix
\mathsf{K}^{-1}
defined by (
1.1
) is the two-sided inverse of Fock’s Kasteleyn matrix
\mathsf{K}
on
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
.

In particular, this immediately yields the following consequence which is not obvious from the definition of AZ graphs.

Corollary 1.4

(cf. Corollary
4.4
)
.

Every AZ graph has equally many black and white vertices and admits a dimer cover.

We obtained the formula (
1.1
) experimentally, guided by the corresponding formula for the Aztec diamond in
[
29
]
. The integrand has the same structure, while the contours and the single-integral correction term are more complicated. It would, however, be very interesting to find a direct derivation explaining this formula.

We now turn to the asymptotic consequences of Theorem
1.3
which are obtained by a steepest descent analysis of the exact formula (
1.1
).

1.3
Phase regions and the arctic curve

Figure 5
:
An astroidal domain for a decagonal Newton polygon, and an arctic curve for a choice of isoradial weights.

We now consider limits of AZ graphs as the size tends to infinity. Let
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
be an AZ graph. For each side
e\in E(N)
, we label the zig-zag paths in the family
\bm{\alpha}_{e}
from left to right as

\bm{\alpha}_{e}=\{\alpha_{e}^{i}:i\in\mathbb{Z}+\tfrac{1}{2}\},

choosing the indexing so that the reference face
\mathsf{f}_{0}
lies between
\alpha_{e}^{-1/2}
and
\alpha_{e}^{1/2}
. We then define

c_{\bm{\beta}}:E(N)\to\mathbb{Z}+\tfrac{1}{2}

by the requirement that

\alpha_{e}^{c_{\bm{\beta}}(e)}=\beta_{e}.

In other words,
c_{\bm{\beta}}(e)
measures the location of the boundary zig-zag path
\beta_{e}
among the zig-zag paths parallel to
e
.

Now consider a sequence of AZ graphs
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}},n=1,2,\dots,
such that for every
e\in E(N)
,

c_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}(e)=n|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}c(e)+O(1)\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty,

for some function
c:E(N)\to\mathbb{R}
, where
|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}
is the lattice length of
e
. In the limit, the admissibility condition for
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}
becomes

\sum_{e\in E(N)}|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}c(e)=0.

(1.2)

For each
e\in E(N)
, the boundary zig-zag path
\beta_{e}^{n}
has as its scaling limit the line

\bm{l}_{e}:=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:-b_{e}x+a_{e}y+c(e)=0\},\qquad e\in E(N),

where

(a_{e},b_{e}):=\frac{\vec{e}}{|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}

is the primitive integer vector in the direction of
e
viewed as a vector in the plane. The resulting collection of lines
(\bm{l}_{e})_{e\in E(N)}
determines a domain

\mathcal{D}_{c}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2},

called an
astroidal domain
.
1
1

1

Rote–Santos–Streinu
[
103
]
called similar-looking polygons

pseudo-quadrilaterals
, in parallel to the
well-established term of
pseudo-triangles
. They did not involve
the continuous version of the admissibility
condition (
1.2
), however. We are grateful to Richard Kenyon and Pavel
Galashin for pointing out the connection with
pseudo-quadrilaterals, and to Pavel Galashin for also drawing our attention
to
[
103
]
.
This domain is the scaling limit of the AZ graphs
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}
; see Figure
4
(right).

In Section
5.3
, we show that if a simple polygonal domain carries a continuous piecewise linear boundary height function whose slopes are prescribed by the Newton polygon, then it is an astroidal domain. In this sense, astroidal domains are exactly the domains compatible with the desired boundary behavior of the limit shape.

For the asymptotic results below, we assume that the angle function is periodic. This assumption is slightly more general than
periodicity of the edge weights; see
[
27
, Section 4.2]
. The asymptotics of the dimer model are governed by a meromorphic
1
-form

\lambda_{c}(x,y)

on
\Sigma
, called the
action
1
-form
, which is characterized as the unique imaginary-normalized meromorphic
1
-form satisfying

\res_{\alpha}\bigl(\lambda_{c}(x,y)\bigr)=-b_{e(\alpha)}x+a_{e(\alpha)}y+c(e(\alpha)),\qquad\text{for all}\penalty\ \alpha\in\bm{\alpha}.

The signs of the residues determine the locations of all but two zeroes of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
. The two remaining zeroes are either both real or form a
\sigma
-conjugate pair. This leads to a phase decomposition of
\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
: the
rough
(or
liquid
) region
\mathcal{L}
, where the remaining zeroes form a
\sigma
-conjugate pair; the
frozen
regions
\mathcal{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
, where the remaining zeroes lie in an open interval
(\alpha,\beta)\subset A_{0}
between consecutive angles with
e(\alpha)\neq e(\beta)
; the
quasi-frozen
regions
\mathcal{Q}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
, where the remaining zeroes lie in an interval
(\alpha,\beta)\subset A_{0}
with
e(\alpha)=e(\beta)
; and the
smooth
(or
gaseous
) regions
\mathcal{G}_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq g
, where the remaining zeroes lie on the oval
A_{j}
. The boundary between the rough and non-rough phases is the
arctic curve
, denoted by
\mathscr{A}
.

In the rough region there is a unique zero
\zeta\in\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
, and this defines a map

\Omega:\mathcal{L}\to\Sigma_{+}^{\circ},\qquad\Omega(x,y)=\zeta.

Proposition 1.5

(cf. Proposition
6.17
)
.

The map
\Omega:\mathcal{L}\to\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
is a diffeomorphism. Its inverse extends smoothly to a map

\Omega^{-1}:\Sigma_{+}\to\overline{\mathcal{L}}

which restricts to a bijection

\Omega^{-1}:\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\to\mathscr{A}.

Thus, the real locus
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
gives a global parametrization of the arctic curve. Figure
5
shows an example of the arctic curve plotted using this global parameterization for a decagonal Newton polygon and a choice of isoradial weights.

Several methods have been developed to compute arctic curves. One prominent approach is the tangent method of
Colomo–Sportiello
[
39
]
and its variants; see Di Francesco–Lapa
[
43
]
, Di Francesco–Guitter
[
41
,
42
]
, Aggarwal
[
5
]
, and Ruelle

[
104
]
. Another approach uses analytic combinatorics in several variables, particularly for dimer models arising from the
octahedron recurrence and
T
-systems; see Petersen–Speyer
[
99
]
, Baryshnikov–Pemantle
[
9
]
,
Pemantle–Wilson–Melczer
[
98
]
, Di Francesco–Soto-Garrido
[
44
]
,
Di Francesco–Vu
[
45
]
, and references
therein. In contrast, the inverse Kasteleyn approach gives access not
only to the arctic curve, but also to the local statistics throughout
the limit shape.

1.4
Limit shape

Let
h_{M}
be the height function on
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}
, normalized with respect to the extremal matching corresponding to a fixed vertex
u_{0}\in V(N)
. If
\mathsf{f}_{n}
is a face whose location is
n(x,y)+O(1)
, define

\mathfrak{h}(x,y):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{n}h_{M}(\mathsf{f}_{n})\right].

For periodic dimer models the existence of this limit is guaranteed by the variational principle. In our setting of AZ graphs, we derive an explicit parameterization for
\mathfrak{h}(x,y)
.

Define
A_{(x,y)}
by

A_{(x,y)}:=\begin{cases}(\alpha,\beta),&\text{if $(x,y)$ lies in a frozen or quasi-frozen region corresponding to }(\alpha,\beta)\subset A_{0},\\
A_{j},&\text{if $(x,y)\in\mathcal{G}_{j}$.}\end{cases}

Theorem 1.6

(cf. Theorem
7.9
)
.

For
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
, let
\zeta_{*}=\Omega(x,y)
if
(x,y)\in\mathcal{L}
, and otherwise let
\zeta_{*}
be any point of
A_{(x,y)}
. Then

\mathfrak{h}(x,y)=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta_{*}}}\lambda_{c}(x,y),

where
C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta_{*}}
is a contour from
\sigma(\zeta_{*})
to
\zeta_{*}
intersecting the component of
A_{0}\setminus\bm{\alpha}
corresponding to
u_{0}
.

1.5
Local statistics

Fix a finite graph
\mathsf{C}\subset{\mathsf{G}}
and translate it to a macroscopic point
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
by setting

\mathsf{C}_{n}=(j_{n},k_{n})+\mathsf{C},\qquad(j_{n},k_{n})=n(x,y)+O(1)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

Theorem 1.7

(cf. Theorem
7.2
)
.

Let
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}\setminus\mathscr{A}
. In the rough region, let
\zeta_{*}=\Omega(x,y)
, and in a frozen, quasi-frozen or smooth region, let
\zeta_{*}
be any point in the corresponding component
A_{(x,y)}\subset\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}
. If
(x,y)\in\mathcal{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
, assume in addition that

(x,y)\notin\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}\cup\bm{l}_{e(\beta)}.

For a black vertex
\mathsf{b}
and a white vertex
\mathsf{w}
of
\mathsf{C}
, let

\mathsf{b}_{n}:=\mathsf{b}+(j_{n},k_{n}),\qquad\mathsf{w}_{n}:=\mathsf{w}+(j_{n},k_{n})

denote their translates in
\mathsf{C}_{n}
. Then

\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}\rightarrow\mathsf{A}^{\zeta_{*}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty,

uniformly over
\mathsf{b}
and
\mathsf{w}
.

Corollary 1.8

(cf. Corollary
7.12
)
.

The local correlations in a neighborhood of
(x,y)
of the dimer model on
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}
defined by the Kasteleyn matrix (
2.5
) converge to those of the Gibbs measure defined by the corresponding whole-plane Kasteleyn matrix with slope
\nabla\mathfrak{h}(x,y)
.

Remark 1.9
.

If the edge weights of the dimer model are periodic, the Gibbs measures obtained in Corollary
1.8
are the translation-invariant Gibbs measures classified by Kenyon–Okounkov–Sheffield
[
79
]
.

1.6
Tropical limit and simulations

Figure 6
:
A simulation of the arctic curve in the tropical limit of the Aztec diamond (left) and the analytically computed arctic curve (right). The Aztec diamond has been vertically rescaled by a factor of
3/2
to match
\mathcal{D}_{c}
.

In Appendix
A
, we outline, via an example, how AZ graphs can be indirectly simulated inside the Aztec diamond by choosing a suitable tropicalization in which the tropical spectral curve is not smooth. This generalizes Berggren–Borodin
[
10
]
, which treats smooth tropical curves that do not leave any dimer randomness. Figure
6
shows one such simulation, containing an astroidal domain for the pentagonal Newton polygon in Figure
1
together with the arctic curve predicted by our theory. Understanding this phenomenon was one of the starting points for the present work, as it naturally led us to the notion of astroidal domains. Trying to understand their discrete counterparts in turn led us to AZ graphs.

1.7
Further directions

We conclude the introduction with a few directions that we believe are promising for further study.

•

It might be possible to give a direct proof that the limit shape of Theorem
1.6
solves the variational problem, in the spirit of Bobenko–Bobenko
[
19
]
for the Aztec diamond.

•

By construction, each edge of the Newton polygon contributes exactly one zig-zag path to the boundary of an AZ graph. However, there are natural domains that do not fit into this framework, for example lozenge tilings of a hexagon, or, more generally, the polygonal domains of Kenyon–Okounkov
[
81
]
, where a given parallel class of zig-zag paths may appear several times around the boundary. It would be very interesting to extend our constructions to this setting.

•

If we drop the assumption that the zig-zag boundary of an AZ graph is simple, we obtain graphs with branching as in Lai–Musiker
[
89
, Figures 46–49]
. As the number of edges of
N
grows, the subset of such graphs among admissible graphs grows, and we would hope that our results extend to this setting.

•

AZ graphs form a
(\#E(N)-3)
-parameter family. On the other hand, the cluster modular group of generalized shufflings also gives rise to a
(\#E(N)-3)
-parameter family, as shown by George–Inchiostro
[
59
]
(see also Fock–Marshakov
[
52
]
and George–Ramassamy
[
60
]
). It is therefore natural to expect that AZ graphs can be studied via shuffling, generalizing the relationship between domino shuffling and the Aztec diamond.

•

Is it possible to extend the results of Berggren–Borodin
[
10
]
from the case of smooth tropical curves to the non-smooth tropical curves in Appendix
A
?

•

It would be very nice to characterize which AZ graphs can be simulated inside the Aztec diamond as in Figure
6
and Appendix
A
. For instance, this procedure never produces a graph with self-intersecting boundary.

Organization of the paper

The paper is organized as follows. Section
2
collects preliminaries on the dimer model. Section
3
introduces AZ graphs and develops their basic combinatorics. Section
4
proves the exact inverse Kasteleyn formula for AZ graphs. Section
5
turns to scaling limits and introduces astroidal domains. Section
6
analyzes the geometry of the phase regions and arctic curve. Section
7
uses steepest descent analysis to derive the local statistics and the explicit formula for the limit shape. Appendix
A
explains how certain astroidal domains arise from a tropical limit of the Aztec diamond and provides simulations illustrating the theory.
