We are grateful to Cédric Boutillier, Béatrice de Tilière, and Bishal Deb for communicating to us their ongoing work
[
28
]
.
We also thank Cédric Boutillier, Béatrice de Tilière, and Marianna Russkikh for sharing with us the proof of Proposition
7.10
, and for allowing us to reproduce it here. We thank Gregg Musiker for drawing our attention to further examples of AZ graphs in the literature. A. B. was partially supported by the NSF grant DMS-2450323. A. B. and T. G. were also partially supported by the Simons Investigator program.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Conventions

Since we will simultaneously refer to vertices and edges of both the graph and
the Newton polygon, we use the usual font for objects associated with the
polygon and the sans-serif font for those associated with
the graph. Whenever it makes sense, we write

V(\cdot),\quad E(\cdot),\quad F(\cdot)

for the sets of vertices, edges, and faces of the object under consideration
(for example, for graphs, polygons, strands, etc).

Given a lattice polygon
N\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we orient its boundary
\partial N
counterclockwise. This induces a cyclic order on
\partial N
: for
u,v,w\in V(N)
, we write
u<v<w
if they appear in counterclockwise order around
\partial N
. By an
edge

of
N
we mean a side of the polygon (possibly containing interior lattice
points), while a
vertex
always means a corner. For
a,b\in\partial N
, we write

I=[a,b]

for the closed cyclic interval in
\partial N
from
a
to
b
, oriented counterclockwise.

We use the subscript
-
(resp.
+
) to indicate the object immediately clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) from a given one. For example, when we write

v=e_{-}\cap e_{+},

the edge
e_{-}
(resp.
e_{+}
) is the edge immediately clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) from
v
. Similarly, for an oriented edge we write

e=[v_{-},v_{+}],

where
v_{-}
and
v_{+}
are its clockwise and counterclockwise endpoints. We freely identify edges of
N
with the corresponding closed intervals in
\partial N
and with their displacement vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Thus we may write an
edge either as
e=[v_{-},v_{+}]
or as the vector
\vec{e}
.

For a side
e\in E(N)
, let
|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}
denote the
integral length
of the vector
e
,
i.e.
the number of primitive lattice segments contained in
e
, or equivalently
|e\cap\mathbb{Z}^{2}|-1
. Let

(a_{e},b_{e}):=\frac{\vec{e}}{|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}

denote the primitive vector along
\vec{e}
.

2.2
The dimer model

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a planar bipartite graph. We denote the black vertices, white vertices, edges and faces of
\mathsf{G}
by
B(\mathsf{G}),W(\mathsf{G}),E(\mathsf{G})
and
F(\mathsf{G})
respectively. If an edge
\mathsf{e}\in E(\mathsf{G})
is incident to a black vertex
\mathsf{b}\in B(\mathsf{G})
and a white vertex

\mathsf{w}\in W(\mathsf{G})
, we write
\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}
. A
dimer cover
on
\mathsf{G}
is a subset of
E(\mathsf{G})
such that every vertex is used exactly once.

An
edge weight
on
\mathsf{G}
is a function
\mathsf{wt}:E({\mathsf{G}})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Two edge weights
\mathsf{wt}_{1}
and
\mathsf{wt}_{2}
are said to be
gauge equivalent
if there is a function
\lambda:B({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup W({\mathsf{G}})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for every edge
\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}
, we have
\mathsf{wt}_{2}(\mathsf{e})=\lambda(\mathsf{b})\mathsf{wt}_{1}(\mathsf{e})\lambda(\mathsf{w})
.

When
\mathsf{G}
is finite, the
Boltzmann measure
associated to an edge weight
\mathsf{wt}
assigns to each dimer cover
M
the probability

\mathbb{P}(M):=\frac{\mathsf{wt}(M)}{Z},

where
\mathsf{wt}(M):=\prod_{\mathsf{e}\in M}\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{e})
and
Z:=\sum_{\text{dimer covers $M$}}\mathsf{wt}(M)
is the
partition function
. Gauge equivalent edge weights define the same Boltzmann measure.

Motivated by this, we define a
dimer model
to be a pair
(\mathsf{G},[\mathsf{wt}])
, where
\mathsf{G}
is a (possibly infinite) planar bipartite graph and
[\mathsf{wt}]
is a gauge equivalence class of edge weights.

2.2.1
The height function

Let
{\mathsf{G}}
be a planar bipartite graph. Each dimer cover
M
can be viewed as a white-to-black flow
\omega_{M}
on
\mathsf{G}
that sends
1
along each edge of
M
and
0
along all other edges. Fix a reference face
\mathsf{f}_{0}
and a reference dimer cover
M_{0}
. Following Thurston
[
108
]
, we define a function

h_{M}:F(\mathsf{G})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z},

called the
height function
of
M
, as follows. For any face
\mathsf{f}
, let
\gamma^{*}
be a path in the dual graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{*}
of
{\mathsf{G}}
from
\mathsf{f}_{0}
to
\mathsf{f}
and let

h_{M}(\mathsf{f}):=(M-M_{0})\wedge\gamma^{*}=\sum_{\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{w}\mathsf{b}}(\mathsf{e}\wedge\gamma^{*})(\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathsf{e}\in M\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathsf{e}\in M_{0}\}}),

where
\wedge
denotes the intersection pairing in the plane with the convention that

\mathsf{e}\wedge\gamma^{*}=\begin{cases}-1,&\text{if $\gamma^{*}$ crosses $\mathsf{e}$ from left to right},\\
1,&\text{if $\gamma^{*}$ crosses $\mathsf{e}$ from right to left}.\end{cases}

(2.1)

The height function allows us to interpret a random dimer cover of
{\mathsf{G}}
as a random discrete surface, which is sometimes a more suitable object for studying scaling limits.

2.2.2
The Kasteleyn matrix

The fundamental tool in the study of the dimer model is the Kasteleyn matrix which we now introduce. A
Kasteleyn sign
is a function
\kappa:E(\mathsf{G})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that
|\kappa(\mathsf{e})|=1
for all
\mathsf{e}\in E(\mathsf{G})
and the alternating product of
\kappa
around the boundary
\partial\mathsf{f}
of each face
\mathsf{f}\in F(\mathsf{G})
is
(-1)^{\frac{|\partial\mathsf{f}|}{2}+1}
. The
Kasteleyn matrix
is the
W(\mathsf{G})\times B(\mathsf{G})
weighted signed bipartite adjacency matrix defined by

\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}:=\sum_{\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{e})\kappa(\mathsf{e}).

Theorem 2.1

(
[
75
,
107
]
)
.

If
\mathsf{G}
is finite, the partition function satisfies

Z=\lvert\det\mathsf{K}\rvert.

A fundamental consequence is that the inverse Kasteleyn matrix encodes all local statistics of the dimer model.

Theorem 2.2

(
[
82
]
)
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be finite and let
\{\mathsf{e}_{i}=\mathsf{b}_{i}\mathsf{w}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}\subset E(\mathsf{G})
be a fixed collection of its edges. Then the probability that all
\mathsf{e}_{i}
occur in a random dimer configuration
M
is

\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{e}_{k}\in M)=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w}_{i},\mathsf{b}_{i}}\det(\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathsf{b}_{i},\mathsf{w}_{j}})_{1\leq i,j\leq k}.

2.2.3
Minimal periodic bipartite graphs

Let
{\mathsf{G}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-periodic graph embedded in the plane such that every face is a topological disk. Let
\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be the torus,
p:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}
be the quotient map and let
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}:={\mathsf{G}}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be the corresponding graph in
\mathbb{T}
.

A
zig-zag path
in
{\mathsf{G}}
is an oriented path that turns maximally right at white vertices and maximally left at black vertices. Equivalently, zig-zag paths can be viewed as certain paths in the medial graph. Recall that the (directed) medial graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\times}
has a vertex at the midpoint of each edge of
{\mathsf{G}}
, and an edge whenever two edges of
{\mathsf{G}}
are consecutive around a face. Thus we have a bijection

V({\mathsf{G}}^{\times})\cong E({\mathsf{G}}),

and we will use this identification hereafter without comment. We orient the edges of
{\mathsf{G}}^{\times}
so that they go clockwise around black vertices and counterclockwise around white vertices. Every vertex of
{\mathsf{G}}^{\times}
has degree four, and the cyclic order of the incident edges determines two pairs of opposite edges. A
(medial) strand
is a directed path in
{\mathsf{G}}^{\times}
with the property that whenever the path enters a vertex, it exits along the opposite edge (
i.e.
, it goes straight through the vertex). The bijection with zig-zag paths is obtained as follows. Given a zig-zag path in
{\mathsf{G}}
, record the sequence of edges it traverses; the corresponding strand is the path in
{\mathsf{G}}^{\times}
visiting the midpoints of these edges in order. The maximal-turn rule at black and white vertices is equivalent to the condition that the strand goes straight at each medial vertex. Zig-zag paths and strands in
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}
are defined in the same way. We say that a vertex
lies on
a strand if it lies on the corresponding zig-zag path.

We say that
{\mathsf{G}}
is
minimal
if:

1.

no strand is a closed loop,

2.

no strand has a self-intersection,

3.

there is no pair of strands
\alpha,\beta
(or, rather, zig-zag paths) that have two edges
\mathsf{e}_{1}\neq\mathsf{e}_{2}\in E({\mathsf{G}})
in common such that both of them are oriented from
\mathsf{e}_{1}
to
\mathsf{e}_{2}
.

Minimal graphs have no multiple edges, leaves or isolated vertices. Informally, the strands in a minimal graph “behave like straight lines”.

Every strand
\alpha
in
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}
is a cycle and therefore, has a homology class
[\alpha]\in H_{1}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. The unique convex lattice polygon
N\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, defined modulo translation by
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, whose sides consist of the vectors
[\alpha]\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
for all strands
\alpha
in
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}
in counterclockwise cyclic order is called the
Newton polygon
of
{\mathsf{G}}
. This construction produces a closed polygon because the homology classes of all strands in
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}
sum to zero: a strand has the same homology class as the corresponding zig-zag path and every edge of the graph is traversed by two zig-zag paths with opposite orientations.

Theorem 2.3

(
[
62
, Theorem 2.5]
)
.

For any convex lattice polygon
N\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, there is a nonempty family of minimal graphs with Newton polygon
N
. Moreover, any two graphs in the same family are related by sequences of the two types of local moves shown in Figure
7
.

Figure 7
:
Local moves: spider move (left) and contraction-uncontraction move (right).

These local moves do not affect the statistical-mechanical properties of the model like the limit shape or local limits.

We denote by
\bm{\alpha}
(resp.
\bm{\alpha}_{\mathbb{T}}
) the set of strands of
{\mathsf{G}}
(resp.
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}
).
Each strand
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
projects to a strand
p(\alpha)\in\bm{\alpha}_{\mathbb{T}}
.
To each strand
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
, we associate the unique edge
e(\alpha)\in E(N)
containing the homology class
[p(\alpha)]
.
We say that
\alpha
is
parallel to

e(\alpha)
.
For each edge
e\in E(N)
, let

\bm{\alpha}_{e}:=\{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}:e(\alpha)=e\}

be the set of strands parallel to
e
. The counterclockwise orientation of
\partial N
induces a partial cyclic order on
\bm{\alpha}
with respect to which two members of
\bm{\alpha}_{e}
for any
e
are incomparable.

Lemma 2.4

(
[
25
, Lemma 8]
; see also
[
62
, Lemma 3.4]
)
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a minimal graph, and let
\mathsf{x}\in B(\mathsf{G})\sqcup W(\mathsf{G})
be a vertex. Then the strands that
\mathsf{x}
lies on are parallel to distinct edges of
N
. Moreover, the cyclic order of these strands (or the corresponding zig-zag paths) around
\mathsf{x}
agrees with the cyclic order of the corresponding edges along
\partial N
if
\mathsf{x}
is white, and with the opposite cyclic order if
\mathsf{x}
is black.

2.3
M-curves

We briefly recall the basic material on M-curves that we will use, following the notation and conventions of
[
27
]
.

An
M
-curve
is a compact Riemann surface
\Sigma
of genus
g

together with an anti-holomorphic involution

\sigma:\Sigma\to\Sigma
such that the fixed-point set

\Sigma(\mathbb{R}):=\{\zeta\in\Sigma:\sigma(\zeta)=\zeta\},

called the
real locus
, consists of
g+1
connected components called
ovals
, each homeomorphic to a circle. We denote these components by
A_{0},\dots,A_{g}
. The real locus separates
\Sigma
into two connected components

\Sigma\setminus\Sigma(\mathbb{R})=\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}\sqcup\Sigma_{-}^{\circ}.

Let

\Sigma_{\pm}:=\overline{\Sigma_{\pm}^{\circ}}
denote their closures. We fix the orientations of the ovals
A_{j}
so that

\partial\Sigma_{+}=A_{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{g}A_{j}.

Let
B_{1},\dots,B_{g}
be dual cycles such that
\sigma_{*}B_{j}=-B_{j}
and

A_{j}\wedge B_{k}=\delta_{jk}

where
\wedge:H_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z})\times H_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is the intersection pairing. Let
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{g}
be the dual basis of holomorphic 1-forms on
\Sigma
such that

\int_{A_{j}}\omega_{k}=\delta_{jk}.

It is known as the basis of normalized holomorphic
1
-forms.

2.3.1
Abel map and Jacobian

The
g\times g
-matrix
\Omega
defined by
\Omega_{jk}=\int_{B_{j}}\omega_{k}
is called the
period matrix
. For an M-curve, the matrix
\Omega
is purely imaginary. The complex torus

\operatorname{Jac}(\Sigma):=\mathbb{C}^{g}/(\mathbb{Z}^{g}\oplus\Omega\mathbb{Z}^{g})

is called the
Jacobian variety
of
\Sigma
.

The
Abel map
with basepoint
\zeta_{0}\in A_{0}
is the map

\mu:\Sigma\rightarrow\operatorname{Jac}(\Sigma),\qquad\mu(\zeta):=\Big(\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta}\omega_{j}\Big)_{j=1}^{g}.

The Abel map is independent of the path from
\zeta_{0}
to
\zeta
because the integrals of
\omega_{j}
along cycles are contained in
\mathbb{Z}^{g}\oplus\Omega\mathbb{Z}^{g}
.

A
divisor
on
\Sigma
is a finite formal
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of points of
\Sigma
,

D=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\zeta_{j}.

Its
degree
is

\deg(D):=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}.

If
s
is a meromorphic section of a holomorphic line bundle on
\Sigma
, we define its divisor

\divisor(s):=\sum_{\zeta\in\Sigma}\ord_{\zeta}(s)\zeta,

where
\ord_{\zeta}
denotes the order of vanishing at
\zeta
.

We extend the Abel map to divisors by linearity,

\mu(D):=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\mu(\zeta_{j}).

By abuse of notation, we will henceforth suppress the Abel map from the notation, writing
\zeta
for
\mu(\zeta)
and similarly
D
for its image
\mu(D)\in\operatorname{Jac}(\Sigma)
.

2.3.2
Theta function and prime form

The
Riemann theta function
is the multi-valued function on
\operatorname{Jac}(\Sigma)
defined by

\theta(\zeta):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{g}}e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\langle n,\Omega n\rangle+2\pi\mathrm{i}\langle n,\zeta\rangle}.

Pulling back via the Abel map, we obtain a multi-valued function on
\Sigma
. By the same abuse of notation as in Section
2.3.1
, we will usually suppress the Abel map and write
\theta(\zeta)
for
\theta(\mu(\zeta))
.

The
prime form

E(\zeta,\eta)
is a holomorphic
(-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2})
-form on
\Sigma\times\Sigma
that is antisymmetric, vanishes simply along the diagonal with the expansion

E(\zeta,\eta)=\frac{\eta-\zeta}{\sqrt{d\zeta}\sqrt{d\eta}}(1+O((\eta-\zeta)^{2})),

and has no other zeroes and poles.

2.3.3
Meromorphic differentials of the third kind

We follow
[
51
, Chapter I]
. A
meromorphic differential of the third kind
on
\Sigma
is a meromorphic
1
-form all of whose poles are simple. Although Fay
[
51
]
states the construction for integral divisors, all formulas are linear in the coefficients, so they extend immediately to degree-zero divisors with real coefficients. If

D=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\zeta_{j}

is a degree-zero real divisor, meaning that
a_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
and

\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}=0,

then, after fixing a basepoint
\zeta_{0}\in\Sigma
, there exists a unique meromorphic
1
-form
\omega_{D}
with vanishing
A
-periods,

\int_{A_{k}}\omega_{D}=0,\qquad 1\leq k\leq g,

whose only singularities are simple poles at the points
\zeta_{j}
, with residues

\res_{\zeta_{j}}(\omega_{D})=a_{j}.

It is given by

\omega_{D}(\zeta):=d\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\log\left(\frac{E(\zeta_{j},\zeta)}{E(\zeta_{0},\zeta)}\right);

(2.2)

here
d
is the differential. By the
Riemann bilinear relations
, for
1\leq k\leq g
,

\int_{B_{k}}\omega_{D}=2\pi\mathrm{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta_{j}}\omega_{k}.

(2.3)

2.3.4
Imaginary-normalized differentials

Instead of normalizing a meromorphic differential of the third kind to have vanishing
A
-periods, we may normalize it by requiring all of its periods to be purely imaginary. We say that a meromorphic
1
-form is
imaginary-normalized
if

\Re\int_{\gamma}\omega=0

for every cycle
\gamma
in
\Sigma
.

Lemma 2.5

(
[
85
, Lemma 2.1]
)
.

Let
D=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\zeta_{j}
be a degree-
0
real divisor on
\Sigma
. Then there exists a unique imaginary-normalized meromorphic
1
-form on
\Sigma
whose only singularities are simple poles at the points
\zeta_{j}
with residues
a_{j}
.

Recall the meromorphic
1
-forms
\omega_{D}
associated to degree-
0
divisors
D
with real coefficients defined in Section
2.3.3
. We now show that, when the support of
D
lies on
A_{0}
, this differential is also imaginary-normalized.

Lemma 2.6
.

For any degree-
0
real divisor
D=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\zeta_{j}
such that
\zeta_{j}\in A_{0}
, the
1
-form
\omega_{D}
is imaginary-normalized and satisfies
\overline{\sigma^{*}\omega_{D}}=\omega_{D}
; in particular,
\omega_{D}
is real-valued on
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

Since the
A
-periods of
\omega_{D}
vanish by definition, it remains only to show that the
B
-periods are purely imaginary. By the Riemann bilinear relations (
2.3
),

\int_{B_{k}}\omega_{D}=2\pi\mathrm{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta_{j}}\omega_{k},

which is purely imaginary since
\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta_{j}}\omega_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
for any
\zeta_{j}\in A_{0}
by
[
27
, Lemma 14]
as
\zeta_{0}\in A_{0}
.

The
1
-form
\overline{\sigma^{*}\omega_{D}}
is imaginary-normalized since

\Re\int_{\gamma}\overline{\sigma^{*}\omega_{D}}=\Re\int_{\sigma_{*}(\gamma)}\omega_{D}=0.

Since
A_{0}
is fixed by
\sigma
,
\overline{\sigma^{*}\omega_{D}}
has the same poles as
\omega_{D}
. To see that the residues are also the same, let
C
be a small counterclockwise-oriented circle around
\alpha
. Then
\sigma_{*}C=-C
, so

\res_{\alpha}(\overline{\sigma^{*}\omega_{D}})=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C}\overline{\sigma^{*}\omega_{D}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\overline{\int_{-C}\omega_{D}}=\res_{\alpha}(\omega_{D}).

By Lemma
2.5
,
\overline{\sigma^{*}\omega_{D}}=\omega_{D}
.
∎

Throughout the paper, we will count zeroes with multiplicity.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
D=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\zeta_{j}
be a degree-
0
real divisor such that
a_{j}\neq 0
,

\zeta_{j}\in A_{0}
for all
j
, and index the points

\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{n}
cyclically along
A_{0}
. Then the differential

\omega_{D}
satisfies:

1.

for each
1\leq k\leq g
, the number of zeroes of
\omega_{D}
on
A_{k}
is even and at least
2
,

2.

for each interval
(\zeta_{j},\zeta_{j+1})\subset A_{0}
, the number of zeroes
of
\omega_{D}
in
(\zeta_{j},\zeta_{j+1})
is odd
if
\res_{\zeta_{j}}(\omega_{D})
and
\res_{\zeta_{j+1}}(\omega_{D})
have the same sign,
and even if they have opposite signs. In particular,
\omega_{D}
has at least one
zero in
(\zeta_{j},\zeta_{j+1})
whenever the residues at
\zeta_{j}
and

\zeta_{j+1}
have the same sign.

Proof.

Both assertions are consequences of the real-valuedness of
\omega_{D}
on

\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
(Lemma
2.6
).

Let
1\leq k\leq g
. Since
\omega_{D}
is imaginary-normalized, we have

\int_{A_{k}}\omega_{D}=0.

Since
\omega_{D}
is not identically zero on
A_{k}
, it cannot have constant sign on

A_{k}
. Thus, it must change sign, which forces the existence of at least two zeroes on

A_{k}
. Since sign changes correspond to zeroes of odd multiplicity and the total number of sign changes is even, the total number of zeroes counted with multiplicity is even.

Now consider an interval
(\zeta_{j},\zeta_{j+1})\subset A_{0}
. Since
\omega_{D}
is
real-valued on this interval, the parity of the number of zeroes is determined by its
sign near the endpoints. Near
\zeta_{j}
, the sign of
\omega_{D}
on the interval is
the sign of
\res_{\zeta_{j}}(\omega_{D})
, while near
\zeta_{j+1}
, it is the opposite
of the sign of
\res_{\zeta_{j+1}}(\omega_{D})
. Therefore, these endpoint signs are
opposite precisely when
\res_{\zeta_{j}}(\omega_{D})
and

\res_{\zeta_{j+1}}(\omega_{D})
have the same sign. In that case the number of zeroes
in
(\zeta_{j},\zeta_{j+1})
is odd, hence, at least one. If the residues have opposite
signs, then the endpoint signs agree, so the number of zeroes is even.
∎

2.4
Fock’s dimer model

In this subsection we recall Fock’s construction of a dimer model from data associated to an M-curve following
[
53
,
27
]
.

2.4.1
Angle functions

Let
\Sigma
be an M-curve. We call a function
\nu:\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow A_{0}
an
angle
function if it is compatible with the partial cyclic order on
\bm{\alpha}
and the total cyclic order on
A_{0}
, and it maps non-parallel strands to distinct points. By a customary abuse of notation, we identify the angle
\nu(\alpha)
with the strand
\alpha
.

2.4.2
The discrete Abel map

Let
\Sigma
be an M-curve and let

\nu

be an angle function. Via
\nu
, we henceforth identify
\mathbb{Z}^{\bm{\alpha}}
with the group of divisors supported on
\nu(\bm{\alpha})\subset A_{0}
. In particular, for
\bm{D}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\bm{\alpha}}
, we define its
degree
to be the degree of the corresponding divisor.

For
\bm{D}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\bm{\alpha}}
and
e\in E(N)
, let

\bm{D}_{\bm{\alpha}_{e}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\bm{\alpha}_{e}}

denote the restriction of
\bm{D}
to
\bm{\alpha}_{e}
, so that

\bm{D}=\sum_{e\in E(N)}\bm{D}_{\bm{\alpha}_{e}}.

For
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
, let

\bm{D}_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{Z}

denote the restriction of
\bm{D}
to
\alpha
; we identify this with the coefficient of
\alpha
in
\bm{D}
.

The
discrete Abel map

\bm{d}:W({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup F({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup B({\mathsf{G}})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{\bm{\alpha}}

is defined as follows:

1.

Choose a reference face
\mathsf{f}_{0}
and set
\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{0})=0
.

2.

For a white vertex
\mathsf{w}
incident to a face
\mathsf{f}
such that they are separated by a strand
\alpha
, we have
\bm{d}(\mathsf{w})=\bm{d}(\mathsf{f})-\alpha
.

3.

For a black vertex
\mathsf{b}
incident to a face
\mathsf{f}
such that they are separated by a strand
\alpha
, we have
\bm{d}(\mathsf{b})=\bm{d}(\mathsf{f})+\alpha
.

Equivalently,

\bm{d}(\mathsf{y})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{x})=\sum_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}(\gamma\wedge\alpha)\alpha,

(2.4)

where
\gamma
is any path from
\mathsf{x}
to
\mathsf{y}
that intersects all strands transversely and
\wedge
denotes the intersection pairing in the plane with the same convention as in (
2.1
).
By definition,
\deg(\bm{d}(\mathsf{x}))
is
-1
if
\mathsf{x}
is a white vertex,
0
if
\mathsf{x}
is a face and
1
if
\mathsf{x}
is a black vertex.

2.4.3
Fock’s Kasteleyn matrix

\mathsf{w}
\mathsf{b}
\mathsf{f}_{+}
\mathsf{f}_{-}
\alpha
\beta

Figure 8
:
Local configuration near an edge in the definition of Fock’s Kasteleyn matrix
\mathsf{K}
.

Given an M-curve
\Sigma
, a point
t\in\mathbb{R}^{g}/\mathbb{Z}^{g}\subset\operatorname{Jac}(\Sigma)
and an angle function
\nu
,
Fock’s Kasteleyn matrix

\mathsf{K}
is defined by

\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}:=\begin{cases}\dfrac{E(\alpha,\beta)}{\theta(t+\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{-}))\theta(t+\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{+}))},&\text{if $\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}$ is an edge},\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(2.5)

where
\alpha,\beta
are the two strands containing
\mathsf{e}
, and
\mathsf{f}_{-},\mathsf{f}_{+}
are the two faces incident to
\mathsf{e}
(Figure
8
).

Proposition 2.8

(
[
27
, Proposition 3]
)
.

Fock’s Kasteleyn matrix is the Kasteleyn matrix of a dimer model
(\mathsf{G},[\mathsf{wt}])
.

2.4.4
Forms in the kernel of
\mathsf{K}

Following
[
53
]
(see also
[
80
]
), we define some differential forms on
\Sigma
that will be part of the integrand in the integral formula for
\mathsf{K}^{-1}
. When
\mathsf{x}\in W({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup F({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup B({\mathsf{G}})
, define
g_{\mathsf{x}}
as follows:

1.

Set
g_{\mathsf{f}_{0}}(\zeta)\equiv 1
.

2.

For a white vertex
\mathsf{w}
incident to a face
\mathsf{f}
such that they are separated by a strand
\alpha
, we have

g_{\mathsf{w}}(\zeta)=\frac{\theta(t+\zeta+\bm{d}(\mathsf{w}))}{E(\alpha,\zeta)}g_{\mathsf{f}}(\zeta).

3.

For a black vertex
\mathsf{b}
incident to a face
\mathsf{f}
such that they are separated by a strand
\alpha
, we have

g_{\mathsf{b}}(\zeta)=\frac{E(\alpha,\zeta)}{\theta(-t+\zeta-\bm{d}(\mathsf{b}))}g_{\mathsf{f}}(\zeta).

More generally, for
\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\in W({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup F({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup B({\mathsf{G}})
, define

g_{\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}}:=\frac{g_{\mathsf{y}}}{g_{\mathsf{x}}}.

(2.6)

In particular,
g_{\mathsf{x}}=g_{\mathsf{f}_{0},\mathsf{x}}
.

Lemma 2.9

(
[
27
, Lemma 13]
; see also
[
80
]
)
.

For any white vertex
\mathsf{w}\in W({\mathsf{G}})
, we have

\sum_{\mathsf{b}\sim\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{x}}=0

(2.7)

for any
\mathsf{x}\neq\mathsf{w}\in W({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup F({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup B({\mathsf{G}})
.

Lemma 2.10

(
[
51
, Proposition 2.10]
and
[
27
, Equation (28)]
)
.

Let
\mathsf{w}\in W({\mathsf{G}})
and
\mathsf{b}\in B({\mathsf{G}})
be adjacent white and black vertices. Then

\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}=\omega_{\beta-\alpha}+\sum_{k=1}^{g}\left(\frac{\partial\log\theta}{\partial z_{k}}(t+\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{+}))-\frac{\partial\log\theta}{\partial z_{k}}(t+\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{-}))\right)\omega_{k},

(2.8)

where
\alpha
and
\beta
are the strands containing the edge
\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}
, and
\mathsf{f}_{+}
and
\mathsf{f}_{-}
are the adjacent faces as in Figure
8
.

We now describe the structure of the zeroes and poles of
g_{\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}}
along
A_{0}
.

Lemma 2.11

(
[
27
, Section 3.4]
)
.

Let
\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\in W({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup F({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup B({\mathsf{G}})
. Then

\divisor(g_{\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}})|_{A_{0}}=\bm{d}(\mathsf{y})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{x}).

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\in W({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup F({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup B({\mathsf{G}})
. Then
g_{\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}}
cannot have both zeroes and poles at angles in
\bm{\alpha}_{e}
for any
e\in E(N)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.11
, we just need to check that for any
e\in E(N)
, there is a path
\gamma
from
\mathsf{x}
to
\mathsf{y}
whose intersections with
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{e}
all have the same orientation. By minimality, the strands in
\bm{\alpha}_{e}
are pairwise disjoint and hence, they divide the plane into an ordered sequence of
strips between consecutive strands. Among all paths from
\mathsf{x}
to
\mathsf{y}
, choose
\gamma
transverse to those strands and minimizing the number of intersections with

\bm{\alpha}_{e}
. Then
\gamma
cannot go back and forth along strips, so it crosses every strand
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{e}
between
\mathsf{x}
and
\mathsf{y}
with the same orientation exactly once.
∎

Lemma 2.13

(
[
26
, Lemma 20]
; see also
[
27
, Lemma 34]
)
.

Let
\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\in W({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup F({\mathsf{G}})\sqcup B({\mathsf{G}})
.
As we move cyclically along
\partial N
, the edges corresponding to angles at which

g_{\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}}
has zeros and the edges corresponding to angles at which it has poles do not interlace.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
\mathsf{b}\in B({\mathsf{G}})
and
\mathsf{w}\in W({\mathsf{G}})
. We define the
null sector

\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\subset\partial N

as follows.

1.

Suppose
g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
has at least one zero in
A_{0}
. By Lemma
2.13
, the zeros and poles of
g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
along
\partial N
do not interlace. We then define
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
to be the maximal closed connected interval of
\partial N
which contains every edge
e
such that
g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
has a zero at some angle
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{e}
, and contains no edge
e
such that
g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
has a pole at some angle
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{e}
.

2.

Suppose
g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
has no zeros in
A_{0}
. Then

\divisor(g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}})|_{A_{0}}=-\alpha-\beta

for some angles
\alpha,\beta\in\bm{\alpha}
. Then there are two complementary cyclic intervals in
\partial N\setminus\{e(\alpha),e(\beta)\}
. In this case, we define
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
by “continuity in
(\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w})
.” Precisely, by contraction moves as in Figure
7
(right), we may assume that at least one of
\mathsf{b}
and
\mathsf{w}
has degree at least three. We then extend a path from
\mathsf{b}
to
\mathsf{w}
into an adjacent face of one of these vertices so as to cross a strand
\gamma\neq\alpha,\beta
. We define
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
to be the interval in
\partial N\setminus\{e(\alpha),e(\beta)\}
containing
e(\gamma)
.

2.4.5
Whole-plane inverse Kasteleyn matrices

We recall the construction of a two-parameter family of inverse Kasteleyn matrices for graph
{\mathsf{G}}
from
[
27
]
. These whole-plane inverse Kasteleyn matrices will be a basic ingredient in the definition of inverse Kasteleyn matrices of finite subgraphs as well as in the description of local statistics.

Since the angle function is compatible with the partial cyclic order on
\bm{\alpha}
, there is an open
interval in
A_{0}\setminus\bm{\alpha}
corresponding to each
v=e_{-}\cap e_{+}\in V(N)
, namely the interval between the angles in

\bm{\alpha}_{e_{-}}
and those in
\bm{\alpha}_{e_{+}}
.

Definition 2.15
.

For
v\in V(N)
and
\zeta\in\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
, let
C_{v}^{\zeta}
be a curve from
\sigma(\zeta)
to
\zeta
intersecting
A_{0}
exactly once in the component of
A_{0}\setminus\bm{\alpha}
corresponding to
v
, and such that
\sigma_{*}(C_{v}^{\zeta})=-C_{v}^{\zeta}
. If
\zeta\in\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}
, let
C_{v}^{\zeta}
denote the natural limit as
\zeta
approaches
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
.

Given
\mathsf{b}\in B({\mathsf{G}})
,
\mathsf{w}\in W({\mathsf{G}})
and
\zeta\in\Sigma_{+}\setminus\bm{\alpha}
, define

C_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{\zeta}:=C_{v}^{\zeta},

where
v
is any vertex in the null sector
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
.

Theorem 2.16

(
[
27
, Theorem 40]
)
.

For any
\zeta\in\Sigma_{+}\setminus\bm{\alpha}
, the matrix

\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{\zeta}:=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{\zeta}}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}

is an inverse of
\mathsf{K}
in the sense that
\mathsf{K}\mathsf{A}^{\zeta}=\mathsf{A}^{\zeta}\mathsf{K}=\mathsf{I}
.

We now introduce some additional notation for the case
\zeta\in\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}
which will be used in Section
4
.

Definition 2.17
.

Given an oriented interval
I\subset\partial N
, let
C(I)\subset\Sigma
be a curve which is the union of small counterclockwise-oriented circles that enclose all the angles
\bm{\alpha}_{e}
for
e\subset I
.

If
\zeta\in\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}
, then
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{\zeta}
depends only on the connected component of
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}
containing
\zeta
. If this component corresponds to
u\in V(N)
, then we can take

C_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{\zeta}=C(I_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{u}),\qquad I_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{u}:=[u,v],

where
v
is any vertex in
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
, and we write
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{u}:=\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{\zeta}
.

2.4.6
Gibbs measures

In this section we assume that the angle function
\nu:\bm{\alpha}\to A_{0}
is
periodic
,
i.e.

\nu(\alpha+(i,j))=\nu(\alpha)\qquad\text{for all}\penalty\ \alpha\in\bm{\alpha}\text{ and }(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

Equivalently, we have a well-defined angle function

\nu:\bm{\alpha}_{\mathbb{T}}\to A_{0}

on the quotient. As explained in
[
27
, Section 4.2]
, this setting is strictly more general than assuming that the weights are periodic; see
[
27
, Proposition 43]
for a characterization of the subset of periodic weights.

Theorem 2.18

(
[
27
, Theorem 62]
)
.

For any
\zeta\in\Sigma_{+}\setminus\bm{\alpha}
, the operator
\mathsf{A}^{\zeta}
defines a Gibbs measure
\mathbb{P}^{\zeta}
on dimer covers of
{\mathsf{G}}
. For any fixed collection of edges
\{\mathsf{e}_{i}=\mathsf{b}_{i}\mathsf{w}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}\subset E({\mathsf{G}})
, the probability that all of the edges
\mathsf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{e}_{k}
are present is

\mathbb{P}^{\zeta}(\mathsf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{e}_{k}\in M)=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w}_{i},\mathsf{b}_{i}}\det\bigl(\mathsf{A}^{\zeta}_{\mathsf{b}_{i},\mathsf{w}_{j}}\bigr)_{1\leq i,j\leq k}.

The
slope

(s^{\zeta},t^{\zeta})
of the Gibbs measure
\mathbb{P}^{\zeta}
is defined as the expected horizontal and vertical height change:

s^{\zeta}:=\mathbb{E}^{\zeta}\left[h_{M}(\mathsf{f}_{0}+(1,0))-h_{M}(\mathsf{f}_{0})\right],\quad t^{\zeta}:=\mathbb{E}^{\zeta}\left[h_{M}(\mathsf{f}_{0}+(0,1))-h_{M}(\mathsf{f}_{0})\right],

(2.9)

where
\mathbb{E}^{\zeta}
denotes expectation with respect to the probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{\zeta}
.

For
u\in V(N)
, let
\zeta\in A_{0}\setminus\bm{\alpha}
be any point in the connected component corresponding to
u
. Since
\mathbb{P}^{\zeta}
depends only on this component, we set

\mathbb{P}^{u}:=\mathbb{P}^{\zeta},\qquad\mathbb{E}^{u}:=\mathbb{E}^{\zeta},\qquad(s^{u},t^{u}):=(s^{\zeta},t^{\zeta}).

By
[
27
, Remark 53]
, the measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
is deterministic: more precisely,

\mathbb{P}^{u}(\mathsf{e}\in M)=\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{e}\in M_{u}},

where
M_{u}
is the dimer cover consisting of all edges
\mathsf{e}
such that
e(\alpha)<u<e(\beta)
along
\partial N
, with
\alpha,\beta
the two strands containing
\mathsf{e}
as in Figure
8
; we call
M_{u}
the
extremal dimer cover
corresponding to
u
.

Recall from Section
2.1
that
|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}
is the lattice length of
e
and
(a_{e},b_{e})=\vec{e}/|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Let
u_{0}\in V(N)
and let
M_{0}:=M_{u_{0}}
be the reference dimer cover used to define the height function. Define

z(\zeta):=\prod_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{\mathbb{T}}}E(\alpha,\zeta)^{-b_{e(\alpha)}},\qquad w(\zeta):=\prod_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{\mathbb{T}}}E(\alpha,\zeta)^{a_{e(\alpha)}}.

(2.10)

Then

(s^{\zeta},t^{\zeta})=\left(-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta}}d\log z,-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta}}d\log w\right);

(2.11)

see
[
27
, Equation 32]
. The set of possible slopes is the Newton polygon
N
rotated by
-\pi/2
; see
[
27
, Corollary 56]
and
[
79
, Section 3.1.4]
.

3
Astroidal zig-zag graphs

In this section, we introduce a class of finite graphs that we will work with and study their basic properties.

3.1
Definition of astroidal zig-zag graphs

e_{1}
e_{2}
e_{3}
e_{4}
e_{5}
e_{6}
e_{7}
e_{8}
e_{9}
e_{10}
\beta_{e_{10}}
\beta_{e_{1}}
\beta_{e_{2}}
\beta_{e_{3}}
\beta_{e_{4}}
\beta_{e_{5}}
\beta_{e_{6}}
\beta_{e_{7}}
\beta_{e_{8}}
\beta_{e_{9}}

Figure 9
:
A Newton polygon
N
and an illustration of
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}
, along with the edge coloring. Note that the actual
\beta_{e_{j}}
’s are (pieces of) strands, but they have the same asymptotic direction as the vectors pictured on the right.

{\mathsf{w}}

Figure 10
:
The left panel shows an example illustrating the necessity of Condition 2 in Definition
3.2
. The white arrows are a portion of
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}
, the thick edges are the corresponding portion of
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
and the shaded region consists of faces of
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
. The white vertex
\mathsf{w}
on the left-hand side is visited twice by non-consecutive zig-zag paths so
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
has a self-intersection. The right panel shows the neighborhood of an outer white vertex that is allowed.

Let
\bm{\beta}=(\beta_{e})_{e\in E(N)}
be a collection of strands such that
\beta_{e}\in\bm{\alpha}_{e}
.
For each vertex
v=e_{-}\cap e_{+}\in V(N)
, choose

\mathsf{e}_{v}\in\beta_{e_{-}}\cap\beta_{e_{+}}\in V({\mathsf{G}}^{\times})\cong E({\mathsf{G}}),

where
e_{-}
(resp.
e_{+}
) is the edge immediately clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) from
v
.

We first define the boundary cycle in the medial graph (see Figure
9
). Let
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}
be the counterclockwise-oriented cycle in
{\mathsf{G}}^{\times}
obtained as follows: it visits the medial vertices
\mathsf{e}_{v}
in the clockwise cyclic order of
v\in V(N)
, and for each edge
e=[v_{-},v_{+}]\in E(N)
it follows the strand
\beta_{e}
from
\mathsf{e}_{v_{+}}
to
\mathsf{e}_{v_{-}}
. We assume throughout that
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}
is a simple cycle.

From this we obtain a subgraph
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
of
{\mathsf{G}}
by defining

\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}:=\bigcup_{\mathsf{e}\in V(\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times})}\mathsf{e}\subset{\mathsf{G}},

i.e.
the union of the corresponding zig-zag path segments.

Even if the medial cycle
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}
is simple, the resulting subgraph
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
need not be: a vertex of
{\mathsf{G}}
may lie on several strands
\beta_{e}
. This is harmless provided such occurrences are consecutive along
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}
. The problematic situation is when two visits to a vertex are separated by other parts of the boundary, which would produce a genuine self-intersection in
{\mathsf{G}}
(see Figure
10
(left)). Throughout, we assume this does not happen;
i.e.
we assume that whenever a vertex of
{\mathsf{G}}
is visited more than once by
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
, the corresponding strands appear consecutively in the cyclic order of
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}
.

We then define the graph
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
to be the finite portion of
\mathsf{G}
contained weakly inside
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
. The reader may find it helpful to look ahead at the examples in Section
3.2
before proceeding with the definitions below.

We next assign a color to each edge
e\in E(N)
. We say that
e
is
black
if its induced orientation agrees with the counterclockwise orientation of
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}
, and
white
otherwise (see Figure
9
). We also define a sign function

\operatorname{sign}_{\bm{\beta}}:E(N)\rightarrow\{+,-\},\qquad\operatorname{sign}_{\bm{\beta}}(e):=\begin{cases}+1,&\text{if $e$ is black},\\
-1,&\text{if $e$ is white}.\end{cases}

(3.1)

We will use the terminology “black/white” and “sign
\pm 1
” interchangeably.

For each edge
e\in E(N)
, the portion of the strand
\beta_{e}
between
\mathsf{e}_{v_{-}}
and
\mathsf{e}_{v_{+}}
separates the vertices of the corresponding zig-zag path into two sides. We call the vertices on the exterior of
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}

\beta_{e}
-outer
vertices. A vertex of
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
that is not
\beta_{e}
-outer for any
e\in E(N)
is called an
inner
vertex. Note that a vertex can be
\beta_{e}
-outer for several
e\in E(N)
. By Lemma
2.4
, such edges all have the same color and form a cyclic interval in
\partial N
(see Figure
10
(right)).

Informally, we think of
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
as defining a polygonal region whose “vertices” are the edges
\mathsf{e}_{v}
and whose “sides” run along the zig-zag paths corresponding to
\beta_{e}
. An edge
e
has the color of the corresponding
\beta_{e}
-outer vertices.

Definition 3.1
.

Given such a
\bm{\beta}
, define an element
\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\bm{\alpha}}
by

(\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}})_{\bm{\alpha}_{e}}:=-\bm{d}(\mathsf{x})_{\bm{\alpha}_{e}}

(3.2)

for all
e\in E(N)
, where
\mathsf{x}
is any
\beta_{e}
-outer vertex. We say that
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
is
admissible
if

\deg(\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}})=0.

(3.3)

Since

\deg(\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}})=\sum_{e\in E(N)}\deg\bigl((\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}})_{\bm{\alpha}_{e}}\bigr),

the admissibility condition (
3.3
) says that the total number of strands between the reference face and
\beta_{e}
, summed over white edges
e
, is equal to the corresponding total summed over black edges.

The role of admissibility will be clarified in Lemma
4.1
.

Definition 3.2
.

We call
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
an
astroidal zig-zag graph
(AZ graph) if:

1.

the medial cycle
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}
is simple,

2.

whenever a vertex of
{\mathsf{G}}
is visited more than once as we go around
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
, all such visits occur consecutively along
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}
,

3.

\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
is admissible.

The reason for the name is that the boundary
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
resembles an astroid curve (see Lemma
3.13
) and is made up of zig-zag paths. Since
{\mathsf{G}}
is
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-periodic and we have one constraint (
3.3
), AZ graphs form a
(\#E(N)-3)
-parameter family. In concrete examples, AZ graphs are easy to construct directly. A general construction showing that the class is nonempty for every Newton polygon with at least four sides will be given in Remark
5.2
.

3.2
Examples of AZ graphs

Example 3.3
.

When
N
is any triangle, the
\mathsf{G}
is the hexagonal lattice
[
68
, Proposition 11.3]
, we have a
0
-parameter family, and there are no AZ graphs.

Figure 11
:
A square Newton polygon
N
(left), the Aztec diamond
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
(middle) and its medial cycle
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}
(right).

Figure 12
:
A quadrilateral Newton polygon and an example of a corresponding AZ graph.

Example 3.4
.

When
N
is a convex lattice quadrilateral, there is a one-parameter family of AZ graphs. If
N
is the unit square, one choice of minimal graph
{\mathsf{G}}
is the square lattice. A general choice of
\bm{\beta}
produces a rectangular subgraph, and admissibility forces this subgraph to be the Aztec diamond (Figure
11
). Any other choice of minimal graph is related to the square lattice by local moves and has the same large-scale statistical-mechanical behavior. Thus, up to local moves, the Aztec diamonds are the only AZ graphs associated with the unit square Newton polygon. Figure
12
shows another quadrilateral Newton polygon together
with an associated AZ graph, called a
tower graph
, which is the unique AZ graph up to size, and it has been studied in
[
21
,
15
,
92
]

and in the physics literature as the brane tiling/dimer model for the suspended pinch point singularity; see for example,
[
54
,
55
,
77
,
49
]
. Another example is given by the
pinecone graphs
associated with Gale–Robinson sequences studied in
[
23
,
70
,
32
]
.

Figure 13
:

An example of an AZ graph for the pentagonal Newton polygon in Figure
1
which we will use as our running example.

Example 3.5
.

Figures
4
and
13
show two examples of AZ graphs for the pentagonal Newton polygon in Figure
1
. For this choice of
{\mathsf{G}}
, the space of AZ graphs forms a two-parameter family, which was studied from the point of view of cluster algebras in
[
57
]
.

Figure 14
:
A hexagonal Newton polygon and an example of an AZ graph.

Example 3.6
.

Let
N
be the hexagon shown in Figure
14
(left). Figure
14
(right) shows a choice of fundamental domain for
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}
and an example of an AZ graph. The class of AZ graphs for this
{\mathsf{G}}
has been studied extensively from the cluster algebra point of view in
[
40
,
111
,
90
,
88
,
37
]
.

Figure 15
:
An octagonal Newton polygon
N
and an AZ graph.

Example 3.7
.

Figure
15
shows an example of an AZ graph for an octagonal Newton polygon. The corresponding torus graph was studied in
[
7
]
.

3.3
Criterion for admissibility

For any
e\in E(N)
, we label the strands
\bm{\alpha}_{e}=\{\alpha_{e}^{i}:i\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}\}
parallel to
e
from left to right so that the reference face
\mathsf{f}_{0}
lies in between
\alpha_{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and
\alpha_{e}^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Define

c_{\bm{\beta}}:E(N)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}+\tfrac{1}{2}

by
\alpha_{e}^{c_{\bm{\beta}}(e)}=\beta_{e}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

For each
e\in E(N)
,

\deg\bigl((\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}})_{\bm{\alpha}_{e}}\bigr)=-c_{\bm{\beta}}(e)+\begin{cases}-\frac{1}{2},&\text{if $e$ is black},\\
\phantom{-}\frac{1}{2},&\text{if $e$ is white}.\end{cases}

In particular,
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
is admissible if and only if

\sum_{e\in E(N)}c_{\bm{\beta}}(e)=\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\#\{e\in E(N):e\text{ is white}\}-\#\{e\in E(N):e\text{ is black}\}\Bigr).

(3.4)

Proof.

We have, for a
\beta_{e}
-outer vertex
\mathsf{b}
,

\bm{d}(\mathsf{b})_{\bm{\alpha}_{e}}=\begin{cases}-\alpha^{c_{\bm{\beta}}(e)+1}_{e}-\cdots-\alpha_{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}},&\text{if $c_{\bm{\beta}}(e)<0$},\\
\alpha_{e}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\cdots+\alpha_{e}^{c_{\bm{\beta}}(e)},&\text{if $c_{\bm{\beta}}(e)>0$},\\
\end{cases}

if
e
is black, so
\deg((\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}})_{\bm{\alpha}_{e}})=-c_{\bm{\beta}}(e)-\frac{1}{2}
.
Similarly, for a
\beta_{e}
-outer vertex
\mathsf{w}
,

\bm{d}(\mathsf{w})_{\bm{\alpha}_{e}}=\begin{cases}-\alpha^{c_{\bm{\beta}}(e)}_{e}-\cdots-\alpha_{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}},&\text{if $c_{\bm{\beta}}(e)<0$},\\
\alpha_{e}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\cdots+\alpha_{e}^{c_{\bm{\beta}}(e)-1},&\text{if $c_{\bm{\beta}}(e)>0$},\\
\end{cases}

if
e
is white, so
\deg((\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}})_{\bm{\alpha}_{e}})=-c_{\bm{\beta}}(e)+\frac{1}{2}
. Summing over all edges gives (
3.4
).
∎

e_{1}
e_{3}
e_{5}
e_{2}
e_{4}
\alpha_{e_{1}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
\alpha_{e_{2}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
\alpha_{e_{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}
\alpha_{e_{3}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
\alpha_{e_{3}}^{\frac{1}{2}}
\alpha_{e_{4}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
\alpha_{e_{4}}^{\frac{1}{2}}
\alpha_{e_{5}}^{\frac{1}{2}}

Figure 16
:
Classes of parallel strands for the graph in Figure
13
. The shaded face is the reference face
\mathsf{f}_{0}
.

Example 3.9
.

Let us verify that the graph in Figure
13
is admissible.
The strands are shown in Figure
16
, from which we read

c_{\bm{\beta}}(e_{1})=-\frac{1}{2},\quad c_{\bm{\beta}}(e_{2})=-\frac{1}{2},\quad c_{\bm{\beta}}(e_{3})=\frac{1}{2},\quad c_{\bm{\beta}}(e_{4})=-\frac{1}{2},\quad c_{\bm{\beta}}(e_{5})=\frac{1}{2}.

Summing gives

\sum_{i=1}^{5}c_{\bm{\beta}}(e_{i})=-\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1}{2}(2-3),

so
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
is admissible by Lemma
3.8
.
Alternatively, from Figure
13
and Figure
16
we see that

\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}=\alpha_{e_{2}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\alpha_{e_{3}}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\alpha_{e_{4}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\alpha_{e_{5}}^{\frac{1}{2}},

(3.5)

hence
\deg(\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}})=0
, which again shows admissibility.

3.4
Characterization of AZ graphs

\mathsf{e}_{v}

Figure 17
:

The faces marked by
\times
are the
\beta_{e_{-}}
- and
\beta_{e_{+}}
-outer faces associated with the vertex
v=e_{-}\cap e_{+}\in V(N)
. The shaded region indicates
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
. The blue edges form a dimer cover
M
of
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
, while the red edges form the extremal dimer cover
M_{v}
. Any dual path between two marked outer faces, such as the dotted path shown, crosses no edges of either
M
or
M_{v}
. Hence, the difference
h_{M_{v}}(\mathsf{f})-\partial h(\mathsf{f})
in (
3.6
) is constant on these outer faces.

In this section we prove that, among graphs whose boundary consists of zig-zag paths in the prescribed order, the only ones that admit dimer covers are the AZ graphs. The converse, namely that every AZ graph admits a dimer cover, is proved later in Corollary
4.4
.

Let
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}
,
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
, and
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
be as in Section
3.1
. We say that a face
\mathsf{f}\in F({\mathsf{G}})\setminus F(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})
is
outer
if it is adjacent to a vertex of
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
, and
\beta_{e}
-outer
for
e\in E(N)
if it is adjacent to a vertex in the zig-zag path corresponding to the strand
\beta_{e}
.

Proposition 3.10
.

Assume that Items
1
and
2
in Definition
3.2
hold. If
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
admits a dimer cover, then it is an AZ graph.

Proof.

We prove that if
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
admits a dimer cover, then Item 3 in Definition
3.2
,
i.e.
the admissibility condition, is satisfied.

Fix a vertex
u_{0}\in V(N)
and let
M_{0}:=M_{u_{0}}
denote the extremal dimer cover from Section
2.4.6
. Let
M
be a dimer cover of
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
and let
h_{M}
be the associated height function on
F(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})
with reference dimer cover
M_{0}
and reference face
\mathsf{f}_{0}
. The union of two dimer covers
M
and
M^{\prime}
of
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
forms a collection of loops. The nontrivial loops can be interpreted as level lines of the height difference
h_{M}-h_{M^{\prime}}
. In particular, this difference vanishes on the outer faces, so
h_{M}
and
h_{M^{\prime}}
induce the same height function on the outer faces,
i.e.
the boundary values of
h_{M}
are independent of
M
.
We denote this common boundary height function by
\partial h
.

Recall that for
v\in V(N)
, the extremal dimer cover
M_{v}
is, by definition, the dimer cover consisting of all edges
\mathsf{e}
such that
e(\alpha)<v<e(\beta)
along
\partial N
, where
\alpha
and
\beta
are the two strands passing though
\mathsf{e}
as in Figure
8
. In particular, if
e\in E(N)
is an edge containing
v
, then every second edge of
\beta_{e}
is contained in
M_{v}
. Hence,

h_{M_{v}}(\mathsf{f})-\partial h(\mathsf{f})

(3.6)

is constant for all
\beta_{e_{-}}
- and
\beta_{e_{+}}
-outer faces
\mathsf{f}
, where
v=e_{-}\cap e_{+}
; see Figure
17
. We denote this constant value by
d_{v}
.

Now let
\mathsf{f}
be any
\beta_{e}
-outer face for
e=[v_{-},v_{+}]
. Then

d_{v_{+}}-d_{v_{-}}=\sum_{\mathsf{e}\in E({\mathsf{G}})}(\mathsf{e}\wedge\gamma^{*})\mathbb{E}[\one_{\mathsf{e}\in M_{v_{+}}}-\one_{\mathsf{e}\in M_{v_{-}}}],

(3.7)

where
\gamma^{*}
is any dual path from the reference face
\mathsf{f}_{0}
to
\mathsf{f}
. The right-hand side of (
3.7
) can be expressed in terms of the whole-plane inverse Kasteleyn matrices from Section
2.4.5
as

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{e}\in E({\mathsf{G}})}(\mathsf{e}\wedge\gamma^{*})\mathbb{E}[\one_{\mathsf{e}\in M_{v_{+}}}-\one_{\mathsf{e}\in M_{v_{-}}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}\in E({\mathsf{G}})}(\mathsf{e}\wedge\gamma^{*})\left(\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{v_{+}}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}-\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{v_{-}}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\mathsf{e}\in E({\mathsf{G}})}(\mathsf{e}\wedge\gamma^{*})\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C([v_{-},v_{+}])}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\cdot\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}.

If
\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}\in E({\mathsf{G}})
, and
\mathsf{f}_{+},\mathsf{f}_{-}\in F({\mathsf{G}})
are adjacent to
\mathsf{e}
as in Figure
8
, then by Lemma
2.10
,

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C([v_{+},v_{-}])}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\cdot\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C([v_{+},v_{-}])}\omega_{\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{+})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{-})}=\deg((\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{+})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{-}))_{\bm{\alpha}_{e}}).

(3.8)

Summing over the edges crossing
\gamma^{*}
yields

d_{v_{+}}-d_{v_{-}}=-\deg(\bm{d}(\mathsf{f})_{\bm{\alpha}_{e}}).

(3.9)

Finally, summing (
3.9
) over all
e=[v_{-},v_{+}]\in E(N)
, and using the definition of
\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}
in (
3.2
), we obtain

0=\sum_{e=[v_{-},v_{+}]\in E(N)}(d_{v_{+}}-d_{v_{-}})=\deg(\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}),

(3.10)

which is the admissibility condition.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

The proof of Proposition
3.10
shows more generally that, if Items
1
and
2
of Definition
3.2
hold, then
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
is an AZ graph whenever the boundary height function is single-valued on the
\beta_{e}
-outer faces; equivalently, the height increments along the boundary close up.

3.5
Convex and concave vertices

v
\vec{e}_{-}
\vec{e}_{+}
\theta
Newton polygon
\beta_{e_{-}}
\beta_{e_{+}}
\pi-\theta
\beta_{e_{-}}
\beta_{e_{+}}
\theta
\beta_{e_{-}}
\beta_{e_{+}}
\pi-\theta
convex vertices
\beta_{e_{-}}
\beta_{e_{+}}
\theta
concave vertices

Figure 18
:
Change of Gauss map
\gamma
at a vertex
v=e_{-}\cap e_{+}
, where
\theta=\arg(\vec{e}_{+})-\arg(\vec{e}_{-})
.

Definition 3.12
.

We call a vertex
v=e_{-}\cap e_{+}\in V(N)

convex
if
e_{-}
and
e_{+}
have opposite colors (equivalently,
\operatorname{sign}_{\bm{\beta}}(e_{-})\neq\operatorname{sign}_{\bm{\beta}}(e_{+})
), and
concave
otherwise.

Lemma 3.13
.

There are exactly four convex vertices in
V(N)
.

Proof.

Consider the “Gauss map”

\gamma:V(\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times})\setminus\{\mathsf{e}_{v}\}_{v\in V(N)}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z},\qquad\gamma(\mathsf{e}):=\arg(\operatorname{sign}_{\bm{\beta}}(e)\vec{e})\quad\text{if }\mathsf{e}\in V(\beta_{e}),

sending a medial vertex
\mathsf{e}\in V(\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times})
to the oriented “tangent direction” when
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}
is oriented counterclockwise.
At
v=e_{-}\cap e_{+}
, the map
\gamma
changes by (see Figure
18
)

\pi-(\arg(\vec{e}_{+})-\arg(\vec{e}_{-}))\quad\text{if $v$ is convex,}\qquad-(\arg(\vec{e}_{+})-\arg(\vec{e}_{-}))\quad\text{if $v$ is concave}.

Since
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}
is simple, the total change of
\gamma
along
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}
is
2\pi
. Therefore,

\pi\cdot\#\{\text{convex vertices}\}-\sum_{v=e_{-}\cap e_{+}\in V(N)}(\arg(\vec{e}_{+})-\arg(\vec{e}_{-}))=2\pi.

On the other hand, since
\partial N
is a simple closed curve,

\sum_{v=e_{-}\cap e_{+}\in V(N)}(\arg(\vec{e}_{+})-\arg(\vec{e}_{-}))=2\pi.

Substituting gives
\pi\cdot\#\{\text{convex vertices}\}=4\pi
, hence, there are exactly four convex vertices.
∎

Thus, the convex vertices partition
\partial N
and
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}
into four intervals that alternate in color (see Figure
9
).

3.6
Chambers

Unlike the case of the Aztec diamond, the relative position of vertices with respect to strands need not be uniform. Thus, we introduce “local sign functions” in addition to the global sign function
\operatorname{sign}_{\bm{\beta}}
from (
3.1
).

The collection of strands
\bm{\beta}
partitions the plane into several (open)
chambers
. Precisely, the chambers are the connected components of

\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\bigcup_{e\in E(N)}\beta_{e}.

We call the chambers contained inside (resp. outside)
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\times}

inner
(resp.
outer
) chambers. Each chamber
\mathsf{R}
corresponds to a
sign function

\operatorname{sign}_{\mathsf{R}}:E(N)\rightarrow\{+,-\},\qquad\operatorname{sign}_{\mathsf{R}}(e):=\begin{cases}+,&\text{if ${\mathsf{R}}$ lies to the left of $\beta_{e}$,}\\
-,&\text{if ${\mathsf{R}}$ lies to the right of $\beta_{e}$.}\end{cases}

(3.11)

Recall that we identify signs with colors, and therefore,
\operatorname{sign}_{\mathsf{R}}
equivalently defines a coloring of
E(N)
for each chamber.

Definition 3.14
.

Let
v=e_{-}\cap e_{+}\in V(N)
be a vertex of the Newton polygon. We say that a chamber
{\mathsf{R}}
has a
sign (or color) change
at
v
if

\operatorname{sign}_{\mathsf{R}}(e_{-})\neq\operatorname{sign}_{\mathsf{R}}(e_{+}).

We write
\operatorname{SC}({\mathsf{R}})\subset V(N)
for the set of sign changes of
{\mathsf{R}}
.

Lemma 3.15
.

An inner chamber has exactly four sign changes, while an outer chamber has exactly two.

Proof.

The argument is identical to that of Lemma
3.13
, with the Gauss map replaced by

\mathsf{e}\mapsto\arg(\operatorname{sign}_{\mathsf{R}}(e)\vec{e})\quad\text{if }\mathsf{e}\in V(\beta_{e}),

i.e.
the tangent direction as seen from
{\mathsf{R}}
and using that the total winding is
2\pi
for an inner chamber and
0
for an outer chamber.
∎

Figure 19
:
The four conical regions. The shaded regions are the inconsistently oriented cones.

We now describe the sign changes geometrically. Let
v=e_{-}\cap e_{+}\in V(N)
. The strands
\beta_{e_{-}}
and
\beta_{e_{+}}
subdivide
\mathbb{R}^{2}
into four conical regions (Figure
19
). Two of these cones have boundaries that are consistently oriented and two have boundaries that are inconsistently oriented. By definition, a chamber
{\mathsf{R}}
has a sign change at
v
if and only if
{\mathsf{R}}
is contained in one of the two inconsistently oriented cones determined by
\beta_{e_{-}}
and
\beta_{e_{+}}
at
v
.

Consider the adjacency graph
\mathcal{R}
of inner chambers: its vertices are the inner chambers, and two vertices are joined by an edge if and only if the boundaries of the corresponding chambers share a nontrivial segment of a strand
\beta_{e}
. In particular, chambers meeting only at a crossing, such as the two gray regions in Figure
19
, are not considered adjacent.

Lemma 3.16
.

Across each edge of
\mathcal{R}
, exactly one of the four sign changes moves one step clockwise or counterclockwise along
\partial N
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathsf{R}}
and
{\mathsf{R}}^{\prime}
be adjacent chambers, separated by a segment of a strand
\beta_{e}
. Then
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}^{\prime}}(e)=-\operatorname{sign}_{\mathsf{R}}(e)
, while
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}^{\prime}}(e^{\prime})=\operatorname{sign}_{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
for all
e^{\prime}\neq e
.

If
e
is not adjacent to the boundary of one of the four sign intervals for
\operatorname{sign}_{\mathsf{R}}
(equivalently, if
e
is not adjacent along
\partial N
to a vertex in
\operatorname{SC}({\mathsf{R}})
), then changing only the sign at
e
would create two additional sign changes (one at each endpoint of
e
along
\partial N
), while the previous four sign changes persist. This would give
\#\operatorname{SC}({\mathsf{R}}^{\prime})\geq 6
, contradicting Lemma
3.15
. Hence,
e
must lie at the boundary of exactly one sign interval, so exactly one sign change shifts by one step along
\partial N
, and the other three remain fixed.
∎

In particular, for every edge
{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{R}}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{R}
there is a canonical bijection

\operatorname{SC}({\mathsf{R}})\cong\operatorname{SC}({\mathsf{R}}^{\prime})

(3.12)

which is the identity on three of the four elements. Consider the discrete bundle over
\mathcal{R}
whose fiber over an inner chamber
{\mathsf{R}}
is the four-element set
\operatorname{SC}({\mathsf{R}})
, equipped with the connection determined by the bijections (
3.12
) along edges.

Lemma 3.17
.

The connection thus defined is flat,
i.e.
the parallel transport around any closed loop in
\mathcal{R}
is the identity.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{R}
is planar, it suffices to check triviality around each face. Each face is a
4
-cycle of inner chambers surrounding an intersection point of two strands
\beta_{e_{1}}
and
\beta_{e_{2}}
in
{\mathsf{G}}
. Label the chambers cyclically by

{\mathsf{R}}\xrightarrow{\beta_{e_{1}}}{\mathsf{R}}_{1}\xrightarrow{\beta_{e_{2}}}{\mathsf{R}}_{12}\xrightarrow{\beta_{e_{1}}}{\mathsf{R}}_{2}\xrightarrow{\beta_{e_{2}}}{\mathsf{R}}.

Crossing
\beta_{e_{i}}
flips the sign at
e_{i}
and, by Lemma
3.16
, moves exactly one sign change by one step along
\partial N
, fixing the other three.
Since the intersection
\beta_{e_{1}}\cap\beta_{e_{2}}
is not on
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
, the edges
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are not adjacent along
\partial N
, hence, the moved sign change for
\beta_{e_{1}}
is different from the moved sign change for
\beta_{e_{2}}
.
Therefore, going once around the
4
-cycle moves the
e_{1}
-sign change one step and then moves it back, and similarly for the
e_{2}
-sign change; all other sign changes remain fixed throughout.
Thus, the composed bijection around the face is the identity, and the connection is flat.
∎

The discrete bundle of sign changes has four canonical sections which we now define. Each convex vertex
v\in V(N)
determines a section

s_{v}:\{\text{inner chambers}\}\rightarrow V(N),\qquad s_{v}({\mathsf{R}})\in\operatorname{SC}({\mathsf{R}}),

as follows. Let
{\mathsf{R}}_{v}
be the inner chamber adjacent to
v
. Then
v\in\operatorname{SC}({\mathsf{R}}_{v})
, and we set
s_{v}({\mathsf{R}}_{v})=v
.
For any other inner chamber
{\mathsf{R}}
, define
s_{v}({\mathsf{R}})
by parallel transporting
v
along any path in
\mathcal{R}
from
{\mathsf{R}}_{v}
to
{\mathsf{R}}
.
By Lemma
3.17
, this is well-defined.

v_{1}
v_{2}=s_{v_{2}}({\mathsf{R}}_{v_{2}})
{s_{v_{3}}({\mathsf{R}}_{v_{2}})}
{s_{v_{1}}({\mathsf{R}}_{v_{2}})}

Figure 20
:
The chosen inconsistently oriented cones in
[v_{1},v_{2})
whose boundary orientations match the boundary orientation of the reference cone at
v_{1}
(shaded).

Lemma 3.18
.

For every inner chamber
{\mathsf{R}}
, the four sections associated to the four convex vertices are pairwise distinct at
{\mathsf{R}}
, together exhaust
\operatorname{SC}({\mathsf{R}})
, and appear in the same cyclic order along
\partial N
as the convex vertices.

Proof.

Let
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}
be the convex vertices in cyclic order along
\partial N
.
Consider any edge
{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{R}}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{R}
. By Lemma
3.16
, under the
connection bijection
\operatorname{SC}({\mathsf{R}})\cong\operatorname{SC}({\mathsf{R}}^{\prime})
exactly one element of
\operatorname{SC}({\mathsf{R}})
moves by
one step along
\partial N
, while the other three are fixed.
In particular, no two transported elements can collide or cross: a collision would
force
\#\operatorname{SC}({\mathsf{R}}^{\prime})<4
, contradicting Lemma
3.15
, and a crossing
would require two elements to pass each other even though at most one moves.
Consequently, if
s_{v_{i}}({\mathsf{R}})\neq s_{v_{j}}({\mathsf{R}})
for
i\neq j
at some chamber
{\mathsf{R}}
, then

s_{v_{i}}
and
s_{v_{j}}
remain distinct over all chambers, and their cyclic order
along
\partial N
is preserved throughout
\mathcal{R}
.

It therefore suffices to verify the distinctness and the cyclic ordering in a single chamber. Without loss of generality, we check the cyclic order at the inner chamber
{\mathsf{R}}_{v_{2}}
adjacent to
v_{2}
. Consider a path in
\mathcal{R}
that moves clockwise along the boundary
\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
from the inner chamber
{\mathsf{R}}_{v_{1}}
adjacent to
v_{1}
to
{\mathsf{R}}_{v_{2}}
. Initially, we have
s_{v_{1}}({\mathsf{R}}_{v_{1}})=v_{1}
. Along this path, the section
s_{v_{1}}
evolves as follows.
For each vertex
u\in[v_{1},v_{2})
there are two inconsistently oriented cones. Among them, choose the one whose boundary orientation agrees with that of the
reference cone at
v_{1}
containing
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
(
i.e.
, in both cones the strands point either toward or away from the apex). Then for every chamber
{\mathsf{R}}
encountered along the path, we set
s_{v_{1}}({\mathsf{R}})=u
, where
u
is the unique vertex in
[v_{1},v_{2})
such that
{\mathsf{R}}
lies in this chosen
cone at
u
(see Figure
20
). In particular, when the path reaches
{\mathsf{R}}_{v_{2}}
, the section
s_{v_{1}}
has advanced monotonically to the vertex immediately counterclockwise of
v_{2}
. Applying the same argument with
v_{3}
in place of
v_{1}
, we get that
s_{v_{3}}({\mathsf{R}}_{v_{2}})
is the vertex immediately clockwise of
v_{2}
. Hence, the sections
s_{v_{1}},s_{v_{2}},s_{v_{3}}
are distinct and appear in the correct cyclic order at
{\mathsf{R}}_{v_{2}}
.

Finally, since
\#\operatorname{SC}({\mathsf{R}})=4
for every chamber
{\mathsf{R}}
, the four sections exhaust
\operatorname{SC}({\mathsf{R}})
for all
{\mathsf{R}}
.
∎

4
Exact inverse Kasteleyn formula for AZ graphs

In this section, we define
\mathsf{K}^{-1}
by a double integral plus single-integral correction term.

4.1
Formula for
\mathsf{K}^{-1}

Let
\mathsf{b}\in B(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})
and
\mathsf{w}\in W(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})
. Define the kernel

\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}(\zeta,\eta):=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\frac{1}{E(\zeta,\eta)}\frac{\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}+\eta-\zeta)}{\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}})}\frac{g_{\mathsf{w}}(\eta)\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\eta)}{g_{\mathsf{b}}(\zeta)\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\zeta)},

(4.1)

where

\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\zeta):=\prod_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}E(\alpha,\zeta)^{(\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}})_{\alpha}}

encodes the dependence on the boundary data. As in Section
2.3.1
, we identify
\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}+\eta-\zeta
with its image
\mu(\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}+\eta-\zeta)
. The parameter
t
here is as in Section
2.4.3
.

Lemma 4.1
.

The kernel
\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}(\zeta,\eta)
is a meromorphic
(1,1)
-form on
\Sigma\times\Sigma
.

Proof.

This is standard; see for instance
[
27
, Lemma 32]
.
Fix
\eta
and view
\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}(\zeta,\eta)
as a function of
\zeta
.
To say it is a meromorphic
1
-form in
\zeta
is equivalent to saying that its divisor is linearly equivalent to the canonical divisor:

\divisor_{\zeta}(\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}})\sim K_{\Sigma}.

By Abel’s theorem, this holds provided

\deg(\divisor_{\zeta}(\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}))=\deg(K_{\Sigma})=2g-2,\qquad\mu(\divisor_{\zeta}(\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}))=K_{\Sigma}\in\operatorname{Jac}(\Sigma).

By Riemann’s theorem
[
51
, Theorem 1.1]
, for any
e\in\mathbb{C}^{g}
such that
\theta(e+\zeta)
is not identically
0
,

\deg(\divisor_{\zeta}(\theta(e+\zeta)))=g,\qquad\mu(\divisor_{\zeta}(\theta(e+\zeta)))=-e+\Delta,

where
\Delta
is the vector of Riemann constants, satisfying
2\Delta=K_{\Sigma}
.

As a function of
\zeta
,

•

E(\zeta,\eta)^{-1}
has a simple pole at
\zeta=\eta
, hence degree
-1
, and
\mu(\divisor_{\zeta}(E(\zeta,\eta)))=\eta
(recall that we often suppress the Abel map; the
\eta
on the right-hand side stands for
\mu(\eta)
).

•

\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}+\eta-\zeta)
contributes degree
g
, and
\mu(\divisor_{\zeta}(\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}+\eta-\zeta)))=-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}+\eta+\Delta
.

•

(g_{\mathsf{b}}\Psi_{\bm{\beta}})^{-1}
contributes degree
g-1+\deg(\divisor(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}))
, and

\mu(\divisor(g_{\mathsf{b}}))=-t-\Delta,\qquad\mu(\divisor(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}))=\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}.

By admissibility,
\deg(\divisor(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}))=\deg(\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}})=0
. Thus,

\deg(\divisor_{\zeta}(\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}))=-1+g+(g-1+\deg(\divisor(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}})))=2g-2.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\mu(\divisor_{\zeta}(\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}))

\displaystyle=-\mu(\divisor_{\zeta}(E(\zeta,\eta)))+\mu(\divisor_{\zeta}(\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}+\eta-\zeta)))-\mu(\divisor_{\zeta}(g_{\mathsf{b}}(\zeta)))-\mu(\divisor_{\zeta}(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\zeta)))

\displaystyle=-\eta+(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}+\eta+\Delta)+(t+\Delta)-\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}

\displaystyle=2\Delta=K_{\Sigma}.

The same argument in the
\eta
-variable shows that
\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}(\zeta,\eta)
is a meromorphic
(1,1)
-form on
\Sigma\times\Sigma
.
∎

The residue of
\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
, viewed as a meromorphic
1
-form in
\zeta
, along the diagonal
\zeta=\eta
, is

\res_{\zeta=\eta}(\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}})=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}.

For an interval
I=[\ell,r]\subset\partial N
, define its boundary to be the
0
-chain

\partial I:=r-\ell.

Recall from Section
2.4.5
the definitions of the contour
C(I_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{u})
and the whole-plane inverse
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{u}
. By integration, we obtain a linear functional on
0
-chains defined by

u\mapsto\langle u,\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle:=\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{u}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C(I_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{u})}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}},

(4.2)

and extended to arbitrary
0
-chains by linearity.

Let
v
be a convex vertex and let
\mathsf{b}\in B(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})
and
\mathsf{w}\in W(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})
. For
\{\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\}=\{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}\}
, define

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{sign}(v)\left(\iint_{C(I_{\mathsf{y}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{x}})}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}-\varepsilon(\mathsf{x})\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{x}}\cap I_{\mathsf{y}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle\right)

(4.3)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{sign}(v)\left(\iint_{C(I_{\mathsf{y}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{x}})}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}-\varepsilon(\mathsf{x})\Bigl(\mathbf{1}_{\{r_{\mathsf{x}}\in I_{\mathsf{y}}\}}{\mathsf{A}}^{r_{\mathsf{x}}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell_{\mathsf{x}}\in I_{\mathsf{y}}\}}{\mathsf{A}}^{\ell_{\mathsf{x}}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\Bigr)\right),

(4.4)

where:

1.

We set
\operatorname{sign}(v)=+1
if the color changes from black to white at the section
s_{v}
, and
\operatorname{sign}(v)=-1
otherwise.

2.

\varepsilon(\mathsf{x})=+1
(resp.
-1
) if
\mathsf{x}
is black (resp. white) in consistency with our sign/color convention (
3.1
).

3.

For each
\mathsf{x}\in B(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})\sqcup W(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})
, let
{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{x}}
denote the chamber containing
\mathsf{x}
. We associate to
\mathsf{x}
an inner chamber
R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{x}}
by setting

R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{x}}:=\begin{cases}{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{x}},&\text{if $\mathsf{x}$ is inner},\\[4.0pt]
\text{the inner chamber adjacent to $\mathsf{x}$ across $\beta_{e}$},&\text{if $\mathsf{x}$ is $\beta_{e}$-outer}.\end{cases}

If
\mathsf{x}
is
\beta_{e}
-outer for more than one
e\in E(N)
, we choose one such
e
arbitrarily. The resulting constructions are independent of this choice; see Proposition
4.11
.

The interval
I_{\mathsf{x}}=[\ell_{\mathsf{x}},r_{\mathsf{x}}]\subset\partial N
is defined as the sign interval of
\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{x}}}
whose color is opposite to that of
\mathsf{x}
, and which is adjacent to
s_{v}(R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{x}})
; the interval
I_{\mathsf{y}}
is defined analogously. The contours
C(I_{\mathsf{x}})
and
C(I_{\mathsf{y}})
are then defined by Definition
2.17
.

Equivalently, instead of specifying the convex vertex
v
, we can choose
I_{\mathsf{x}}
to be either of the sign intervals for
\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{x}}}
with opposite color as
\mathsf{x}
, and similarly for
I_{\mathsf{y}}
. The convex vertex is then uniquely determined as the common endpoint of both intervals when they are parallel transported to the same chamber.

4.

Throughout,
\zeta
is integrated over
C(I_{\mathsf{b}})
and
\eta
is integrated over
C(I_{\mathsf{w}})
. The notation

C(I_{\mathsf{y}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{x}})

denotes the product
C(I_{\mathsf{b}})\times C(I_{\mathsf{w}})
with the convention that if both contours
C(I_{\mathsf{x}})
and
C(I_{\mathsf{y}})
contain small circles around the same angle
\alpha
, we choose them so that the circle in
C(I_{\mathsf{y}})
has smaller radius than the one in
C(I_{\mathsf{x}})
.

Remark 4.2
.

Since there are four choices of convex vertices and two choices of
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})=(\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w})
or
(\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b})
, the definition (
4.3
) gives eight different expressions. We will prove in Proposition
4.11
that the resulting quantities for all of these choices are equal. If we take
\mathsf{x}=\mathsf{b}
and choose the intervals
I_{\mathsf{b}}
and
I_{\mathsf{w}}
so that
v
is a convex vertex at which
\operatorname{sign}_{\bm{\beta}}
changes from black to white, then the signs
\operatorname{sign}(v)
and
\varepsilon(\mathsf{x})
in (
4.3
)–(
4.4
) are
+1
. However, the freedom to vary these choices will be important in our proofs.

Our main result of this section is:

Theorem 4.3
.

The matrix
\mathsf{K}^{-1}
defined by (
4.3
) is the two-sided inverse of Fock’s Kasteleyn matrix
\mathsf{K}
on
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
.

Corollary 4.4
.

The graph
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
has an equal number of black and white vertices, and admits a dimer cover.

{v_{1}}
{v_{2}}
{v_{3}}
{v_{4}}
{\bm{\alpha}}
{\bm{\beta}}
{\bm{\gamma}}
{\bm{\delta}}
\alpha_{1}
\beta_{1}
\gamma_{1}
\delta_{1}
\alpha_{2}
\beta_{2}
\gamma_{2}
\delta_{2}
\alpha_{3}
\beta_{3}
\gamma_{3}
\delta_{3}

Figure 21
:
The sign intervals (left) and strands (right) in the Aztec diamond. The shaded face is the reference face
\mathsf{f}_{0}
.

4.2
Example: Aztec diamond

Consider the Aztec diamond with reference face
\mathsf{f}_{0}
and strands labeled as shown in Figure
21
(right). We switch to the notation in
[
29
]
and denote the four families of parallel strands by
\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma}
and
\bm{\delta}
as shown in Figure
21
(left). We check that (
4.3
) recovers the formula

\displaystyle K^{-1}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{C_{\mathsf{w}}}\int_{C_{\mathsf{b}}}\frac{1}{E(\zeta,\eta)}\frac{\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}+\eta-\zeta)}{\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}})}\frac{g_{\mathsf{w}}(\eta)}{g_{\mathsf{b}}(\zeta)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{E(\delta_{i},\eta)}{E(\beta_{i},\eta)}\frac{E(\beta_{i},\zeta)}{E(\delta_{i},\zeta)}

(4.5)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathsf{b}\text{ right of }\mathsf{w}\}}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{\mathsf{w}}}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}

(4.6)

in
[
29
, (17)]
, where
C_{\mathsf{b}}
(resp.
C_{\mathsf{w}}
) is a counterclockwise oriented curve that encloses only the angles
\bm{\gamma}
(resp.
\bm{\alpha}
).

In this example,

\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\beta_{i},\qquad\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\zeta)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{E(\delta_{i},\zeta)}{E(\beta_{i},\zeta)},

so the integrand inside the double integral is the same as in (
4.3
). There is only one inner chamber with sign intervals as shown in Figure
21
(left). Thus, we can take

I_{\mathsf{b}}=[v_{2},v_{3}],\qquad I_{\mathsf{w}}=[v_{1},v_{2}],\qquad v=v_{2},\qquad\operatorname{sign}(v)=1,

so that
C_{\mathsf{b}}=C(I_{\mathsf{b}})
and
C_{\mathsf{w}}=C(I_{\mathsf{w}})
have no common angles. Therefore, the double integral terms are identical.

Our single integral term is

-\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{b}}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle=A_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{v_{2}}.

If
\mathsf{b}
is on the left of (resp. above)
\mathsf{w}
,
g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
has no poles at angles in
\bm{\gamma}
(resp.
\bm{\alpha}
), so

-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathsf{b}\text{ right of }\mathsf{w}\}}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{\mathsf{w}}}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}=0.

On the other hand, in both cases,
v_{2}\in\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
so
A_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{v_{2}}=0
as well. If
\mathsf{b}
is below and to the right of
\mathsf{w}
, then
g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
has no poles at angles in
\bm{\beta}
and
\bm{\delta}
, so
v_{1}\in\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
, so we can take
I_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{v_{2}}=[v_{2},v_{1}]
. Since the total sum of residues of
g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
is
0
,

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C([v_{1},v_{2}])}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C([v_{2},v_{1}])}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}=0.

Hence,

A_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{v_{2}}=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{\mathsf{w}}}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}.

Therefore, the single integral terms agree in all cases, and the two formulas for
K^{-1}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
coincide.

Remark 4.5
.

In this example, the eight
a priori
different choices reduce to four distinct
formulas: when we interchange the roles of
\mathsf{b}
and
\mathsf{w}
, the double integral is unchanged since
C(I_{\mathsf{b}})
and
C(I_{\mathsf{w}})
do not enclose any common angles, and the single integral is unchanged since
I_{\mathsf{b}}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}}=\{v\}
and so changing
\varepsilon(\mathsf{b})
to
\varepsilon(\mathsf{w})
cancels the sign coming from changing
\partial I_{\mathsf{b}}
to
\partial I_{\mathsf{w}}
. The resulting expressions, indexed by the choice of convex vertex
v
, are

\displaystyle v_{1}:\qquad

\displaystyle-\int_{C([v_{1},v_{2}])}\int_{C([v_{4},v_{1}])}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}+\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{v_{1}},

(4.7)

\displaystyle v_{2}:\qquad

\displaystyle\int_{C([v_{1},v_{2}])}\int_{C([v_{2},v_{3}])}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}+\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{v_{2}},

(4.8)

\displaystyle v_{3}:\qquad

\displaystyle-\int_{C([v_{3},v_{4}])}\int_{C([v_{2},v_{3}])}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}+\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{v_{3}},

\displaystyle v_{4}:\qquad

\displaystyle\int_{C([v_{3},v_{4}])}\int_{C([v_{4},v_{1}])}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}+\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{v_{4}}.

Let us check that the expressions (
4.7
) and (
4.8
), corresponding to

v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, agree.
Keep
C(I_{\mathsf{w}})=C([v_{1},v_{2}])
fixed and deform
C(I_{\mathsf{b}})
from
C([v_{4},v_{1}])
to

-C([v_{2},v_{3}])
. During this deformation, the only pole of
\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
crossed is along the diagonal
\zeta=\eta
, so the change in the
double integral is the residue contribution

-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C([v_{1},v_{2}])}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}=\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{v_{2}}-\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{v_{1}}.

Finally, replacing the inner contour
-C([v_{2},v_{3}])
by
C([v_{2},v_{3}])
reverses the sign of the double integral. Therefore, the formulas
(
4.7
) and (
4.8
) coincide. The general case, proved in Proposition
4.11
, uses
the same underlying idea.

Remark 4.6
.

It is worth emphasizing that the indicators that appear in our formula (
4.4
) are of a different nature than the indicator in (
4.5
); indeed, as we have seen above, the latter indicator term is simply the whole-plane inverse
A_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{v_{2}}
.

Figure 23
:

A choice of uniformly spaced angles (left) and the corresponding isoradial embedding (right) of the graph in Figure
