. The colored edges form the quad graph
\mathsf{G}^{\diamond}
.

For simplicity, we consider the setting of
isoradial weights
from
[
78
]
. Namely, we take
\Sigma=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
and let

A_{0}=\{\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}:\ |\zeta|=1\}

be the unit circle. In this case, the theta function is identically equal to
1
, and the prime form is

E(\zeta,\eta)=\frac{\eta-\zeta}{\sqrt{d\zeta}\sqrt{d\eta}}.

After performing a gauge transformation that removes the factor

\frac{1}{\sqrt{d\zeta}\sqrt{d\eta}}
, Fock’s Kasteleyn matrix (
2.5
)
simplifies to

\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}=\beta-\alpha.

(4.9)

These weights have the following geometric interpretation. We associate to
\mathsf{G}
its
quad graph

\mathsf{G}^{\diamond}
, whose vertices are the vertices and faces of
\mathsf{G}
, and whose edges are the pairs
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{f})
with

\mathsf{x}\in B(\mathsf{G})\sqcup W(\mathsf{G}),\mathsf{f}\in F(\mathsf{G}),

such that
\mathsf{x}
lies on the boundary of
\mathsf{f}
. Thus, every face of
\mathsf{G}^{\diamond}
is a quadrilateral, and each edge of
\mathsf{G}
appears as one of its diagonals. The choice of angles determines an embedding of
\mathsf{G}^{\diamond}
in which every quadrilateral is a rhombus; see Figure
23
(left). Precisely, if an edge
\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}
is crossed by the strands
\alpha,\beta
, then the corresponding rhombus has opposite sides in the directions given by the unit complex numbers
\alpha
and
\beta
. In this
embedding, the Kasteleyn matrix entry (
4.9
) is the complex vector representing the dual diagonal of the rhombus. In particular, the isoradial weight is its modulus,
i.e.
the Euclidean length of the dual edge.

For small graphs, the formula (
4.3
) can be evaluated explicitly using residues.

Consider the graph shown in Figure
22
. For each edge

e\in E(N)
, we assume that all angles in
\bm{\alpha}_{e}
are
equal. To simplify notation, we denote these angles by

\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\epsilon
, and we label each edge of
N
by its
associated angle. Then, Fock’s Kasteleyn matrix is

\mathsf{K}=\begin{array}[]{cccccccc}&\mathsf{b}_{1}&\mathsf{b}_{2}&\mathsf{b}_{3}&\mathsf{b}_{4}&\mathsf{b}_{5}&\mathsf{b}_{6}&\mathsf{b}_{7}\\
\mathsf{w}_{1}&\delta-\gamma&\epsilon-\delta&0&\alpha-\epsilon&0&\beta-\alpha&\gamma-\beta\\
\mathsf{w}_{2}&\gamma-\beta&\beta-\epsilon&0&0&0&0&0\\
\mathsf{w}_{3}&0&\gamma-\beta&\beta-\alpha&0&0&0&0\\
\mathsf{w}_{4}&0&\delta-\gamma&\alpha-\delta&\gamma-\alpha&0&0&0\\
\mathsf{w}_{5}&0&0&0&\epsilon-\gamma&\beta-\epsilon&\gamma-\beta&0\\
\mathsf{w}_{6}&0&0&0&0&\epsilon-\delta&\delta-\gamma&0\\
\mathsf{w}_{7}&0&0&0&0&0&\alpha-\delta&\delta-\gamma\\
\end{array}.

Directly computing the inverse, we find, for instance,

\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{w}_{1}}^{-1}=-\frac{(\alpha-\beta)(\beta-\epsilon)(\gamma-\delta)}{(\alpha-\gamma)(\beta-\delta)^{2}(\gamma-\epsilon)},\qquad\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{b}_{5},\mathsf{w}_{1}}^{-1}=\frac{(\beta-\gamma)(\gamma-\delta)^{2}}{(\alpha-\gamma)(\beta-\delta)^{2}(\gamma-\epsilon)}.

(4.10)

We now verify that these expressions agree with the evaluation of
(
4.3
). From Figure
16
, we compute the discrete Abel map:

\begin{array}[]{c|c}&\bm{d}\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{w}_{1}&-\alpha\\
\mathsf{w}_{2}&-\alpha-\beta+\delta\\
\mathsf{w}_{3}&-\alpha-\beta-\gamma+\delta+\epsilon\\
\mathsf{w}_{4}&-\alpha+\gamma+\epsilon\\
\mathsf{w}_{5}&-\gamma\\
\mathsf{w}_{6}&\beta-\gamma-\delta\\
\mathsf{w}_{7}&-\alpha+\beta-\delta\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{c|c}&\bm{d}\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{b}_{1}&-\alpha+\gamma+\delta\\
\mathsf{b}_{2}&-\alpha+\delta+\epsilon\\
\mathsf{b}_{3}&-\gamma+\delta+\epsilon\\
\mathsf{b}_{4}&\epsilon\\
\mathsf{b}_{5}&\beta-\gamma+\epsilon\\
\mathsf{b}_{6}&\beta\\
\mathsf{b}_{7}&-\alpha+\beta+\gamma\end{array}

With our notation, (
3.5
) is

\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}}=\beta-\gamma+\delta-\epsilon,

so the boundary term

\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\zeta)=\frac{(\zeta-\beta)(\zeta-\delta)}{(\zeta-\gamma)(\zeta-\epsilon)}.

We first consider
\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{w}_{1}}
. We have

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}}=\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}_{1}}}=\vbox{\hbox{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}

so we can take

I_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}=[v_{1},v_{2}],\qquad I_{\mathsf{w}_{1}}=[v_{4},v_{1}],\qquad v=v_{1},\qquad\operatorname{sign}(v)=1.

The double integral is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}_{1}})}\int_{C(I_{\mathsf{b}_{1}})}\frac{1}{(\eta-\zeta)}\frac{(\zeta-\alpha)(\zeta-\epsilon)}{(\zeta-\beta)(\zeta-\delta)^{2}}\frac{(\eta-\beta)(\eta-\delta)}{(\eta-\alpha)(\eta-\gamma)(\eta-\epsilon)}d\zeta d\eta

\displaystyle=-\frac{(\alpha-\beta)(\beta-\epsilon)}{(\beta-\delta)^{2}}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}_{1}})}\frac{(\eta-\delta)}{(\eta-\alpha)(\eta-\gamma)(\eta-\epsilon)}d\eta

\displaystyle=-\frac{(\alpha-\beta)(\beta-\epsilon)}{(\beta-\delta)^{2}}\left(\frac{(\alpha-\delta)}{(\alpha-\gamma)(\alpha-\epsilon)}-\frac{(\delta-\epsilon)}{(\alpha-\epsilon)(\gamma-\epsilon)}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{(\alpha-\beta)(\beta-\epsilon)(\gamma-\delta)}{(\alpha-\gamma)(\beta-\delta)^{2}(\gamma-\epsilon)}.

Since
\bm{d}(\mathsf{w}_{1})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{b}_{1})=-\gamma-\delta
, the null sector
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{w}_{1}}=[v_{4},v_{2}]
. Since

\partial I_{\mathsf{b}}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}}=(v_{2}-v_{1})\cap[v_{4},v_{1}]=-v_{1},

the single integral term is

-\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{b}}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle=\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{v_{1}}=0

since
v_{1}\in\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{w}_{1}}
. Thus, the total agrees with (
4.10
).

We now consider
\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathsf{b}_{5},\mathsf{w}_{1}}
. We have

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}_{5}}}=\vbox{\hbox{

}}

so we can take

I_{\mathsf{b}_{5}}=[v_{3},v_{4}],\qquad I_{\mathsf{w}_{1}}=[v_{2},v_{3}],\qquad v=v_{3},\qquad\operatorname{sign}(v)=1.

The double integral is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}_{1}})}\int_{C(I_{\mathsf{b}_{5}})}\frac{1}{(\eta-\zeta)}\frac{(\zeta-\gamma)^{2}}{(\zeta-\beta)^{2}(\zeta-\delta)}\frac{(\eta-\beta)(\eta-\delta)}{(\eta-\alpha)(\eta-\gamma)(\eta-\epsilon)}d\zeta d\eta

\displaystyle=\frac{(\gamma-\delta)^{2}}{(\beta-\delta)^{2}}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}_{1}})}\frac{(\eta-\beta)}{(\eta-\alpha)(\eta-\gamma)(\eta-\epsilon)}d\eta

\displaystyle=\frac{(\beta-\gamma)(\gamma-\delta)^{2}}{(\alpha-\gamma)(\beta-\delta)^{2}(\gamma-\epsilon)},

Since
\bm{d}(\mathsf{w}_{1})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{b}_{5})=-\alpha-\beta+\gamma-\epsilon
, the null sector
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b}_{5},\mathsf{w}_{1}}=[v_{2},v_{4}]
. Since

\partial I_{\mathsf{b}}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}}=(v_{4}-v_{3})\cap[v_{2},v_{3}]=-v_{3},

the single integral term is

-\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{b}}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle=\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{v_{3}}=0

since
v_{3}\in\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b}_{5},\mathsf{w}_{1}}
. Thus, the total again agrees with (
4.10
).

4.4
Correctness of the definition of
\mathsf{K}^{-1}

\mathsf{b}
\beta_{e}
{\mathsf{x}}
\mathsf{b}
\beta_{e}
{\mathsf{x}}
{\mathsf{f}}

Figure 24
:
The two cases in the proof of Lemma
4.7
:
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{x}}}(e)=+
(left) and
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{x}}}(e)=-
(right).

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
e\in E(N)
. If
\mathsf{x}\in V(\mathsf{G})
is a vertex and
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{x}}}(e)=+
(resp.
-
), then

\ord_{\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{g_{\mathsf{x}}\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}}\right)\geq 0\quad(\text{resp. }\leq 0)

for all
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{e}
.

Proof.

By (
3.2
) and Lemma
2.11
, we have

\ord_{\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{g_{\mathsf{x}}\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}}\right)=(\bm{d}(\mathsf{y})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{x}))_{\alpha}

(4.11)

where
\mathsf{y}
is any
\beta_{e}
-outer vertex. Assume first that
e
is black so that
\mathsf{y}=\mathsf{b}
is a black vertex. We have two cases (see Figure
24
):

1.

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{x}}}(e)=+
: Any path from
\mathsf{x}
to
\mathsf{b}
crosses
\beta_{e}
positively,
i.e.

(\bm{d}(\mathsf{b})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{x}))_{\beta_{e}}=1
. By Lemma
2.12
,
(\bm{d}(\mathsf{b})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{x}))_{\alpha}\geq 0
for all
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{e}
.

2.

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{x}}}(e)=-
: Let
\mathsf{f}
be the face separated from
\mathsf{b}
by
\beta_{e}
. Any path from
\mathsf{x}
to
\mathsf{f}
crosses
\beta_{e}
negatively,
i.e.

(\bm{d}(\mathsf{f})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{x}))_{\beta_{e}}=-1
. By Lemma
2.12
,
(\bm{d}(\mathsf{f})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{x}))_{\alpha}\leq 0
for all
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{e}
. Since
\bm{d}(\mathsf{b})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{x})=\bm{d}(\mathsf{f})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{x})+\beta_{e}
, we get
(\bm{d}(\mathsf{b})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{x}))_{\alpha}\leq 0
for all
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{e}
.

The argument for white
e
is identical, with
\mathsf{b}
replaced by
\mathsf{w}
.
∎

Recall Definition
2.14
of the null sector
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
e\in E(N)
,
\mathsf{b}\in B(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})
and
\mathsf{w}\in W(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})
.
Assume that one of the following holds:

1.

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=+
and
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)=-
.

2.

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)=-
and
\mathsf{b}
lies on
\beta_{e}
.

3.

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=+
and
\mathsf{w}
lies on
\beta_{e}
.

Then
e\subset\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
.

Proof.

We consider each of the three cases:

1.

Suppose
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=+
and
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)=-
. Then

(\bm{d}(\mathsf{w})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{b}))_{\beta_{e}}=1,

so Lemma
2.11
shows that
g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
has a zero at
\beta_{e}
. The conclusion therefore follows from the definition of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
.

2.

Suppose
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)=-
and
\mathsf{b}
lies on
\beta_{e}
. Let
\mathsf{f}
be the face on the other side of
\beta_{e}
from
\mathsf{b}
, as in Figure
24
(right). Then

\bm{d}(\mathsf{w})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f})=\bm{d}(\mathsf{w})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{b})+\beta_{e}.

Thus,
g_{\mathsf{f},\mathsf{w}}
has the same zeros and poles in
A_{0}
as
g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
, except for one additional zero at
\beta_{e}
.

We now distinguish the two cases in Definition
2.14
of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
.

(a)

If
g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
has at least one zero, then
e\not\subset\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
would imply that the edges carrying zeros and poles of
g_{\mathsf{f},\mathsf{w}}
interlace along
\partial N
, contradicting Lemma
2.13
.

(b)

Suppose
g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
has no zeros in
A_{0}
and

\divisor(g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}})|_{A_{0}}=-\alpha-\beta,

where
\alpha,\beta\in\bm{\alpha}
are as in Definition
2.14
. Since
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)=-
, the vertex
\mathsf{w}
does not lie on the other side of
\beta_{e}
from
\mathsf{b}
, so
\alpha
and
\beta
are distinct from
\beta_{e}
. Choosing
\gamma=\beta_{e}
in Case 2 of Definition
2.14
, we get
e\subset\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
.

3.

This is symmetric to Case 2.

∎

We say that a cyclic interval
[a,b]\subset\partial N
is
nontrivial
if
a\neq b
; equivalently,
[a,b]
is neither a single point nor all of
\partial N
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
J=[a,b]
and
K=[c,d]
be nontrivial cyclic closed intervals in
\partial N
with endpoints in
V(N)
. Then, as
0
-chains,

\partial(J\cap K)=\mathbf{1}_{\{b\in K\}}b-\mathbf{1}_{\{a\in K\}}a+\mathbf{1}_{\{d\in J\}}d-\mathbf{1}_{\{c\in J\}}c+\mathbf{1}_{\{a=c\}}a-\mathbf{1}_{\{b=d\}}b.

(4.12)

Proof.

If
J
and
K
have no endpoints in common, then the formula is immediate: the boundary of
J\cap K
consists of those endpoints of
J
that lie in
K
, together with those endpoints of
K
that lie in
J
, with the usual signs. This gives

\partial(J\cap K)=\mathbf{1}_{\{b\in K\}}b-\mathbf{1}_{\{a\in K\}}a+\mathbf{1}_{\{d\in J\}}d-\mathbf{1}_{\{c\in J\}}c.

In general, the same expression remains correct except when two endpoints of the same type coincide. If
a=c
, then the point
a
is counted twice as a left endpoint, so one copy must be added back; this produces the term
\mathbf{1}_{\{a=c\}}a
. Similarly, if
b=d
, then the point
b
is counted twice as a right endpoint, so one copy must be subtracted; this produces the term
-\mathbf{1}_{\{b=d\}}b
. By contrast, if
a=d
or
b=c
, the two contributions occur with opposite signs and therefore cancel automatically, so no further correction is needed. This proves (
4.12
).
∎

Corollary 4.10
.

Let
\mathsf{b}\in B(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})
and
\mathsf{w}\in W(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})
. Let
J=[a,b]
and
K=[c,d]
be any two nontrivial closed intervals in
\partial N
with endpoints in
V(N)
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C(J\cap K)}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}=-\langle\partial J\cap K,\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle-\langle\partial K\cap J,\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle-\mathbf{1}_{\{a=c\}}\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{a}+\mathbf{1}_{\{b=d\}}\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{b}.

(4.13)

Proof.

We apply the linear functional
\langle\cdot,\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle
from (
4.2
) to (
4.12
); see also Definition
2.17
.
∎

Proposition 4.11
.

The formula (
4.3
) is:

1.

Invariant under swapping
\mathsf{x}
and
\mathsf{y}
.

2.

Invariant under fixing
I_{\mathsf{w}}
and expanding
I_{\mathsf{b}}
across any edge
e
such that:

•

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=+
, or

•

\mathsf{b}
lies on
\beta_{e}
and
e
is black (in particular, this holds whenever
\mathsf{b}
is
\beta_{e}
-outer).

Precisely, if
I_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}=[\ell_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime},r_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}]
is obtained from
I_{\mathsf{b}}
by a sequence of such expansions, then

\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}=\operatorname{sign}(v)\left(\iint_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime})}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}-\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle\right).

(4.14)

Similarly, we can fix
I_{\mathsf{b}}
and expand
I_{\mathsf{w}}
across any edge such that:

•

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)=-
, or

•

\mathsf{w}
lies on
\beta_{e}
and
e
is white (in particular, this holds whenever
\mathsf{w}
is
\beta_{e}
-outer).

3.

Independent of the choice of convex vertex
v
.

4.

Invariant under changing the choice of
R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{x}}
for an outer vertex
\mathsf{x}
whenever it is not unique.

Proof.

1.

It suffices to take
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})=(\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w})
. Exchanging the order of integration requires deforming contours so that, at common angles, the circles in
C(I_{\mathsf{w}})
lie strictly inside those in
C(I_{\mathsf{b}})
. The only new contribution arises from crossing the simple pole on the diagonal
\zeta=\eta
, giving

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{sign}(v)\left(\iint_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{b}})}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}-\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{b}}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{sign}(v)\left(\iint_{C(I_{\mathsf{b}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{w}})}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C(I_{\mathsf{b}}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}})}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}-\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{b}}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle\right).

Applying Corollary
4.10
to
I_{\mathsf{b}}=[\ell_{\mathsf{b}},r_{\mathsf{b}}]
and
I_{\mathsf{w}}=[\ell_{\mathsf{w}},r_{\mathsf{w}}]
, we get

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C(I_{\mathsf{b}}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}})}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}-\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{b}}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle=\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{w}}\cap I_{\mathsf{b}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle+\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell_{\mathsf{b}}=\ell_{\mathsf{w}}\}}\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{\ell_{\mathsf{b}}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{r_{\mathsf{b}}=r_{\mathsf{w}}\}}\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{r_{\mathsf{b}}}.

It remains to show that the two terms with indicators vanish. Consider the term
\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell_{\mathsf{b}}=\ell_{\mathsf{w}}\}}\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{\ell_{\mathsf{b}}}
. If
\ell_{\mathsf{b}}=\ell_{\mathsf{w}}
, let
e
be the boundary edge immediately clockwise from the common point
\ell_{\mathsf{b}}=\ell_{\mathsf{w}}
. Then, by definition of
I_{\mathsf{b}}
and
I_{\mathsf{w}}
,

\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=+,\qquad\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)=-.

We have the following cases:

•

\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)
and
\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)=\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)
: Lemma
4.8
implies that
\ell_{\mathsf{b}}=\ell_{\mathsf{w}}\in\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
and therefore,
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{\ell_{\mathsf{b}}}=0.

•

\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)\neq\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)
and
\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)=\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)
: Then
\mathsf{b}
is
\beta_{e}
-outer; in particular it lies on
\beta_{e}
. Therefore, Lemma
4.8
again implies that
\ell_{\mathsf{b}}=\ell_{\mathsf{w}}\in\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
and therefore,
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{\ell_{\mathsf{b}}}=0.

•

\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)
and
\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)\neq\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)
: This is symmetric to the previous case.

•

\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)\neq\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)
and
\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)\neq\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)
: This case cannot happen since it would mean that
\mathsf{b}
and
\mathsf{w}
are both
\beta_{e}
-outer but
\beta_{e}
-outer vertices must all have the same color.

Similarly,
\mathbf{1}_{\{r_{\mathsf{b}}=r_{\mathsf{w}}\}}\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{r_{\mathsf{b}}}=0
.

\ell_{\mathsf{w}}
\ell_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}
\ell_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}
r_{\mathsf{w}}
\ell_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}
\ell_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}

Figure 25
:
The two possible configurations in the case

\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)=\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)
considered in the proof of Item 2. The inner (resp. outer) cyclic interval is
I_{\mathsf{w}}
(resp.
I_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}
). The left panel shows the case
\ell^{\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}=\ell_{\mathsf{w}}
, and the right panel the case
\ell^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}=r_{\mathsf{w}}
. In both cases
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)=-
by definition of
I_{\mathsf{w}}
.

2.

By Item 1, it suffices to consider the case where
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})=(\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w})
and we expand
I_{\mathsf{b}}
. Moreover, since moving the right endpoint is analogous, it suffices to show that (
4.14
) is invariant under moving the left endpoint of
I_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}
one step,
i.e.
let

I^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}=[\ell^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{b}},r_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}],\qquad e=[\ell^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{b}},\ell_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}]\in E(N).

We deform the contour
C(I^{\prime}_{\mathsf{b}})
to
C(I^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{b}})
, keeping
C(I_{\mathsf{w}})
fixed. Since
\bm{\alpha}_{e}
does not contain any poles of the integrand (by Lemma
4.7
when
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=+
, or because
\eqref{eq:order_at_alpha}=0
if
\mathsf{b}
lies on
\beta_{e}
and
e
is black), the only poles that the deformation crosses are along the diagonal
\zeta=\eta
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\operatorname{sign}(v)\left(\iint_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime})}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}-\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{sign}(v)\left(\iint_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime})}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C(e\cap I_{\mathsf{w}})}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}-\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle\right).

Thus, it suffices to show

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C(e\cap I_{\mathsf{w}})}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}-\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle+\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C(e\cap I_{\mathsf{w}})}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}+\langle\partial e\cap I_{\mathsf{w}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle=0.

Applying Corollary
4.10
to the pair of intervals

e
and
I_{\mathsf{w}}=[\ell_{\mathsf{w}},r_{\mathsf{w}}]
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C(e\cap I_{\mathsf{w}})}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}+\langle\partial e\cap I_{\mathsf{w}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle

(4.15)

\displaystyle=-\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{w}}\cap e,\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle-\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}=\ell_{\mathsf{w}}\}}\mathsf{A}^{\ell^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}+\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell^{\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}=r_{\mathsf{w}}\}}\mathsf{A}^{\ell^{\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}

(4.16)

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell_{\mathsf{w}}\in e\}}\mathsf{A}^{\ell_{\mathsf{w}}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}=\ell_{\mathsf{w}}\}}\mathsf{A}^{\ell^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\Big)+\Big(-\mathbf{1}_{\{r_{\mathsf{w}}\in e\}}\mathsf{A}^{r_{\mathsf{w}}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}+\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell^{\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}=r_{\mathsf{w}}\}}\mathsf{A}^{\ell^{\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\Big).

(4.17)

Since
e=[\ell^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{b}},\ell^{\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}]
is an edge,

\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell_{\mathsf{w}}\in e\}}=\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell_{\mathsf{w}}=\ell_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}\}}+\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell_{\mathsf{w}}=\ell_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}\}},\qquad\mathbf{1}_{\{r_{\mathsf{w}}\in e\}}=\mathbf{1}_{\{r_{\mathsf{w}}=\ell_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}\}}+\mathbf{1}_{\{r_{\mathsf{w}}=\ell_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}\}}.

Thus, (
4.15
) becomes

\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell^{\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}=\ell_{\mathsf{w}}\}}\mathsf{A}^{\ell^{\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}=r_{\mathsf{w}}\}}\mathsf{A}^{\ell^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}.

(4.18)

We now have two cases:

•

\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)=\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)
: If
\ell^{\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}=\ell_{\mathsf{w}}
or if
\ell^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{b}}=r_{\mathsf{w}}
, then
e
is adjacent to an endpoint of
I_{\mathsf{w}}
, hence
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)=-
by definition of
I_{\mathsf{w}}
(see Figure
25
). Thus, the common endpoint lies in
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
by Cases 1 and 2 of Lemma
4.8
, corresponding exactly to the two cases in the hypothesis of Item 2.

•

\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)\neq\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e)
: Then
\mathsf{w}
is
\beta_{e}
-outer; in particular,
e
is white so the second hypothesis case that
\mathsf{b}
lies on
\beta_{e}
and
e
is black cannot occur. In the first hypothesis case where
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=+
, any common endpoints are again, as in the previous bullet point, in
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
by Case 1 of Lemma
4.8
.

Thus, in both cases, both terms in (
4.18
) are
0
, completing the proof of Item 2.

3.

By Item 1, it suffices to show that the formula is invariant under replacing
I_{\mathsf{b}}
with the other white sign interval
I_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}
for
R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}
. We can enlarge
I_{\mathsf{b}}
until it becomes the complement of
I_{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}
. For every edge
e
that we absorb along the way, either

•

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=+
, or

•

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)\neq\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)
so
\mathsf{b}
is
\beta_{e}
-outer.

Thus, (
4.14
) is unchanged by Item 2. We then reverse the orientation, which complements the interval and flips
\operatorname{sign}(v)
.

4.

By Item 1, we may assume that
\mathsf{x}=\mathsf{b}
is a black vertex.
By Condition 2 of Definition
3.2
, the edges

e\in E(N)
for which
\mathsf{b}
is
\beta_{e}
-outer form a consecutive
sequence

e_{1}<e_{2}<\cdots<e_{k}

along
\partial N
; see Figure
10
(right). Let
v_{1}<v_{2}<v_{3}<v_{4}
be the convex vertices of
N

such that
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}\subset[v_{2},v_{3}]
.

If we choose
e_{i}
to define
R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}
, then

\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e_{i})=+,\qquad\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e_{j})=-\quad(j\neq i).

Considering the inconsistently oriented cones as in the proof of Lemma
3.18
and Figure
20
, we obtain that
s_{v_{2}}(R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}})
is the left endpoint of
e_{i}
and

s_{v_{3}}(R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}})
is the right endpoint of
e_{i}
. If
v\in\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
, then

I_{\mathsf{b}}=[s_{v_{1}}(R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}),s_{v_{2}}(R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}})].

As
e_{i}
varies, the right endpoint of
I_{\mathsf{b}}
moves across the edges
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
. By Item 2, expanding or shrinking
I_{\mathsf{b}}
across these edges does not change the value of (
4.3
). The case
v\in\{v_{3},v_{4}\}
is analogous.

∎

4.5
Proof of Theorem
4.3

Our proof is based on the same general ideas as those used for the Aztec diamond in
[
29
]
. Proposition
4.11
allows us to simplify parts of the argument from that setting. The added complexity of our proof comes from features of the general setting that do not arise in the Aztec diamond case.

It suffices to check that
\mathsf{K}\mathsf{K}^{-1}=\mathsf{I}
; then
\mathsf{K}^{-1}\mathsf{K}=\mathsf{I}
(or, rather, its transpose) follows by Item 1 of Proposition
4.11
. Let
\mathsf{w},\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\in W(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})
. We need to show that

\sum_{\mathsf{b}\sim\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime},\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}=\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathsf{w}=\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\}}.

(4.19)

We distinguish two cases:

1.

\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
is an inner vertex: Suppose first that
R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}=R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}
for all
\mathsf{b}\sim\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
so that
I_{\mathsf{b}}
is the same interval
J=[\ell,r]
for all of them. Then, we get

\sum_{\mathsf{b}\sim\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime},\mathsf{b}}\left(\iint_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{b}})}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\right)=\iint_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}})\prec C(J)}\left(\sum_{\mathsf{b}\sim\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime},\mathsf{b}}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\right)=0

(4.20)

by Lemma
2.9
applied to
\mathsf{x}=\mathsf{f}_{0}
so that
\frac{1}{g_{\mathsf{b}}(\zeta)}=g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{f}_{0}}(\zeta)
. Moreover, by (
4.4
) and Theorem
2.16
,

-\sum_{\mathsf{b}\sim\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime},\mathsf{b}}\operatorname{sign}(v)\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{b}}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle=\operatorname{sign}(v)\big(\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell\in I_{\mathsf{w}}\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{r\in I_{\mathsf{w}}\}}\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathsf{w}=\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\}}.

In order for the right-hand side to be nonzero, we need
\mathsf{w}=\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
. In that case, we show that

\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell\in I_{\mathsf{w}}\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{r\in I_{\mathsf{w}}\}}=\operatorname{sign}(v).

(4.21)

Indeed, if
\operatorname{sign}(v)=+1
, then

J
(resp.
I_{\mathsf{w}}
) is the interval immediately counterclockwise (resp. clockwise)
of
s_{v}(R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}})=s_{v}(R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{w}})
, so
\ell\in I_{\mathsf{w}}
and
r\notin I_{\mathsf{w}}
, hence, the left-hand side equals
1
. If instead
\operatorname{sign}(v)=-1
, then
J
(resp.
I_{\mathsf{w}}
) is the interval immediately clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) of
s_{v}(R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}})
, so
r\in I_{\mathsf{w}}
and
\ell\notin I_{\mathsf{w}}
, hence, the left-hand side equals
-1
.

Now we consider the general case where the intervals
I_{\mathsf{b}}
may depend on
\mathsf{b}\sim\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
. We first claim that we can replace
I_{\mathsf{b}}
for all
\mathsf{b}\sim\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
by the single uniform choice of interval

J:=\bigcup_{\mathsf{b}\sim\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}I_{\mathsf{b}}

without changing the value of (
4.3
).

Let
I
denote the
-
sign interval for
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}}
that is adjacent to
s_{v}({\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime}})
. Let
e_{-}
(resp.
e_{+}
) denote the edge immediately clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) of
I
.

Lemma 4.12
.

For every
\mathsf{b}\sim\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
, the interval
I_{\mathsf{b}}
is obtained from
I

by adjoining a (possibly empty) subset of
\{e_{-},e_{+}\}
.

Proof.

We have two cases.

•

\mathsf{b}
is an inner vertex:
As we move from
{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}
to
{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}
, we cross exactly the two strands
passing through the edge
\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
. If one of the strands is
\beta_{e}
for some
e\in E(N)
, then
\operatorname{sign}{R_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}}(e)=+
changes to
\operatorname{sign}{R_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=-
. In particular, if we cross
\beta_{e}
for
e\in\{e_{-},e_{+}\}
,
I_{\mathsf{b}}
is obtained from
I
by shifting the corresponding endpoint outwards.

We claim that an endpoint cannot shift by more than one step.
If it did, then the two crossed strands would correspond to consecutive
edges
e
and
e^{\prime}
along
\partial N
.
In that case the edge
\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
would lie on both the zig-zag
paths
\beta_{e}
and
\beta_{e^{\prime}}
, hence would lie on

\partial\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
. This contradicts the assumption that both
\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{b}
are inner. Therefore, each endpoint moves by at most one step.

•

\mathsf{b}
is an outer vertex:
Since
\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
is inner by assumption and
\mathsf{b}\sim\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
, the edge
\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
lies on
at least one strand
\beta_{e}
for which
\mathsf{b}
is
\beta_{e}
-outer.
We may use such an
e
to define
R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}
. Then, moving from
{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}
to
R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}
crosses exactly one strand, namely
the strand through
\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
other than
\beta_{e}
. Hence, at most one endpoint of
I
shifts and by at most one step.

∎

We claim that every such adjoining step is allowed by Item 2 of
Proposition
4.11
.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
\mathsf{b}\sim\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
and let
e\in\{e_{-},e_{+}\}
. If
\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
lies on
\beta_{e}
and
e
is white, then
e\subset I_{\mathsf{b}}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{b}
lies on
\beta_{e}
,
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=-
. Since
e
is white,
\mathsf{b}
is not
\beta_{e}
-outer, so
\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=-
. On the other hand,
e
is one of the two boundary edges adjacent to the interval

I
. By Lemma
4.12
, the only way to obtain
I_{\mathsf{b}}
from
I
is
by adjoining some subset of
\{e_{-},e_{+}\}
. Since
\operatorname{sign}_{R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=-
, the edge
e
must be adjoined. Therefore,
e\subset I_{\mathsf{b}}
.
∎

Let
e\in\{e_{-},e_{+}\}
with
e\subset J\setminus I_{\mathsf{b}}
. Since
e
is adjacent to the white sign interval
I
for
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}}
, we have
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}}(e)=+
.
If
\mathsf{b}
does not lie on
\beta_{e}
, then passing from
{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}
to
{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}

does not cross
\beta_{e}
, so

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e)=\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}}(e)=+,

and the first hypothesis of Item 2 of Proposition
4.11
applies. If instead
\mathsf{b}
lies on

\beta_{e}
and
e
is black, then the second hypothesis of Item 2 of Proposition
4.11
applies.
Finally, if
\mathsf{b}
lies on
\beta_{e}
and
e
is white, then
Lemma
4.13
implies that
e\subset I_{\mathsf{b}}
already,
contrary to
e\subset J\setminus I_{\mathsf{b}}
. Thus, this case cannot occur.

Hence, every edge needed to enlarge
I_{\mathsf{b}}
to
J
can be adjoined using
Item 2 of Proposition
4.11
, and therefore, we may replace
every
I_{\mathsf{b}}
by the common interval
J
without changing the value of

\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
.

To complete the proof for inner
\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
, it remains to verify (
4.21
) when
\mathsf{w}=\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
. If
\operatorname{sign}(v)=+1
, then
I
(resp.
I_{\mathsf{w}}
) is the interval immediately counterclockwise (resp. clockwise) of
s_{v}(R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{w}})
.
Regardless of whether the endpoints of
J=[\ell,r]

are the same as
I
or pushed out one step,
\ell\in I_{\mathsf{w}}
and
r
is contained in the other black sign interval, so
r\notin I_{\mathsf{w}}
and therefore,
\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell\in I_{\mathsf{w}}\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{r\in I_{\mathsf{w}}\}}=1
. The case when
\operatorname{sign}(v)=-1
is analogous.

{\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}
\mathsf{b}_{1}
\mathsf{b}_{2}
\mathsf{b}_{3}
\mathsf{b}_{4}
\beta_{e_{1}}
\beta_{e_{3}}
{\beta_{e_{2}}}

Figure 26
:
The labeling of black vertices in a neighborhood of an outer white vertex
\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
when
k=3
. The shaded region represents faces of
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}}
. Solid edges belong to
E(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})
while dashed edges do not.

2.

\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
is an outer vertex:
By Condition 2 of Definition
3.2
, the edges

e\in E(N)
for which
\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
is
\beta_{e}
-outer form a consecutive block

e_{1}<e_{2}<\cdots<e_{k}

along
\partial N
. Let
v
be the nearest convex vertex to this block such that
\operatorname{sign}(v)=1
, and set

J:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}e_{i}.

(4.22)

Let
\mathsf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{k+1}
be the black vertices adjacent to
\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
that lie on at least one of the strands
\beta_{e_{i}}
,
1\leq i\leq k
, listed in counterclockwise order around
\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
, as in Figure
26
. By considering the inconsistently oriented cones, exactly as in the proof of Lemma
3.18
and in Figure
20
, we obtain

I_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}=e_{1},\quad I_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}=e_{1}\cup e_{2},\quad\dots,\quad I_{\mathsf{b}_{k}}=e_{k-1}\cup e_{k},\quad I_{\mathsf{b}_{k+1}}=e_{k}.

(4.23)

Equivalently, one can argue as follows. Passing from
\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
to
\mathsf{b}_{i}
, the number of sign changes increases from two to four by Lemma
3.15
. The only signs that change are those on the intervals appearing in (
4.23
), and they change from
+
to
-
. These intervals must therefore be precisely the negative sign intervals.

Now suppose that, in enlarging
I_{\mathsf{b}_{i}}
to
J
, one has to adjoin an edge
e_{j}
. Figure
26
shows that
\mathsf{b}_{i}
lies to the left of
\beta_{e_{j}}
, hence,
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}_{i}}}(e_{j})=+
. We may therefore apply Case 1 of Item 2 in Proposition
4.11
.

To use the same calculation as in (
4.20
), we extend the definition of
\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
to the black vertices
\mathsf{b}\sim\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{b}\in B({\mathsf{G}})\setminus B(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})
(the black vertices joined to
\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
by dashed edges in Figure
26
) by setting

\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}:=\operatorname{sign}(v)\left(\iint_{C(J)\prec C(I_{\mathsf{w}})}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}+\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{w}}\cap J,\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle\right).

(4.24)

We claim that (
4.24
) vanishes.

Indeed, since
\mathsf{b}
lies to the left of each strand
\beta_{e_{i}}
, the sign function

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}
is equal to
+
on
J
. Hence, by Lemma
4.7
,
the contour
C(J)
encloses no poles of
\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
, and therefore,

\iint_{C(J)\prec C(I_{\mathsf{w}})}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}=0.

For the remaining term, we need to know that the sign stays positive also on the two boundary edges adjacent to
J
.

Lemma 4.14
.

Assume
\mathsf{b}\sim\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{b}\in B({\mathsf{G}})\setminus B(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})
. Let
e_{-}
(resp.
e_{+}
) denote the edge immediately clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) of
J
. Then

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e_{-})=\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e_{+})=+.

Proof.

As we move from
\mathsf{b}_{1}
to
\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
to
\mathsf{b}
, Condition 2 of
Definition
3.2
implies that the only boundary strand crossed is

\beta_{e_{1}}
. Therefore,

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e_{-})=\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}}(e_{-})=+.

Applying the same argument to
\mathsf{b}_{k+1}
gives

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e_{+})=\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}_{k+1}}}(e_{+})=+.

∎

Write
I_{\mathsf{w}}=[\ell_{\mathsf{w}},r_{\mathsf{w}}]
. Then,

-\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{w}}\cap J,\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\rangle=\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell_{\mathsf{w}}\in J\}}\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{\ell_{\mathsf{w}}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{r_{\mathsf{w}}\in J\}}\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{r_{\mathsf{w}}}.

We show that both terms on the right-hand side vanish. Consider first the term

\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell_{\mathsf{w}}\in J\}}\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{\ell_{\mathsf{w}}}
, and assume that
\ell_{\mathsf{w}}\in J
. Let
e^{\prime}
be the edge immediately clockwise from
I_{\mathsf{w}}
. Since
e^{\prime}
is adjacent to
I_{\mathsf{w}}
, we have

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{w}}}(e^{\prime})=-.

On the other hand,
\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}
is positive on
J
and on the two edges adjacent to
J
, so

\operatorname{sign}_{{\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}}}(e^{\prime})=+.

It follows from Case 1 of Lemma
4.8
that
\ell_{\mathsf{w}}\in\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
, and therefore,

\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell_{\mathsf{w}}\in J\}}\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{\ell_{\mathsf{w}}}=0.

A similar argument shows that

\mathbf{1}_{\{r_{\mathsf{w}}\in J\}}{\mathsf{A}}^{r_{\mathsf{w}}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}=0.

We have thus shown that both terms in (
4.24
) are
0
. Therefore,

\sum_{\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\in E(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime},\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}=\sum_{\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\in E({\mathsf{G}})}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime},\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}=\operatorname{sign}(v)\big(\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell\in I_{\mathsf{w}}\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{r\in I_{\mathsf{w}}\}}\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathsf{w}=\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\}}.

as in (
4.20
).

Finally, we need to verify (
4.21
) when
\mathsf{w}=\mathsf{w}^{\prime}
. Recall the definition of
v
above (
4.22
). Suppose we use
e_{1}
to define
R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}
, so that

R^{\mathrm{in}}_{\mathsf{w}^{\prime}}={\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}.

Then
I_{\mathsf{w}}
and
I_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}=e_{1}
are adjacent sign intervals that meet at the vertex
s_{v}({\mathsf{R}}_{\mathsf{b}})=\ell
, so

\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell\in I_{\mathsf{w}}\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{r\in I_{\mathsf{w}}\}}=1=\operatorname{sign}(v).

The proof of Theorem
4.3
is complete.
∎

5
Scaling limits and astroidal domains

We now turn to the asymptotic consequences of the exact inverse Kasteleyn formula (
4.3
). Throughout the remainder of the paper, we assume that the angle function is periodic as in Section
2.4.6
.

5.1
Astroidal domains

In this section, we consider scaling limits of AZ graphs. Recall from Section
2.1
that
|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}
is the lattice length of
e\in E(N)
and
(a_{e},b_{e})=\vec{e}/|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Let

c:E(N)\to\mathbb{R}

be a function. For each
e\in E(N)
, consider the line

\bm{l}_{e}:=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:-b_{e}x+a_{e}y+c(e)=0\}.

(5.1)

For a vertex
v=e_{-}\cap e_{+}\in V(N)
, let

\bm{p}_{v}:=\bm{l}_{e_{-}}\cap\bm{l}_{e_{+}}

denote the point of intersection.

We first define a cycle in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
analogous to the medial boundary cycle construction. Let
\partial\mathcal{D}_{c}
be the counterclockwise-oriented cycle obtained as follows: it visits the points
\bm{p}_{v}
in the clockwise cyclic order of
v\in V(N)
, and for each edge
e=[v_{-},v_{+}]\in E(N)
, it goes from
\bm{p}_{v_{+}}
to
\bm{p}_{v_{-}}
along
\bm{l}_{e}
. We do not assume that
\bm{p}_{v_{+}}\neq\bm{p}_{v_{-}}
.

Assume that
\partial\mathcal{D}_{c}
is a simple cycle, and let
\mathcal{D}_{c}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the closed set bounded by
\partial\mathcal{D}_{c}
. We say that
\mathcal{D}_{c}
is
admissible
if

\sum_{e\in E(N)}|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}c(e)=0.

(5.2)

Definition 5.1
.

We call
\mathcal{D}_{c}
an
astroidal domain
if:

1.

the cycle
\partial\mathcal{D}_{c}
is simple,

2.

\mathcal{D}_{c}
is admissible.

Astroidal domains naturally arise as scaling limits of AZ graphs. Let
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}},n=1,2,\dots,
be a family of AZ graphs such that for each
e\in E(N)
,

c_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}(e)=n|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}c(e)+O(1)\qquad\text{as $n\to\infty$}.

(5.3)

By Lemma
3.8
,
\mathcal{D}_{c}
is admissible. If
\partial\mathcal{D}_{c}
is also simple, then
\mathcal{D}_{c}
is an astroidal domain.

Remark 5.2
.

For every Newton polygon
N
, one can construct an astroidal domain. Choose two consecutive edges
e_{1},e_{2}\in E(N)
, and write

e_{1}=[v_{0},v_{1}],\qquad e_{2}=[v_{1},v_{2}],

where
v_{0},v_{1},v_{2}\in V(N)
occur consecutively in the cyclic order on

\partial N
. We first choose
c:E(N)\to\mathbb{R}
so that

\partial\mathcal{D}_{c}
is simple and so that the three vertices of
\partial\mathcal{D}_{c}
corresponding to
v_{0},v_{1},v_{2}
are three consecutive convex vertices. To arrange this, first place the oriented lines
\bm{l}_{e_{1}}
and

\bm{l}_{e_{2}}
. Among the four cones determined by these two oriented lines at

\bm{p}_{v_{1}}=\bm{l}_{e_{1}}\cap\bm{l}_{e_{2}},

choose one of the inconsistently oriented cones. Then choose the
remaining lines
\bm{l}_{e}
,
e\neq e_{1},e_{2}
, in their cyclic order,
so that the rest of
\partial\mathcal{D}_{c}
is a simple polygonal
chain contained in this cone.

We color the sides as in the definition of AZ graphs: an edge

e\in E(N)
is black if the orientation of
\bm{l}_{e}
induced by

e
agrees with the orientation of the corresponding side of

\partial\mathcal{D}_{c}
, and white otherwise. The color changes occur precisely at the convex vertices of
\mathcal{D}_{c}
. Hence, the two sides corresponding to
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
have opposite colors. Moreover, either of them can be translated arbitrarily far outward while keeping
\partial\mathcal{D}_{c}
simple. Replacing
c(e)
by
c(e)+t
for a black side, or by

c(e)-t
for a white side, translates that side outward. Thus, by translating
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
outward, we can make the domain admissible.

Astroidal domains can also be obtained from the tropical limit of the
Aztec diamond, as in Appendix
A
.

Once
an astroidal domain
\mathcal{D}_{c}
is fixed, one can choose boundary
zig-zag paths so as to obtain a sequence of AZ graphs
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}

satisfying (
5.3
).

We expect the inverse Kasteleyn formula and the asymptotic analysis
developed in this paper to extend to more general domains, including domains whose boundary cycle is not simple. For this reason, we do not pursue a more systematic construction of astroidal domains here beyond the above argument showing that the class is nonempty.

5.2
Cell decomposition and sign changes

The collection of lines
\{\bm{l}_{e}:e\in E(N)\}
determines a cell decomposition of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
; see Figure
5
. To each (0-, 1- or 2-) cell
R
, we associate a
sign function

\operatorname{sign}_{R}:E(N)\rightarrow\{+,-,0\},

defined exactly as in (
3.11
), except that we use the oriented line
\bm{l}_{e}
(oriented in the direction of
\vec{e}
) in place of the strand
\beta_{e}
. Explicitly, for
e\in E(N)
,

\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e)=\begin{cases}+,&\text{if $R$ lies on the left-hand side of $\bm{l}_{e}$,}\\
-,&\text{if $R$ lies on the right-hand side of $\bm{l}_{e}$,}\\
0,&\text{if $R\subset\bm{l}_{e}$.}\end{cases}

The
chambers
are precisely those cells for which
\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e)\neq 0
for all
e\in E(N)
. We say that a cell
R
has a
sign change

1.

at a vertex
v\in V(N)
if
\operatorname{sign}_{R}
takes opposite values on the two edges of
\partial N
incident to
v
.

2.

along a closed interval
[v_{-},v_{+}]\subset\partial N
with
v_{-},v_{+}\in V(N)
if
\operatorname{sign}_{R}=0
on
[v_{-},v_{+}]
and
\operatorname{sign}_{R}
takes opposite values on the two adjacent boundary intervals.

With this convention, all definitions and results of Section
3.6
admit direct continuous analogues. In particular, we have the following continuous analogue of Lemma
3.15
.

Lemma 5.3
.

For each cell
R\subset\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
(resp.
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
),
\operatorname{sign}_{R}
has four (resp. two) sign changes.

5.3
Characterization of astroidal domains

The limit shape offers a natural perspective on the astroidal domains, which we discuss in this section. Throughout this discussion, we assume that the points
\bm{p}_{v}
are pairwise distinct for all
v\in V(N)
.

Let
N^{\perp}
be the polygon obtained (up to translation) by rotating
N
by
-\frac{\pi}{2}
. For any
v\in V(N)
we denote the corresponding vertex of
N^{\perp}
by
(s^{v},t^{v})\in V(N^{\perp})
.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
\partial\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a simple polygon and assume there is an order-preserving bijection
v\mapsto\bm{p}_{v}
from
V(N)
to
V(\partial\mathcal{D})
. Assume further that there is a continuous piecewise linear function
\mathfrak{h}_{0}:\partial\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}
such that, for all
v\in V(N)
,

\mathfrak{h}_{0}(x,y)=(x,y)\cdot(s^{v},t^{v})-d_{v}

(5.4)

along the two line segments of
\partial\mathcal{D}
adjacent to
\bm{p}_{v}
, for some constant
d_{v}
. Then the enclosed region
\mathcal{D}
is an astroidal domain
\mathcal{D}_{c}
with

c(e)=\frac{d_{v_{+}}-d_{v_{-}}}{|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}}

(5.5)

for
e=[v_{-},v_{+}]\in E(N)
.

Remark 5.5
.

We do not assume that the edges of
\partial\mathcal{D}
are parallel to the edges of
N
, nor that the map
v\mapsto\bm{p}_{v}
reverses orientation. Both properties follow from (
5.4
).

Remark 5.6
.

In Section
7.2
below, we obtain the limit shape for astroidal domains, and that limit shape indeed satisfies the boundary condition (
5.4
).

Proof.

Recall the conventions from Section
2.1
. We divide the proof into three steps. First, we show that each edge of
\partial\mathcal{D}
is parallel to its corresponding edge of
N
. Next, we show that the orientation of
\partial\mathcal{D}
is reversed compared to that of
\partial N
. Finally, we verify admissibility.

1.

Let
e=[v_{-},v_{+}]\in E(N)
and let
\bm{l}_{e}
be the line through
\bm{p}_{v_{-}}
and
\bm{p}_{v_{+}}
. For any
(x,y)\in\bm{l}_{e}
, (
5.4
) holds with
v=v_{-}
and with
v=v_{+}
. Subtracting the two expressions, we find that the equation for the line
\bm{l}_{e}
is

(x,y)\cdot(s^{v_{+}}-s^{v_{-}},t^{v_{+}}-t^{v_{-}})-d_{v_{+}}+d_{v_{-}}=0.

(5.6)

By the definition of
(s^{v},t^{v})\in V(N^{\perp})
,

(s^{v_{+}}-s^{v_{-}},t^{v_{+}}-t^{v_{+}})=|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}(b_{e},-a_{e}),

(5.7)

and therefore,

(x,y)\cdot|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}(b_{e},-a_{e})-d_{v_{+}}+d_{v_{-}}=0.

(5.8)

In particular,
\vec{e}=|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}(a_{e},b_{e})
and
\bm{l}_{e}
are parallel.

2.

For any
e=[v_{-},v_{+}]\in E(N)
, let

\vec{u}_{e}:=(s^{v_{+}}-s^{v_{-}},t^{v_{+}}-t^{v_{-}}),\quad\text{and}\quad\vec{w}_{e}:=\bm{p}_{v_{+}}-\bm{p}_{v_{-}}.

(5.9)

For any
v=e_{-}\cap e_{+}\in V(N)
, let
\theta_{v}{\in(-\pi,\pi)}
be the angle between
\vec{u}_{e_{-}}
and
\vec{u}_{e_{+}}
and let
\varphi_{v}{\in(-\pi,\pi)}
be the angle between
\vec{w}_{e_{-}}
and
\vec{w}_{e_{+}}
. Since
N
is convex and oriented counterclockwise,
\theta_{v}\in(0,\pi)
. It follows, using
\vec{u}_{e}\perp\vec{w}_{e}
, that

\varphi_{v}=\theta_{v}\quad\text{or}\quad\varphi_{v}=\theta_{v}-\pi.

(5.10)

We say that
\vec{w}_{e}
is
reversed
at
v
if
\varphi_{v}=\theta_{v}-\pi
. If
\vec{w}_{e}
is reversed at
v
, it means that
\vec{w}_{e}
changes from being an inward pointing normal to an outward pointing normal of
N^{\perp}
, or the other way around.

Since
\partial N
is simple and oriented counterclockwise,

\sum_{v\in V(N)}\theta_{v}=2\pi.

(5.11)

So, by summing over the angles
\varphi_{v}
, we get

\sum_{v\in V(N)}\theta_{v}-\pi\#\{v\in V(N):\vec{w}_{e}\text{ is reversed at $v$}\}=2\pi-\pi\#\{v\in V(N):\vec{w}_{e}\text{ is reversed at $v$}\}.

(5.12)

Since
\partial D
is simple, the above sum is equal to
2\pi
or
-2\pi
according to whether
\partial D
is oriented counterclockwise or clockwise. We therefore only need to show that
\vec{w}_{e}
is reversed at least once.

By the assumption (
5.4
),

\mathfrak{h}_{0}(\bm{p}_{v_{+}})-\mathfrak{h}_{0}(\bm{p}_{v_{-}})=\vec{w}_{e}\cdot(s^{v_{+}},t^{v_{+}}).

(5.13)

Summing over all
e\in E(N)
yields

\sum_{e=[v_{-},v_{+}]\in E(N)}\vec{w}_{e}\cdot(s^{v_{+}},t^{v_{+}})=0.

(5.14)

Now fix
(s,t)\in N^{\perp}
. Since
\sum_{e\in E(N)}\vec{w}_{e}=0
, we have

\sum_{e=[v_{-},v_{+}]\in E(N)}\vec{w}_{e}\cdot(s^{v_{+}}-s,t^{v_{+}}-t)=0.

(5.15)

Since
N^{\perp}
is convex, the quantity

\vec{w}_{e}\cdot(s^{v_{+}}-s,t^{v_{+}}-t)

(5.16)

changes sign at
v\in V(N)
only if
\vec{w}_{e}
is reversed at
v
. Hence,
\vec{w}_{e}
is reversed at least once and so
\partial\mathcal{D}
is oriented opposite to
\partial N
.

3.

Let
e=[v_{-},v_{+}]
. With
c(e)=(d_{v_{+}}-d_{v_{-}})/|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}
as in the statement, the line
\bm{l}_{e}
given by the equation (
5.8
) coincides with the corresponding line given before Definition
5.1
. The domain is admissible since

\sum_{e\in E(N)}|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}c(e)=\sum_{e=[v_{-},v_{+}]\in E(N)}(d_{v_{+}}-d_{v_{-}})=0.

(5.17)

∎

Remark 5.7
.

The previous proposition shows that, in the language of
[
8
]
, astroidal domains are natural domains with natural boundary values.

6
Phase regions and the arctic curve

In this section, we study the geometry of the phase regions and the arctic curve, building on the methods developed in
[
17
,
11
,
19
,
35
,
48
]
in the context of dimer models. See also
[
85
]
for similar arguments in a different context. Recall that we assume that the angle function is periodic as in Section
2.4.6
.

6.1
Action
1
-form and action function

Fix an astroidal domain
\mathcal{D}_{c}
and a sequence of AZ graphs

\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}},n=1,2,\dots,

such that

c_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}(e)=n|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}c(e)+O(1),\qquad e\in E(N).

Recall the notion of imaginary-normalized differentials from Section
2.3.4
. We also recall the (multi-valued) functions

z(\zeta):=\prod_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{\mathbb{T}}}E(\alpha,\zeta)^{-b_{e(\alpha)}},\qquad w(\zeta):=\prod_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{\mathbb{T}}}E(\alpha,\zeta)^{a_{e(\alpha)}},

introduced in (
2.10
). By Lemma
2.6
, the logarithmic differentials
d\log z
and
d\log w
are
imaginary-normalized meromorphic
1
-forms with residues

\res_{\alpha}(d\log z)=-b_{e(\alpha)},\qquad\res_{\alpha}(d\log w)=a_{e(\alpha)}.

Definition 6.1
.

For
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we define the
action
1
-form

\lambda_{c}(x,y)
to be the unique
imaginary-normalized meromorphic
1
-form such that

\res_{\alpha}\bigl(\lambda_{c}(x,y)\bigr)=-b_{e(\alpha)}x+a_{e(\alpha)}y+c(e(\alpha))\qquad\text{for all }\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}.

Let

\bm{D}_{c}:=\sum_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}c(e(\alpha))\alpha.

(6.1)

Then the differential

xd\log z+yd\log w+\omega_{\bm{D}_{c}}

is imaginary-normalized by Lemma
2.6
and it has residue at
\alpha
equal to

-b_{e(\alpha)}x+a_{e(\alpha)}y+c(e(\alpha)).

Hence, by Lemma
2.5
,

\lambda_{c}(x,y)=xd\log z+yd\log w+\omega_{\bm{D}_{c}}.

(6.2)

The reason for introducing
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
is that it governs the leading exponential factor in the inverse Kasteleyn matrix formula (
4.3
). To make this explicit, we introduce its multi-valued primitive.

Definition 6.2
.

The
action function
is the multi-valued function on
\Sigma
defined by

\Lambda_{c}(\zeta;x,y):=\sum_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}\bigl(-b_{e(\alpha)}x+a_{e(\alpha)}y+c(e(\alpha))\bigr)\log E(\alpha,\zeta).

(6.3)

By construction,

d\Lambda_{c}(\cdot;x,y)=\lambda_{c}(x,y).

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and let
(j_{n},k_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be such that

(j_{n},k_{n})=n(x,y)+O(1).

For every
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\Bigl(-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{0}+(j_{n},k_{n}))-\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}\Bigr)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-b_{e(\alpha)}x+a_{e(\alpha)}y+c(e(\alpha))+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\res_{\alpha}\bigl(\lambda_{c}(x,y)\bigr)+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right).

Proof.

Lemma
3.8
implies that

(\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})_{\alpha}=-\frac{c_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}(e(\alpha))}{|e(\alpha)|_{\mathbb{Z}}}+O(1).

On the other hand,

\bm{d}(\mathsf{x}+(j,k))=\bm{d}(\mathsf{x})+\sum_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}\bigl(b_{e(\alpha)}j-a_{e(\alpha)}k\bigr)\alpha,

so

\Bigl(-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{0}+(j_{n},k_{n}))-\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}\Bigr)_{\alpha}=-b_{e(\alpha)}j_{n}+a_{e(\alpha)}k_{n}+\frac{c_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}(e(\alpha))}{|e(\alpha)|_{\mathbb{Z}}}+O(1).

Using

(j_{n},k_{n})=n(x,y)+O(1),\qquad c_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}(e)=n|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}c(e)+O(1),

we obtain

\frac{1}{n}\Bigl(-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{0}+(j_{n},k_{n}))-\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}\Bigr)_{\alpha}=-b_{e(\alpha)}x+a_{e(\alpha)}y+c(e(\alpha))+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right).

∎

The previous lemma identifies the asymptotic exponent of the prime-form factor in the kernel (
4.1
). More precisely, if
\mathsf{b}_{n}
and
\mathsf{w}_{n}
are vertices whose macroscopic location is
(x,y)
, then

(\bm{d}(\mathsf{b}_{n})+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})_{\alpha}=n\res_{\alpha}(\lambda_{c}(x,y))+O(1),\qquad(\bm{d}(\mathsf{w}_{n})+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})_{\alpha}=n\res_{\alpha}(\lambda_{c}(x,y))+O(1),

and therefore,

\frac{\prod_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}E(\alpha,\eta)^{(\bm{d}(\mathsf{w}_{n})+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})_{\alpha}}}{\prod_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}E(\alpha,\zeta)^{(\bm{d}(\mathsf{b}_{n})+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})_{\alpha}}}=\exp\Bigl(n\bigl(\Lambda_{c}(\eta;x,y)-\Lambda_{c}(\zeta;x,y)\bigr)+O(1)\Bigr).

(6.4)

In other words,
\Lambda_{c}
is the action governing the exponential part of the kernel.

6.2
Phase regions and boundary strata of the limit shape

The continuous analogue of Lemma
4.7
is:

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
e\in E(N)
and let
R
be a cell. Then, for all
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
and
(x,y)\in R
,

\operatorname{sign}(\res_{\alpha}(\lambda_{c}(x,y)))=\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e(\alpha)).

Proof.

By Definition
6.1
, for every
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{e}
we have

\res_{\alpha}(\lambda_{c}(x,y))=-b_{e(\alpha)}x+a_{e(\alpha)}y+c(e(\alpha)),

which is the defining linear function of
\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}
. Since
\bm{l}_{e}
is oriented along
(a_{e(\alpha)},b_{e(\alpha)})
, this linear function is positive on the left-hand side of
\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}
, negative on the right-hand side, and zero on
\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}
. Therefore,

\operatorname{sign}\bigl(\res_{\alpha}(\lambda_{c}(x,y))\bigr)=\operatorname{sign}_{R}({e(\alpha)})

for all
(x,y)\in R
.
∎

Recall that we always count zeroes with multiplicity.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and let
R
be the cell containing
(x,y)
. Then:

•

For each
1\leq j\leq g
, the number of zeroes of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
in
A_{j}
is even and at least two.

•

For consecutive poles
\alpha,\beta\in A_{0}
with
e(\alpha)\neq e(\beta)
,
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has an odd (resp. even) number of zeroes in
(\alpha,\beta)\subset A_{0}
when the residues at
\alpha
and

\beta
have the same (resp. opposite) sign. In particular, there is at least one zero in
(\alpha,\beta)
when its residues have the same sign.

•

For consecutive poles
\alpha,\beta\in A_{0}
with
e(\alpha)=e(\beta)
and
\res_{\alpha}(\lambda_{c}(x,y))=\res_{\beta}(\lambda_{c}(x,y))\neq 0
,
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has an odd number of zeroes in
(\alpha,\beta)\subset A_{0}
. In particular, there is at least one zero.

The above accounts for all but possibly two zeroes of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
. If
R\subset\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
, there are exactly two remaining zeroes, while if
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
, then all zeroes are accounted for.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.7
, together with
Lemma
6.4
, the zeroes forced by the above conditions account for

2g+\sum_{e\in E(N):\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e)\neq 0}|\bm{\alpha}_{e}|-\#\{\text{sign changes of }\operatorname{sign}_{R}\}

zeroes of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
. Since
\deg(\lambda_{c}(x,y))=2g-2
and
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has

\sum_{e\in E(N):\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e)\neq 0}|\bm{\alpha}_{e}|

simple poles, its total number of zeroes is

2g+\sum_{e\in E(N):\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e)\neq 0}|\bm{\alpha}_{e}|-2.

Hence, the number of zeroes of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
not accounted for by the above
conditions is exactly

\#\{\text{sign changes of $\operatorname{sign}_{R}$}\}-2.

The final claim now follows from Lemma
5.3
, which gives four sign changes when
R\subset\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
and two sign changes when
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
. With the conclusion of Lemma
6.5
, exactly one of the following holds:

1.

The two remaining zeroes form a
\sigma
-conjugate pair

\{\zeta,\sigma(\zeta)\},\qquad\zeta\in\Sigma_{+}^{\circ},\ \sigma(\zeta)\in\Sigma_{-}^{\circ}.

2.

The two remaining zeroes are real and lie in the same connected
component of

\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\{\text{poles of $\lambda_{c}(x,y)$}\}.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.6
, the zero set of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
is invariant under
\sigma
. Hence, these two remaining zeroes either both lie on
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
or else they form a
\sigma
-conjugate pair.

Assume they lie on
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
. By Lemma
6.5
, on each connected
component of
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\{\text{poles of $\lambda_{c}(x,y)$}\}
, the number of zeroes of

\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has a prescribed parity, and the parity of the number of zeroes already forced is correct, so the two remaining zeroes must lie in the same connected component of
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\{\text{poles of $\lambda_{c}(x,y)$}\}
.
∎

We now give a partial classification of regions in
\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
according to the locations of these two remaining zeroes. For some of these regions, we also define a map
\Omega
to
\Sigma_{+}
. The reader is encouraged to look ahead to Section
6.8
where we give a geometric reformulation.

Definition 6.7
.

We say that
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
:

1.

lies in the
rough region

\mathcal{L}
if
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has a

\sigma
-conjugate pair of zeroes. In this case, there is a unique zero

\zeta\in\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
, and we define

\Omega:\mathcal{L}\to\Sigma_{+}^{\circ},\qquad\Omega(x,y):=\zeta.

2.

lies in the
frozen region

\mathcal{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
, where

(\alpha,\beta)\subset A_{0}
is the open interval between two consecutive
angles
\alpha,\beta
with
e(\alpha)\neq e(\beta)
, such that

\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has exactly two simple zeroes in
(\alpha,\beta)
when
the vertex
e(\alpha)\cap e(\beta)\in V(N)
is convex, and two or three
simple zeroes when it is concave.

3.

lies in the
quasi-frozen region

\mathcal{Q}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
,
where
(\alpha,\beta)\subset A_{0}
is the open interval between two consecutive
angles
\alpha,\beta
with
e(\alpha)=e(\beta)
, if
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has
three simple zeroes in
(\alpha,\beta)
.

4.

lies in the
smooth region

\mathcal{G}_{j}
, for
1\leq j\leq g
, if

\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has four simple zeroes in
A_{j}
.

5.

lies on the
frozen-rough boundary

\mathscr{A}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
, where

(\alpha,\beta)\subset A_{0}
is the open interval between two consecutive
angles
\alpha,\beta
with
e(\alpha)\neq e(\beta)
, if
\lambda_{c}(x,y)

has a zero
\zeta
of order two or three in
(\alpha,\beta)
. In this case,

\zeta
is unique, and we define

\Omega:\mathscr{A}_{(\alpha,\beta)}\to(\alpha,\beta),\qquad\Omega(x,y):=\zeta.

If
\zeta
is a zero of order three, which can occur only when the vertex

e(\alpha)\cap e(\beta)\in V(N)
is concave, we call
(x,y)
a

frozen cusp
.

6.

lies on the
quasi-frozen-rough boundary

\mathscr{A}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
, where

(\alpha,\beta)\subset A_{0}
is the open interval between two consecutive
angles
\alpha,\beta
with
e(\alpha)=e(\beta)
, if
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has
a zero
\zeta
of order two or three in
(\alpha,\beta)
. In this case,

\zeta
is unique, and we define

\Omega:\mathscr{A}_{(\alpha,\beta)}\to(\alpha,\beta),\qquad\Omega(x,y):=\zeta.

If
\zeta
is a zero of order three, we call
(x,y)
a

quasi-frozen cusp
.

7.

lies on the
smooth-rough boundary

\mathscr{A}_{A_{j}}
, for

1\leq j\leq g
, if
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has a total of four zeroes in
A_{j}
(counted with multiplicity), one of which is a zero
\zeta
of order two or three. In this case,
\zeta
is unique: if
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
had
two double zeroes on
A_{j}
, then it would have constant sign on
A_{j}
,
forcing

\int_{A_{j}}\lambda_{c}(x,y)\neq 0,

contradicting imaginary-normalization. We therefore define

\Omega:\mathscr{A}_{A_{j}}\to A_{j},\qquad\Omega(x,y):=\zeta.

If
\zeta
is a zero of order three, we call
(x,y)
a

smooth cusp
; an order four zero is excluded by the same argument that rules out two double zeroes.

Remark 6.8
.

The above list is only a partial classification of the various singularities that can occur and does not in general exhaust
\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
; for example, it does not include the frozen–frozen boundary in Figure
6
. We do not attempt a complete classification of all possibilities in this paper.

We set

\mathscr{A}_{A_{0}\setminus\bm{\alpha}}:=\bigsqcup_{(\alpha,\beta)\subset A_{0}}\mathscr{A}_{(\alpha,\beta)},

where the union ranges over all open intervals
(\alpha,\beta)
between
consecutive angles on
A_{0}
. We also set

\mathscr{A}^{\circ}:=\left(\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{g}\mathscr{A}_{A_{j}}\right)\sqcup\mathscr{A}_{A_{0}\setminus\bm{\alpha}}.

The next few subsections describe the geometry of the map
\Omega
.
We first show in Section
6.3
that
\Omega
is a diffeomorphism from the rough region
\mathcal{L}
onto
\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
. We then extend the inverse map
\Omega^{-1}
to

\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}
in Section
6.4
. Finally, in Section
6.5
, we extend
\Omega^{-1}
to the points of
\bm{\alpha}
, whose images will be denoted by
\mathscr{A}_{\bm{\alpha}}
; these points are called
turning points
. Once
\mathscr{A}_{\bm{\alpha}}
has been defined, we define the
arctic curve

\mathscr{A}:=\mathscr{A}^{\circ}\sqcup\mathscr{A}_{\bm{\alpha}}.

We then show in Proposition
6.17
that these constructions glue together to give a smooth map

\Omega^{-1}:\Sigma_{+}\to\overline{\mathcal{L}}.

In particular, we will prove that the arctic curve is precisely the boundary of the rough region,
i.e.

\mathscr{A}=\partial\mathcal{L},

and moreover that the restriction

\Omega^{-1}\big|_{\Sigma(\mathbb{R})}:\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\to\mathscr{A}

is a smooth parametrization of
\mathscr{A}
. By a smooth parameterization, we mean that the components of
\Omega^{-1}
are smooth functions on
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
. The image
\mathscr{A}
need not be smooth;
indeed, it typically has cusps at the critical values of
\Omega^{-1}
.

Remark 6.9
.

By general theory
[
8
]
, the arctic curve is algebraic. The characterization of the arctic curve via the zeros of
\lambda_{c}
fits naturally into the framework developed in
[
101
]
. It may provide an alternative proof that the arctic curve is algebraic while simultaneously determining the degree of the curve. We will not pursue this argument here.

6.3
The rough region and the interior parametrization

Recall from (
2.10
) the definition of the functions
z(\zeta)
and
w(\zeta)
. While these functions may be multi-valued, the logarithmic differentials
d\log z
and
d\log w
are nevertheless well-defined meromorphic 1-forms.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
. All zeroes of the

1
-form

xd\log z+yd\log w

lie in
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}.
Moreover, all zeroes that lie in the oval
A_{0}
are simple.

Proof.

The
1
-form has residues

\res_{\alpha}(xd\log z+yd\log w)=-b_{e(\alpha)}x+a_{e(\alpha)}y=\frac{1}{|{e(\alpha)}|_{\mathbb{Z}}}\langle{e(\alpha)},(y,-x)\rangle.

Since
N
is convex, the residues have exactly two sign changes, and therefore, Lemma
2.7
accounts for all of its zeroes. In particular, there are no zeroes in any interval of

A_{0}\setminus\{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}:-b_{e(\alpha)}x+a_{e(\alpha)}y\neq 0\}

(6.5)

at whose endpoints the residues of
xd\log z+yd\log w
have opposite signs. It remains only to exclude zeroes at angles
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
where the residue vanishes. Such an angle satisfies

-b_{e(\alpha)}x+a_{e(\alpha)}y=0,

so the edge
e(\alpha)
is parallel to
(x,y)
. By convexity of
N
,
\alpha
lies in an interval of (
6.5
) at whose endpoints the residues have opposite sign, and these intervals contain no zeroes as shown above.

∎

Given a local coordinate
\zeta
on
\Sigma^{\circ}_{+}
, write

d\log z=f_{1}(\zeta)d\zeta,\qquad d\log w=f_{2}(\zeta)d\zeta,\qquad\omega_{\bm{D}_{c}}=f_{3}(\zeta)d\zeta.

(6.6)

Proposition 6.11
.

The map
\Omega:\mathcal{L}\rightarrow\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
is a diffeomorphism with inverse

\Omega^{-1}:\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}
given, for
\zeta\in\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
, by

x=\frac{\Im(\overline{f_{2}}f_{3})}{\Im(\overline{f_{1}}f_{2})},\qquad y=\frac{\Im(f_{1}\overline{f_{3}})}{\Im(\overline{f_{1}}f_{2})},

(6.7)

where all functions are evaluated at
\zeta
.

Proof.

Let
\zeta\in\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
. The vanishing of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
at
\zeta
is equivalent to the system of linear equations

\begin{bmatrix}\Re f_{1}(\zeta)&\Re f_{2}(\zeta)\\
\Im f_{1}(\zeta)&\Im f_{2}(\zeta)\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
y\end{bmatrix}=-\begin{bmatrix}\Re f_{3}(\zeta)\\
\Im f_{3}(\zeta)\end{bmatrix}.

(6.8)

The system has a unique solution
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
provided the determinant

\det\begin{bmatrix}\Re f_{1}(\zeta)&\Re f_{2}(\zeta)\\
\Im f_{1}(\zeta)&\Im f_{2}(\zeta)\end{bmatrix}=\Im(\overline{f_{1}(\zeta)}f_{2}(\zeta))\neq 0.

(6.9)

If this determinant vanishes, then

r:=\frac{f_{1}(\zeta)}{f_{2}(\zeta)}\in\mathbb{R}.

By Lemma
6.10
, the
1
-form

d\log z-rd\log w

cannot vanish at
\zeta
, a contradiction.

Thus, the determinant is nonzero, so for every
\zeta\in\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}

the system (
6.8
) has a unique solution
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, which is given by (
6.7
).

We now show that
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
, and therefore,
(x,y)\in\mathcal{L}
. If
(x,y)\notin\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
, then there are only two sign changes in the residues of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
and so Lemma
6.5
accounts for all the zeroes which must therefore all be in
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
, contradicting that
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has a

\sigma
-conjugate pair of zeroes. Thus,
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
.

Hence, we obtain a map

\Omega^{-1}:\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{L}

sending
\zeta
to the unique solution (
6.7
). By construction,
\Omega^{-1}
is a right inverse to
\Omega
. Moreover, uniqueness of the solution to (
6.8
) implies that
\Omega
is injective, so
\Omega
is a bijection with inverse
\Omega^{-1}
.

Let

F(x,y;\zeta):=\frac{\lambda_{c}(x,y)}{d\zeta}=xf_{1}(\zeta)+yf_{2}(\zeta)+f_{3}(\zeta).

(6.10)

Let
\zeta=u+\mathrm{i}v
and let

G(x,y;u,v):=\begin{bmatrix}\Re F(x,y;u+\mathrm{i}v)\\
\Im F(x,y;u+\mathrm{i}v)\end{bmatrix}.

Implicitly differentiating
G(x,y;\Omega(x,y))=0
, we obtain

\mathscr{J}_{(x,y)}G+\mathscr{J}_{(u,v)}G\mathscr{J}\Omega=0,

where
\mathscr{J}
denotes the Jacobian matrix. Hence,

\mathscr{J}\Omega=-(\mathscr{J}_{(u,v)}G)^{-1}\mathscr{J}_{(x,y)}G,

and, therefore,

\det\mathscr{J}\Omega=\frac{\det\mathscr{J}_{(x,y)}G}{\det\mathscr{J}_{(u,v)}G}.

The numerator is (
6.9
) and the denominator is

\left|\frac{dF(x,y;\zeta)}{d\zeta}\right|^{2}

since
F
is holomorphic in
\zeta
. Therefore,

\det\mathscr{J}\Omega=\frac{\Im(\overline{f_{1}}f_{2})}{|xf_{1}^{\prime}+yf_{2}^{\prime}+f_{3}^{\prime}|^{2}}.

(6.11)

We have already seen that
\Im(\overline{f_{1}}f_{2})\neq 0
, and the denominator is nonzero because
\zeta
is a simple zero of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
. Therefore,
\Omega
is a local diffeomorphism. Since it is a bijection, it is a diffeomorphism.
∎

6.4
Boundary parametrization away from angles

For
\zeta_{0}\in\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}
, choose a local coordinate
\zeta=u+\mathrm{i}v
centered at
\zeta_{0}
such that
\sigma(\zeta)=\overline{\zeta}
and such that
\Sigma_{+}
is locally given by
v\geq 0
. By Lemma
2.6
, the
1
-forms in (
6.6
) are
\sigma
-real, so
f_{j}(\bar{\zeta})=\overline{f_{j}(\zeta)},
and hence,

f_{j}(u)\in\mathbb{R}

for
v=0
.

Proposition 6.12
.

The map

\Omega:\mathscr{A}^{\circ}\to\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}

is a homeomorphism with inverse

\Omega^{-1}:\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}\to\mathscr{A}^{\circ}

given, for
\zeta=u\in\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}
, by

x=\frac{f_{2}f_{3}^{\prime}-f_{2}^{\prime}f_{3}}{f_{1}f_{2}^{\prime}-f_{1}^{\prime}f_{2}},\qquad y=\frac{f_{1}^{\prime}f_{3}-f_{1}f_{3}^{\prime}}{f_{1}f_{2}^{\prime}-f_{1}^{\prime}f_{2}},

(6.12)

where all functions are evaluated at
u
.

Proof.

The condition
\Omega(x,y)=u
means that
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
vanishes at
u
to order at least two, which is equivalent to the linear system

\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}(u)&f_{2}(u)\\
f_{1}^{\prime}(u)&f_{2}^{\prime}(u)\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
y\end{bmatrix}=-\begin{bmatrix}f_{3}(u)\\
f_{3}^{\prime}(u)\end{bmatrix}.

(6.13)

We claim that the matrix is invertible. Indeed, if its determinant
were zero, there would exist a nonzero vector
(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that

\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}(u)&f_{2}(u)\\
f_{1}^{\prime}(u)&f_{2}^{\prime}(u)\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}a\\
b\end{bmatrix}=0.

Equivalently, the
1
-form

ad\log z+bd\log w

would vanish at
u
to order at least two, contradicting
Lemma
6.10
. Thus, the matrix in
(
6.13
) is invertible, and the system has a unique solution which is given by (
6.12
).

If
(x(u),y(u))\notin\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
, then there are only two sign changes in the residues of
\lambda_{c}(x(u),y(u))
, and so Lemma
6.5
accounts for all the zeroes. Hence:

•

if
u\in A_{0}
, then
u
must be a simple zero, contradicting that it has order at least two.

•

if
u\in A_{j}
and is a zero of order two, then since there are no other zeroes in
A_{j}
,
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
would have constant sign on
A_{j}
, forcing

\int_{A_{j}}\lambda_{c}(x,y)\neq 0,

contradicting imaginary-normalization.

Thus,
(x(u),y(u))\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
, and hence,
(x(u),y(u))\in\mathscr{A}^{\circ}
.

Hence, we obtain a map

\Omega^{-1}:\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}^{\circ}

sending
u
to this unique solution. By construction,
\Omega^{-1}
is a right inverse to
\Omega
. Moreover, uniqueness of the solution to (
6.13
) implies that
\Omega
is injective. Therefore,
\Omega
is a homeomorphism with inverse
\Omega^{-1}
.
∎

6.5
Turning points

Definition 6.13
.

We say that
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}
is a
turning point corresponding to
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
if
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has a zero at
\alpha
. We denote by
\mathscr{A}_{\bm{\alpha}}
the set of turning points.

For
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
, choose a local coordinate
\zeta
centered at
\alpha
such that
\sigma(\zeta)=\overline{\zeta}
, and
write

f_{j}(\zeta)=\frac{r_{j}}{\zeta}+s_{j}+O(|\zeta|),\qquad r_{j},s_{j}\in\mathbb{R},\qquad j=1,2,3,

where, by definitions (
2.10
) of
z
and
w
and (
6.1
) of
\bm{D}_{c}
,

r_{1}=-b_{e(\alpha)},\qquad r_{2}=a_{e(\alpha)},\qquad r_{3}=c({e(\alpha)}).

(6.14)

We then define

\Omega^{-1}:\bm{\alpha}\to\mathscr{A}_{\bm{\alpha}}

by sending
\alpha
to

x=\frac{r_{2}s_{3}-s_{2}r_{3}}{r_{1}s_{2}-s_{1}r_{2}},\qquad y=\frac{s_{1}r_{3}-r_{1}s_{3}}{r_{1}s_{2}-s_{1}r_{2}}.

(6.15)

Proposition 6.14
.

For each
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
, the point
(x,y)=\Omega^{-1}(\alpha)
is the unique turning point corresponding to
\alpha
, and it lies on
\bm{l}_{e}
.

Proof.

The
1
-form
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has a zero at
\alpha
if and only if both the pole
term and the constant term vanish. Equivalently,
(x,y)
satisfies

\begin{bmatrix}r_{1}&r_{2}\\
s_{1}&s_{2}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
y\end{bmatrix}=-\begin{bmatrix}r_{3}\\
s_{3}\end{bmatrix}.

(6.16)

Solving (
6.16
) gives (
6.15
), provided
the matrix is invertible.

We claim that the matrix in (
6.16
) is invertible.
Indeed, if its determinant were zero, the
1
-form

a_{e(\alpha)}d\log z+b_{e(\alpha)}d\log w

would have a zero at
\alpha
, contradicting Lemma
6.10
. Therefore, (
6.16
) has a unique solution.

The point
(x,y)
is contained in
\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}
since
\res_{\alpha}\bigl(\lambda_{c}(x,y)\bigr)=-b_{e(\alpha)}x+a_{e(\alpha)}y+c({e(\alpha)})=0,
and this is exactly the linear function defining
\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}
.

Finally, we show that
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}
. Suppose that
(x,y)\notin\mathcal{D}_{c}
, and let
R
be the cell containing
(x,y)
. Since

(x,y)\in\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}
, we have
\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e(\alpha))=0
. Let

I\subset\partial N
be the maximal closed interval containing
e(\alpha)

on which
\operatorname{sign}_{R}=0
, and let
e_{-}
and
e_{+}
be the nearest edges on the
two sides of
I
such that

\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e_{-})\neq 0,\qquad\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e_{+})\neq 0.

Since
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
, Lemma
5.3

implies that
\operatorname{sign}_{R}
has exactly two sign changes. Crossing the line

\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}
through
(x,y)
changes the sign at
e(\alpha)
and leaves all other signs unchanged. The two adjacent cells are still contained
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
, and hence their sign functions also
have exactly two sign changes. Therefore, the number of sign changes cannot
change under this crossing, which is possible only if

\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e_{-})=-\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e_{+}).

Thus,
\operatorname{sign}_{R}
has a sign change along the closed interval
I
.

By Lemma
6.5
, all zeroes of

\lambda_{c}(x,y)
are accounted for. Moreover, the connected component of

A_{0}\setminus\{\text{poles of }\lambda_{c}(x,y)\}

corresponding to
I
has endpoints with opposite residues, and therefore
contains no zeroes of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
. In particular,

\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has no zero at
\alpha
, contradicting the definition of

(x,y)
. Hence
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}
.
∎

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{e}
, and let
(x,y)=\Omega^{-1}(\alpha)\in\mathscr{A}_{\bm{\alpha}}

be the turning point corresponding to
\alpha
. Then:

1.

If
(x,y)\in\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}\cap\partial\mathcal{D}_{c}
, then
\alpha
is a simple zero of

\lambda_{c}(x,y)
.

2.

If
(x,y)\in\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}\cap\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
, then
\alpha
is a zero of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
of order at
most two.

Proof.

If
(x,y)\in\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}\cap\partial\mathcal{D}_{c}
, then
(x,y)
lies on the boundary of
\mathcal{D}_{c}
. By Lemma
6.5
,
\alpha
is simple.

Now suppose
(x,y)\in\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}\cap\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
and let
R
be the cell containing
(x,y)
. Let
e_{-}
(resp.
e_{+}
) denote the edges of
N

immediately clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) of
e
. Then

\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e)=0,\qquad\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e_{-})\neq\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e_{+}).

By Lemma
6.5
, no zero in the connected component of

A_{0}\setminus\{\text{poles of }\lambda_{c}(x,y)\}

containing
\alpha
is forced, so there are either none or two zeroes in that component. In particular,
\alpha
is a zero of order at most two.
∎

Definition 6.16
.

For
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
, we call the turning point
(x,y)=\Omega^{-1}(\alpha)\in\mathscr{A}_{\bm{\alpha}}
a
turning-point cusp
if
\alpha
is a zero of order two of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
.

6.6
Global extension of
\Omega^{-1}
and the arctic curve

Let

\overline{\mathcal{L}}

denote the closure of the rough region
\mathcal{L}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and let

\partial\mathcal{L}:=\overline{\mathcal{L}}\setminus\mathcal{L}

denote the boundary of
\mathcal{L}
.

Proposition 6.17
.

The maps

\Omega^{-1}:\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{L},\qquad\Omega^{-1}:\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}\to\mathscr{A}^{\circ},\qquad\Omega^{-1}:\bm{\alpha}\to\mathscr{A}_{\bm{\alpha}}

glue together to a smooth map

\Omega^{-1}:\Sigma_{+}\to\overline{\mathcal{L}}.

Moreover,

\Omega^{-1}\bigl(\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\bigr)=\partial\mathcal{L}=\mathscr{A}.

Proof.

First let
\zeta_{0}\in\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}
. As in Section
6.4
, choose a local coordinate
\zeta=u+\mathrm{i}v
centered at
\zeta_{0}
such that
\sigma(\zeta)=\overline{\zeta}
and such that
\Sigma_{+}
is locally given by
v\geq 0
. Since
f_{j}(u+\mathrm{i}v)\in\mathbb{R}
when
v=0
, the functions

\Im(\overline{f_{j}(\zeta)}f_{k}(\zeta))

vanish on
v=0
. By Taylor’s theorem, there exist smooth functions

B_{jk}(u,v)
such that locally

\Im(\overline{f_{j}(\zeta)}f_{k}(\zeta))=vB_{jk}(u,v).

Hence, for
v>0
, (
6.7
) becomes

x=\frac{B_{23}}{B_{12}},\qquad y=\frac{B_{31}}{B_{12}}.

Locally expanding
f_{j}(\zeta)
and
f_{k}(\zeta)
, we obtain

B_{jk}(u,v)=f_{j}(\zeta_{0})f_{k}^{\prime}(\zeta_{0})-f_{j}^{\prime}(\zeta_{0})f_{k}(\zeta_{0})+O(|\zeta|).

(6.17)

Thus,

B_{12}(0,0)=f_{1}(\zeta_{0})f_{2}^{\prime}(\zeta_{0})-f_{1}^{\prime}(\zeta_{0})f_{2}(\zeta_{0})\neq 0

by Proposition
6.12
, so the above
formulas define a smooth extension of
\Omega^{-1}
across
\zeta_{0}
, and its
value at
\zeta_{0}
is exactly (
6.12
).

Now let
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
, and choose a local coordinate
\zeta=u+\mathrm{i}v
centered
at
\alpha
such that
\sigma(\zeta)=\bar{\zeta}
. Write

f_{j}(\zeta)=\frac{r_{j}}{\zeta}+s_{j}+O(|\zeta|),\qquad r_{j},s_{j}\in\mathbb{R},

and let

h_{j}(\zeta):=\zeta f_{j}(\zeta)=r_{j}+s_{j}\zeta+O(|\zeta|^{2}).

Then each
h_{j}
is holomorphic and satisfies

h_{j}(\bar{\zeta})=\overline{h_{j}(\zeta)},

so
h_{j}(u)\in\mathbb{R}
for
v=0
. Since
|\zeta|^{2}
is real, the formula (
6.7
) can be rewritten for
v>0
as

x=\frac{\Im(\overline{h_{2}}h_{3})}{\Im(\overline{h_{1}}h_{2})},\qquad y=\frac{\Im(h_{1}\overline{h_{3}})}{\Im(\overline{h_{1}}h_{2})}.

Again by Taylor’s theorem, locally

\Im(\overline{h_{j}(\zeta)}h_{k}(\zeta))=v\widetilde{B}_{jk}(u,v)

for smooth
\widetilde{B}_{jk}
. Hence,

x=\frac{\widetilde{B}_{23}}{\widetilde{B}_{12}},\qquad y=\frac{\widetilde{B}_{31}}{\widetilde{B}_{12}}.

At
(u,v)=(0,0)
,

\widetilde{B}_{12}(0,0)=r_{1}s_{2}-s_{1}r_{2}\neq 0

by Proposition
6.14
. Therefore, these formulas extend
smoothly across
\alpha
, and their value at
\alpha
is exactly given by (
6.15
). We have therefore obtained a smooth map

\Omega^{-1}:\Sigma_{+}\to\overline{\mathcal{L}}

whose restrictions to
\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
,
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}
, and

\bm{\alpha}
are the three maps above.

Since
\Omega^{-1}(\Sigma(\mathbb{R}))\subset\mathscr{A}^{\circ}\sqcup\mathscr{A}_{\bm{\alpha}}
,
it is disjoint from
\mathcal{L}
, and hence,

\Omega^{-1}(\Sigma(\mathbb{R}))\subset\partial\mathcal{L}.

Conversely, let
(x,y)\in\partial\mathcal{L}
, and choose
(x_{n},y_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
with

(x_{n},y_{n})\to(x,y)
. Set
\zeta_{n}:=\Omega(x_{n},y_{n})\in\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
.
By compactness of
\Sigma_{+}
, after passing to a subsequence, we may assume

\zeta_{n}\to\zeta\in\Sigma_{+}
. By continuity of
\Omega^{-1}
,
