(x,y)=\lim_{n\to\infty}(x_{n},y_{n})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\Omega^{-1}(\zeta_{n})=\Omega^{-1}(\zeta).

If
\zeta\in\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
, then
(x,y)=\Omega^{-1}(\zeta)\in\mathcal{L}
, a
contradiction. Hence,
\zeta\in\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
, and therefore

\partial\mathcal{L}\subset\Omega^{-1}(\Sigma(\mathbb{R})).

Thus,

\partial\mathcal{L}=\Omega^{-1}(\Sigma(\mathbb{R})).

Since

\Omega^{-1}(\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha})=\mathscr{A}^{\circ},\qquad\Omega^{-1}(\bm{\alpha})=\mathscr{A}_{\bm{\alpha}},

we conclude that

\partial\mathcal{L}=\mathscr{A}^{\circ}\sqcup\mathscr{A}_{\bm{\alpha}}=\mathscr{A}.

∎

6.7
Geometry of the arctic curve

The map

\gamma:\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{1},\qquad\zeta\mapsto\frac{d\log w}{d\log z}(\zeta)

is called the
logarithmic Gauss map
.

By Proposition
6.17
, the restriction

\Omega^{-1}\big|_{\Sigma(\mathbb{R})}:\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}^{2}

is a smooth parametrization of the arctic curve
\mathscr{A}
. We now identify its critical points and compute tangent lines to
\mathscr{A}
. We phrase the statement about tangent lines in terms of branches since we have not yet established injectivity of the parameterization; we will show this in Corollary
6.21
.

Proposition 6.18
.

Let
\zeta_{0}\in\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
. Then:

1.

The point
\zeta_{0}
is a critical point of

\Omega^{-1}\big|_{\Sigma(\mathbb{R})}
if and only if

\Omega^{-1}(\zeta_{0})
is one of the four types of cusps in Definition
6.7
and Definition
6.16
.

2.

Each of these cusps is an ordinary cusp in the geometric sense,
i.e.
after a smooth change of coordinates, it is locally given by
y^{2}=x^{3}
.

3.

The tangent line to the branch of
\mathscr{A}
at
\Omega^{-1}(\zeta_{0})

determined by
\zeta_{0}
has slope

-\frac{1}{\gamma(\zeta_{0})}=-\frac{f_{1}(\zeta_{0})}{f_{2}(\zeta_{0})}.

In particular, if
\zeta_{0}=\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
, then the tangent line is
\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}
.

Proof.

First, we consider the case
\zeta_{0}\in\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}
. Choose a local coordinate

\zeta=u+\mathrm{i}v
centered at
\zeta_{0}
such that

\sigma(\zeta)=\overline{\zeta}
. Write

(x(u),y(u)):=\Omega^{-1}(u),

and set

W:=f_{1}f_{2}^{\prime}-f_{1}^{\prime}f_{2},\qquad H:=xf_{1}^{\prime\prime}+yf_{2}^{\prime\prime}+f_{3}^{\prime\prime},

(6.18)

evaluated at
\zeta_{0}
. Since
\lambda_{c}(x(u),y(u))
has a zero of order at least two at
u
, we have

x(u)f_{1}(u)+y(u)f_{2}(u)+f_{3}(u)=0,\qquad x(u)f_{1}^{\prime}(u)+y(u)f_{2}^{\prime}(u)+f_{3}^{\prime}(u)=0.

Differentiating with respect to
u
gives

\displaystyle f_{1}x^{\prime}+f_{2}y^{\prime}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle f_{1}^{\prime}x^{\prime}+f_{2}^{\prime}y^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-H.

Solving this linear system yields

(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\frac{H}{W}(f_{2},-f_{1}).

(6.19)

By Proposition
6.12
, we have
W\neq 0
and

(f_{2},-f_{1})\neq 0
. Hence,
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=0
if and only if
H=0
,
i.e.
if and only if
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has a zero of order at least three at
\zeta_{0}
. This proves Item 1 in this case.

A standard criterion for an ordinary cusp (see, for example,

[
47
, Definition 5.3.9]
) is that

(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\det\begin{bmatrix}x^{\prime\prime}&y^{\prime\prime}\\
x^{\prime\prime\prime}&y^{\prime\prime\prime}\end{bmatrix}\neq 0;

Suppose
\Omega^{-1}(\zeta_{0})
is one of the cusp types from
Definition
6.7
. Since
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has a zero of exactly order three at
\zeta_{0}
,

H=0,\qquad xf_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}+yf_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}+f_{3}^{\prime\prime\prime}\neq 0.

Therefore,

(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\frac{H}{W}(f_{2},-f_{1})=0.

(6.20)

Differentiating (
6.19
), we get

\displaystyle(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{H}{W}\right)^{\prime}(f_{2},-f_{1})+\frac{H}{W}(f_{2}^{\prime},-f_{1}^{\prime}),

(6.21)

\displaystyle(x^{\prime\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{H}{W}\right)^{\prime\prime}(f_{2},-f_{1})+2\left(\frac{H}{W}\right)^{\prime}(f_{2}^{\prime},-f_{1}^{\prime})+\frac{H}{W}(f_{2}^{\prime\prime},-f_{1}^{\prime\prime}).

By (
6.20
),

H^{\prime}=x^{\prime}f_{1}^{\prime\prime}+y^{\prime}f_{2}^{\prime\prime}+xf_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}+yf_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}+f_{3}^{\prime\prime\prime}=xf_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}+yf_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}+f_{3}^{\prime\prime\prime}.

Using
H=0
and simplifying, we get

\det\begin{bmatrix}x^{\prime\prime}&y^{\prime\prime}\\
x^{\prime\prime\prime}&y^{\prime\prime\prime}\end{bmatrix}=\frac{2(xf_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}+yf_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}+f_{3}^{\prime\prime\prime})^{2}}{W}\neq 0,

so
\Omega^{-1}(\zeta_{0})
is an ordinary cusp in the geometric sense, proving Item 2 in this case.

If
\zeta_{0}
is not a critical point, then the tangent line to the branch of
\mathscr{A}
at
(x,y)
determined by
\zeta_{0}
has slope

\frac{y^{\prime}}{x^{\prime}}=-\frac{f_{1}}{f_{2}}=-\frac{1}{\gamma(\zeta_{0})}.

When
\zeta_{0}
is a critical point, the ordinary cusp
\Omega^{-1}(\zeta_{0})
still has a well-defined tangent line spanned by

(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime})=\frac{H^{\prime}}{W}(f_{2},-f_{1})

which gives the same slope
-{1}/{\gamma(\zeta_{0})}
, completing the proof of Item 3 in this case.

Now we consider the case
\zeta_{0}=\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
. Define

h_{j}(\zeta):=\zeta f_{j}(\zeta).

All items now follow exactly as in the previous case with
h_{j}
in place of
f_{j}
.

Finally, the tangent vector at
\Omega^{-1}(\alpha)
is

(h_{2},-h_{1})=(a_{e(\alpha)},b_{e(\alpha)}),

which is the vector along
\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}
. Since
\Omega^{-1}(\alpha)\in\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}
, the tangent line is
\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}
.
∎

Corollary 6.19
.

The map
\Omega:\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{L}
is orientation reversing.

Proof.

Choose
\zeta_{0}\in A_{0}\setminus\bm{\alpha}
and local coordinates

\zeta=u+\mathrm{i}v
with
v\geq 0
on
\Sigma_{+}
. Since
f_{j}
is real,
\Omega^{-1}(u+\mathrm{i}v)
given by (
6.7
) is an even function of
v
. Therefore, locally we have

(x(u+\mathrm{i}v),y(u+\mathrm{i}v))=\Omega^{-1}(u+\mathrm{i}v)=(x(u),y(u))+\frac{1}{2}v^{2}(\partial_{vv}x(u),\partial_{vv}y(u))+O(v^{4}).

Expanding

F(x(u+\mathrm{i}v),y(u+\mathrm{i}v);u+\mathrm{i}v)=0

in
v
, with
F(x,y;\zeta)
as in (
6.10
), and using the vanishing of the
v^{2}
-term, we obtain

(f_{1}(u),f_{2}(u))\cdot(\partial_{vv}x(u),\partial_{vv}y(u))=H\neq 0

where
H
is as in (
6.18
). In particular,

\partial_{v}\Omega^{-1}(u+\mathrm{i}v)=v(\partial_{vv}x(u),\partial_{vv}y(u))+O(v^{3})\neq 0.

Differentiating with respect to
u
and using (
6.19
), we also get

\partial_{u}\Omega^{-1}(u+\mathrm{i}v)=(\partial_{u}x(u),\partial_{u}y(u))+O(v^{2})=\frac{H}{W}(f_{2}(u),-f_{1}(u))+O(v^{2})\neq 0.

It follows that

\det(\partial_{u}\Omega^{-1}(u+\mathrm{i}v),\partial_{v}\Omega^{-1}(u+\mathrm{i}v))=v\frac{H^{2}}{W}+O(v^{3}).

In particular,
\Omega^{-1}
is orientation reversing if
W<0
.

We use the associated
amoeba
to show that
W<0
. Define

X(\zeta):=\Re\left(\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta}d\log z\right),\qquad Y(\zeta):=\Re\left(\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta}d\log w\right),

where
\zeta_{0}
is a fixed point on
A_{0}
. Since
d\log z
and
d\log w
are imaginary-normalized differentials,
X
and
Y
are well-defined single-valued functions on
\Sigma
. The amoeba is the image of the map
\Sigma\ni\zeta\mapsto(X(\zeta),Y(\zeta))\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Under this map,
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}
is mapped to the boundary of the amoeba, while
\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
is mapped to its interior. In the algebraic (periodic) setting, this agrees with the classical amoeba of the spectral curve; see
[
85
]
for a proper introduction.

The connected components of the complement of the amoeba are convex
[
85
, Proposition 2.7]
. This convexity implies that, along the boundary, the tangent vector turns to the right. Consequently, for
\zeta\in\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
, the vector
(-Y^{\prime}(\zeta),X^{\prime}(\zeta))
is an inward-pointing normal, and

(-Y^{\prime}(\zeta),X^{\prime}(\zeta))\cdot(X^{\prime\prime}(\zeta),Y^{\prime\prime}(\zeta))<0.

Locally we may write

X^{\prime}(\zeta)=f_{1}(\zeta),\qquad Y^{\prime}(\zeta)=f_{2}(\zeta),

so that

(-Y^{\prime}(\zeta),X^{\prime}(\zeta))\cdot(X^{\prime\prime}(\zeta),Y^{\prime\prime}(\zeta))=(-f_{2}(\zeta),f_{1}(\zeta))\cdot(f_{1}^{\prime}(\zeta),f_{2}^{\prime}(\zeta))=W.

This proves that
W<0
and, hence, that
\Omega^{-1}
is orientation reversing.
∎

Proposition 6.20
.

If
(x,y)=\Omega^{-1}(\zeta_{0})
is a smooth point of
\mathscr{A}
, then the curvature
\kappa(x,y)<0
. In particular,
\mathcal{L}
is locally convex at
(x,y)
.

Proof.

Let
\zeta_{0}\in\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\setminus\bm{\alpha}
. Choose a local coordinate

\zeta=u+\mathrm{i}v
centered at
\zeta_{0}
such that
\sigma(\zeta)=\bar{\zeta}
,
so that
\Sigma_{+}
is locally given by
v\geq 0
. Let
W
and
H
be as in (
6.18
). Using (
6.19
) and (
6.21
), we get that the curvature is

\kappa(x,y)=\frac{\det\begin{bmatrix}x^{\prime}&y^{\prime}\\
x^{\prime\prime}&y^{\prime\prime}\end{bmatrix}}{|(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})|^{3}}=\frac{|W|^{3}}{|H|(f_{1}^{2}+f_{2}^{2})^{3/2}}\frac{1}{W}.

Note that
H\neq 0
since
(x,y)
is assumed to be a smooth point and therefore, not a cusp. We saw in the proof of Corollary
6.19
that
W<0
, which proves that
\kappa(x,y)<0
.

Since
\Omega^{-1}
is orientation reversing, the boundary is traversed clockwise, and therefore, negative curvature means that
\mathscr{A}
bends towards the right,
i.e.
towards the rough region.

The argument for
\zeta_{0}=\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
is similar with
h_{j}(\zeta)=\zeta f_{j}(\zeta)
in place of
f_{j}(\zeta)
as in Proposition
6.18
.
∎

Corollary 6.21
.

The map

\Omega^{-1}:\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\to\mathscr{A}

is a bijection.

Proof.

Surjectivity follows from Proposition
6.17
. It remains to
show that
\Omega^{-1}
is injective.

By Proposition
6.18
, every point of
\mathscr{A}
is
locally either a smooth point of a branch of
\mathscr{A}
or an ordinary cusp. In particular,

\Omega^{-1}\big|_{\Sigma(\mathbb{R})}
is locally injective. Therefore, if

\Omega^{-1}
were not injective, then
\mathscr{A}
would have a
self-intersection.

We first claim that no self-intersection can occur at a point of
\partial\mathcal{D}_{c}
.
Indeed, there are no cusps on
\partial\mathcal{D}_{c}
, so such a point would have to be
a smooth point of two distinct branches of
\mathscr{A}
with a common tangent
line. Since
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
is locally convex by
Proposition
6.20
and the map
\Omega^{-1}\big|_{\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}}
is a diffeomorphism, the two branches would have to lie on opposite
sides of this common tangent line. This is impossible because
\partial\mathcal{D}_{c}
is
simple.

Therefore, any self-intersection
(x,y)
of
\mathscr{A}
would have to lie
in the interior
\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
. But then each branch through
(x,y)
imposes a
different condition on the location of the two remaining zeroes of

\lambda_{c}(x,y)
, which is impossible.

Thus,

\Omega^{-1}:\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\to\mathscr{A}

is globally injective, and therefore, a bijection.
∎

6.8
Zeroes of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
and tangent lines to the arctic curve

(A)

(B)

(C)

(D)

Figure 27
:
Local tangent configurations associated with the zeroes of

\lambda_{c}(x,y)
. (A)–(D) correspond respectively to the frozen region
at a convex vertex, the frozen region at a concave vertex, the quasi-frozen
region and the smooth region. In (B), the number of simple zeroes in
(\alpha,\beta)
is either two or three, depending on which side of the
dashed line
(x,y)
lies; here the frozen region is
\mathcal{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
. Orange points denote zeroes of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
under the identification
\Omega^{-1}\bigl|_{\Sigma(\mathbb{R})}
.

The zeroes of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
admit a simple geometric interpretation in terms of tangent lines to the arctic curve. This gives a convenient dictionary between the phase classification from Definition
6.7
and the geometry of
\mathscr{A}
, generalizing the picture of Kenyon and Okounkov
[
81
, Section 1.8]
; see also
[
19
, Proposition 15]
.

Proposition 6.22
.

Let
(x_{0},y_{0})\in\mathscr{A}
and let

\zeta_{0}:=\Omega(x_{0},y_{0}).

For
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the following are equivalent:

1.

(x,y)
lies on the tangent line to
\mathscr{A}
at
(x_{0},y_{0})
.

2.

\zeta_{0}
is a zero of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.18
,
(x,y)
lies on the tangent line through

(x_{0},y_{0})
if and only if

(x-x_{0})f_{1}(\zeta_{0})+(y-y_{0})f_{2}(\zeta_{0})=0.

(6.22)

Since
\zeta_{0}=\Omega(x_{0},y_{0})
, we have

x_{0}f_{1}(\zeta_{0})+y_{0}f_{2}(\zeta_{0})+f_{3}(\zeta_{0})=0.

Thus, (
6.22
) is equivalent to

xf_{1}(\zeta_{0})+yf_{2}(\zeta_{0})+f_{3}(\zeta_{0})=0,

i.e.
to
\zeta_{0}
being a zero of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
.
∎

Proposition
6.22
translates the conditions on the zeroes of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
in Definition
6.7
into statements about tangent lines to
\mathscr{A}
. The possible degenerations as we approach
\mathscr{A}
may be summarized as follows.

1.

As
(x,y)
approaches the arctic curve from outside, two real tangents merge into a double tangent. When it crosses into the rough region, this double tangent splits into a
\sigma
-conjugate pair.

2.

As
(x,y)
approaches a cusp of
\mathscr{A}
, three tangents merge into a triple tangent.

3.

As
(x,y)
approaches a turning point corresponding to
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
that is not a turning-point cusp, two tangents collide with the pole at
\alpha
and cancel, producing a simple zero.

4.

At a turning-point cusp, a triple tangent collides with the pole, producing a double zero.

7
Asymptotics: local statistics and the limit shape

We now turn to the asymptotic analysis of the exact inverse Kasteleyn formula (
4.3
). The argument follows the general steepest-descent framework developed in
[
11
]
; see also
[
16
]
, with the necessary modifications for our general setting.

7.1
Local statistics

Fix an astroidal domain
\mathcal{D}_{c}
and a sequence of AZ graphs

\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}},n=1,2,\dots,

such that

c_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}(e)=n|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}c(e)+O(1),\qquad e\in E(N),

see Sections
3.1
and
5.1
for definitions.
Let
\mathsf{C}\subset{\mathsf{G}}
be a finite subgraph, and let
(j_{n},k_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
satisfy

(j_{n},k_{n})=n(x,y)+O(1)

for some
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
. Set

\mathsf{C}_{n}:=(j_{n},k_{n})+\mathsf{C}.

Recall the phase regions
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
and
\mathcal{G}_{j}
from Definition
6.7
. We define
A_{(x,y)}
by

A_{(x,y)}:=\begin{cases}(\alpha,\beta),&\text{if $(x,y)$ lies in a frozen or quasi-frozen region corresponding to }(\alpha,\beta)\subset A_{0},\\
A_{j},&\text{if $(x,y)\in\mathcal{G}_{j}$},\end{cases}

where

\Sigma(\mathbb{R})=A_{0}\sqcup\left(\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{g}A_{j}\right)

as in Section
2.3
.

Definition 7.1
.

We say that a point
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
lies in a
phase region
if it belongs to a rough, smooth, frozen, or quasi-frozen region. For such a point
(x,y)
, let

\zeta_{*}=\zeta_{*}(x,y):=\begin{cases}\Omega(x,y),&\text{if $(x,y)\in\mathcal{L}$},\\
\text{any point of $A_{(x,y)}$},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(7.1)

Convergence of local correlations is captured by the following statement.

Theorem 7.2
.

Assume that the angle function is periodic. Let
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
lie in a phase region and let
\zeta_{*}
be as in Definition
7.1
. If
(x,y)\in\mathcal{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
, assume also that

(x,y)\notin\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}\cup\bm{l}_{e(\beta)}.

For a black vertex
\mathsf{b}
and a white vertex
\mathsf{w}
of
\mathsf{C}
, let

\mathsf{b}_{n}:=\mathsf{b}+(j_{n},k_{n}),\qquad\mathsf{w}_{n}:=\mathsf{w}+(j_{n},k_{n})

denote their translates in
\mathsf{C}_{n}
. Then

\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}\rightarrow\mathsf{A}^{\zeta_{*}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty,

uniformly over
\mathsf{b}
and
\mathsf{w}
.

Remark 7.3
.

An equivalent way of stating Theorem
7.2
without formulas is that the local statistics are described by the Gibbs measure of the corresponding slope; see Corollary
7.12
below for a precise statement.

Remark 7.4
.

If
(x,y)\in\mathcal{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}\cap(\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}\cup\bm{l}_{e(\beta)})
, then we expect that
(x,y)
lies either in the frozen region or on a frozen-frozen boundary. The analysis of these points requires additional technical arguments which we do not pursue here (see Remark
7.8
below); we expect that it does not lead to interesting new phenomena.

The rest of the section is dedicated to the proof of Theorem
7.2
. We begin by introducing notation of the relevant sign intervals. For
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
, let
I_{(x,y),+}
(resp.
I_{(x,y),-}
) denote the union of the two
+
(resp.
-
) sign intervals for
\operatorname{sign}_{R}
where
R
is the cell containing
(x,y)
as in Section
6.1
.
Let
A_{R,+}\subset A_{0}
(resp.
A_{R,-}\subset A_{0}
) be the union of the two smallest intervals in
A_{0}
containing all angles
\bm{\alpha}_{e}
for all
e\in I_{(x,y),+}
(resp.
e\in I_{(x,y),-}
) and any
(x,y)\in R
. Note that if
(x,y)\in\bm{l}_{e}
for some edge
e
, then some signs vanish, and hence,
I_{(x,y),+}\cup I_{(x,y),-}\neq\partial N
.

Similarly, for
\mathsf{x}\in B(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})\sqcup W(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}})
, let
I_{{\mathsf{x}},+}
(resp.
I_{\mathsf{x},-}
) denote the union of the two
+
(resp.
-
) sign intervals for
\operatorname{sign}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{x}}}
as in Section
3.6
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
R\subset\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
be a cell, and let

\mathsf{x}_{n}=\mathsf{x}+(j_{n},k_{n})\in B(\mathsf{C}_{n})\sqcup W(\mathsf{C}_{n}),\qquad\mathsf{x}\in B(\mathsf{C})\sqcup W(\mathsf{C}),

be a sequence such that

(j_{n},k_{n})=n(x,y)+O(1)
for some
(x,y)\in R
. Then, for any
e\in E(N)
such that
\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e)\neq 0
, we have

\operatorname{sign}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{x}_{n}}}(e)=\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e)
for all sufficiently large
n
. In particular,

I_{(x,y),+}\subseteq I_{{\mathsf{x}_{n}},+},\qquad I_{(x,y),-}\subseteq I_{{\mathsf{x}_{n}},-}

for all sufficiently large
n
.

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{C}
is a fixed finite subgraph,

(\bm{d}(\mathsf{x})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{0}))_{\alpha}=O(1),\quad\text{as }n\to\infty,

for every
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
. Hence,

\bigl(-\bm{d}(\mathsf{x}_{n})-\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}\bigr)_{\alpha}=\bigl(-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{0}+(j_{n},k_{n}))-\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}\bigr)_{\alpha}-(\bm{d}(\mathsf{x})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{0}))_{\alpha}.

Dividing by
n
and using Lemma
6.3
, we obtain

\frac{1}{n}\bigl(-\bm{d}(\mathsf{x}_{n})-\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}\bigr)_{\alpha}=-b_{e}x+a_{e}y+c(e)+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\qquad\text{for all }\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{e}.

Therefore, recalling Lemma
6.4
, for all
e\in E(N)
such that

{\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e)=\operatorname{sign}\bigr(-b_{e}x+a_{e}y+c(e)\bigl)\neq 0}

and all sufficiently large
n
,

\operatorname{sign}\bigl((-\bm{d}(\mathsf{x}_{n})-\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})_{\alpha}\bigr)=\operatorname{sign}\bigl(-b_{e}x+a_{e}y+c(e)\bigr)=\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e)\qquad\text{for all }\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{e}.

As in the proof of Lemma
2.12
, this implies that
\mathsf{x}_{n}
lies in one of the strips on the corresponding side of
\beta_{e}
, and hence,

\operatorname{sign}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{x}_{n}}}(e)=\operatorname{sign}_{R}(e)

for all sufficiently large
n
. The inclusions

I_{(x,y),+}\subseteq I_{\mathsf{x}_{n},+},\qquad I_{(x,y),-}\subseteq I_{\mathsf{x}_{n},-}

follow immediately.
∎

Fix
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
. We call a curve
\gamma\subset\Sigma
a
steepest descent
(resp.
ascent
)
curve
if:

•

it passes through a zero
\zeta_{0}
of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
,

•

for
\zeta\in\gamma
, we have

\Im\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta}\lambda_{c}(x,y)=0,

where the integral is taken along
\gamma
,

•

the function

\gamma\ni\zeta\mapsto\Re\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta}\lambda_{c}(x,y)

is locally decreasing (resp. locally increasing) as
\zeta
leaves
\zeta_{0}
along
\gamma
.

By Lemma
2.6
a steepest descent/ascent curve is invariant (up to orientation) under the involution
\sigma
. We therefore first describe the portion of the curve lying in
\Sigma_{+}
and postpone specifying its orientation until Lemma
7.7
below.

We say that
\zeta_{0}\in\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
is a
local minimum
(resp.
maximum
) if
\zeta_{0}
is a simple zero of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
and a local minimum (resp. maximum) of the function

\Sigma(\mathbb{R})\ni\zeta\mapsto\Re\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta}\lambda_{c}(x,y).

Such a point is a saddle for
\zeta\mapsto\Re\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta}\lambda_{c}(x,y)
: along the curve going perpendicular to
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
, it is a local maximum (resp. minimum). Our convention is that the term local minimum (resp. maximum) refers to the behavior along
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
(x,y)\in R\subset\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
and assume that
(x,y)
lies in a phase region.

1.

If
(x,y)
is in the frozen, quasi-frozen, or smooth region, and
\zeta_{0}\in A_{(x,y)}
is a local minimum (resp. maximum), there exists a simple curve
C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{desc}}^{+}\subset\Sigma_{+}
(resp.
C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{asc}}^{+}\subset\Sigma_{+}
) going between
\zeta_{0}
and
A_{R,+}
(resp.
A_{R,-}
), such that
\Im\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta}\lambda_{c}(x,y)=0
and
\Re\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta}\lambda_{c}(x,y)
is decreasing (resp. increasing) along the curve.

2.

If
(x,y)\in\mathcal{L}
and
\zeta_{0}=\Omega(x,y)\in\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
, there exists a simple curve
C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{desc}}^{+}\subset\Sigma_{+}
(resp.
C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{asc}}^{+}\subset\Sigma_{+}
) with both endpoints in
A_{R,+}
(resp.
A_{R,-}
), passing through
\zeta_{0}
, such that
\Im\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta}\lambda_{c}(x,y)=0
and
\Re\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta}\lambda_{c}(x,y)
is decreasing (resp. increasing) along the curve as we move away from
\zeta_{0}
.

Moreover, if
\zeta_{0}\in A_{(x,y)}
is a local minimum and
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\in A_{(x,y)}
is a local maximum, then

C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{desc}}^{+}\cap C_{\zeta_{0}^{\prime},\mathrm{asc}}^{+}=\varnothing.

If instead
(x,y)\in\mathcal{L}
and
\zeta_{0}=\Omega(x,y)\in\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
, then

C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{desc}}^{+}\cap C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{asc}}^{+}=\{\zeta_{0}\}.

See Figure
28
below for examples of the curves.

Proof.

We first consider the case
\zeta_{0}\in\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
, that is,
(x,y)
is in the frozen, quasi-frozen, or smooth region. Since
(x,y)\not\in\mathscr{A}
, the zero
\zeta_{0}
of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
is simple. Hence, there is one level line of

\Im\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta}\lambda_{c}(x,y)=0

along
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
and one level line entering
\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
. If
\zeta_{0}
is a local minimum (resp. maximum), the function

\zeta\mapsto\Re\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta}\lambda_{c}(x,y)

(7.2)

is decreasing (resp. increasing) along the level line entering
\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
. Following that level line, we eventually reach either another zero of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
which has to be in
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
, since
(x,y)\notin\mathcal{L}
, and is a local maximum (resp. minimum) (recall that the maximum (resp. minimum) refers to the behavior along
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
)
or a point where (
7.2
) tends to
-\infty
(resp.
+\infty
). In the latter case, the endpoint must be an angle
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}_{e}
with
e\in I_{R,+}
(resp.
e\in I_{R,-}
), which means that
\alpha\in A_{R,+}
(resp.
\alpha\in A_{R,-}
).

We define the curve
C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{desc}}^{+}\subset\Sigma_{+}
(resp.
C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{asc}}^{+}\subset\Sigma_{+}
) to be this level line, extended if necessary until it hits
A_{R,+}
(resp.
A_{R,-}
); see Figure
28
(B)–(D) for an illustration. If it first hits another zero
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}
of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
, then either:

1.

\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\in A_{R,+}
(resp.
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\in A_{R,-}
), and we are done.

2.

\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\in A_{(x,y)}
, which is impossible. Indeed,
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}
would be a local maximum (resp. minimum) and since the types of extrema alternate along
A_{(x,y)}
,
\zeta_{0}
and
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}
would be joined by a steepest ascent (resp. descent) curve along
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
with no zero between them. This is a contradiction, since they are also joined by a steepest descent (resp. ascent) curve in
\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
.

3.

\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\in A_{j}\neq A_{(x,y)}
, for some
j=1,\dots,g
, in which case we continue the curve along
A_{j}
in either direction until it hits the other zero of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
in
A_{j}
which is a local minimum (resp. maximum), and then continue again into
\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
.

Since a curve constructed by this procedure cannot return to the same
A_{j}
twice, it eventually has to end up at an angle
\alpha\in A_{R,+}
(resp.
\alpha\in A_{R,-}
) or a local maximum (resp. minimum) which lies in
A_{R,+}
(resp.
A_{R,-}
).

The resulting curve is simple because the function (
7.2
) is monotone along it (recall that this function is well-defined since
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
is imaginary-normalized).
If
\zeta_{0}\in A_{(x,y)}
is a local minimum and
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\in A_{(x,y)}
is a local maximum, there cannot be another local minimum or maximum in
A_{(x,y)}
between
\zeta_{0}
and
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}
. Thus, the same monotonicity argument shows that

C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{desc}}^{+}\cap C_{\zeta_{0}^{\prime},\mathrm{asc}}^{+}=\varnothing.

Now we consider the case
\zeta_{0}\in\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
,
i.e.

(x,y)\in\mathcal{L}
. The local level set
\Im\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta}\lambda_{c}(x,y)=0
consists of two curves going through
\zeta_{0}
orthogonally. One of those curves is used to define
C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{desc}}^{+}\subset\Sigma_{+}
and the other is used to define
C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{asc}}^{+}\subset\Sigma_{+}
. To construct
C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{desc}}^{+}\subset\Sigma_{+}
(resp.
C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{asc}}^{+}\subset\Sigma_{+}
), we follow the level line for which
\zeta\mapsto\Re\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta}\lambda_{c}(x,y)
is decreasing (resp. increasing); see Figure
28
(A) for an illustration. The same argument as in the first case now proves the statement for this second case.
∎

Steepest descent (blue) and steepest ascent (orange) curves in
\Sigma_{+}
for a genus-

A_{0}
, while the inner circles are
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
. Green points indicate zeros of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
. (A)–(D) correspond respectively to
(x,y)
lying in the rough region, frozen region
\mathcal{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
such that
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has two simple zeroes in
(\alpha,\beta)
, quasi-frozen region (when
e(\alpha)=e(\beta)
) or frozen region
\mathcal{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
such that
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
has three simple zeroes in
(\alpha,\beta)
(when
e(\alpha)\neq e(\beta)
), and smooth region. In keeping with our sign/color convention, the black and white intervals in
A_{0}
correspond to
A_{R,+}
and
A_{R,-}
respectively.

We now extend the curves defined in Lemma
7.6
to
\Sigma
. Define
C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{desc}}\subset\Sigma
(resp.
C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{asc}}\subset\Sigma
) to be the
\sigma
-invariant extension of
C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{desc}}^{+}\subset\Sigma_{+}
(resp.
C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{asc}}^{+}\subset\Sigma_{+}
) such that

\sigma_{*}(C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{desc}})=-C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{desc}}\qquad(\text{resp. }\sigma_{*}(C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{asc}})=-C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{asc}}).

Note that we have not specified the orientation of the curves yet, however, we assume they are given some orientation. Any segment of the curve along
\Sigma(\mathbb{R})
is traversed in both directions, so its contribution to the integral cancels.
By Lemma
7.6
, the curve
C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{desc}}
(resp.
C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{asc}}
) is a curve of steepest descent (resp. ascent) and intersects
A_{0}
in at most two points.

We then define
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}
and
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}
by

C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}:=\begin{cases}C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{desc}},&\text{if }\zeta_{0}=\Omega(x,y)\in\Sigma_{+}^{\circ},\\
\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\zeta_{0}\text{ local minimum in $A_{(x,y)}$}}C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{desc}},&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

and

C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}:=\begin{cases}C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{asc}},&\text{if }\zeta_{0}=\Omega(x,y)\in\Sigma_{+}^{\circ},\\
\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\zeta_{0}\text{ local maximum in $A_{(x,y)}$}}C_{\zeta_{0},\mathrm{asc}},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

\beta
\alpha

Figure 29
:

On the left-hand side, the union of the two black (resp. white) intervals in the outer circle corresponds to the angles in
I_{\mathsf{b}_{n},+}
(resp.
I_{\mathsf{b}_{n},-}
), while union of the two black (resp. white) intervals in
A_{0}
corresponds to
A_{R,+}
(resp.
A_{R,-}
) as in Figure
28
. The steepest descent curve (blue) in Figure
28
(C) can be deformed to the curve
C(I_{\mathsf{b}_{n}})
(green) without crossing any angles in
I_{\mathsf{b}_{n},-}
. Similarly, the right-hand side shows that the steepest ascent curve (orange) can be deformed to
C(I_{\mathsf{w}_{n}})
(green) without crossing any angles in
I_{\mathsf{w}_{n},+}
(note that
\alpha
and
\beta
are consecutive angles).

The following lemma shows that the contours appearing in the inverse Kasteleyn formula (
4.3
) may be deformed to the steepest descent and steepest ascent curves respectively without crossing the poles of the integrand as specified in the following claim (see Figure
29
).

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
lie in a phase region, and let
\zeta_{0}=\zeta_{*}(x,y)
. If
(x,y)\in\mathcal{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
, where
(\alpha,\beta)\subset A_{0}
is the interval between consecutive angles
\alpha,\beta
, assume that
(x,y)\notin\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}\cup\bm{l}_{e(\beta)}
. Then we may choose orientations of
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}
and
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}
such that, for all sufficiently large
n
and all

\mathsf{b}_{n}\in B(\mathsf{C}_{n}),\mathsf{w}_{n}\in W(\mathsf{C}_{n}),

the curve
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}
is homologous to
C(I_{\mathsf{b}_{n}})
in

\Sigma\setminus\bigcup_{e\in I_{\mathsf{b}_{n},-}}\bm{\alpha}_{e},

and the curve
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}
is homologous to
C(I_{\mathsf{w}_{n}})
in

\Sigma\setminus\bigcup_{e\in I_{\mathsf{w}_{n},+}}\bm{\alpha}_{e}.

Proof.

We distinguish cases according to the location of
(x,y)
.

1.

Assume first that
(x,y)\in\mathcal{G}_{j}
for some
j=1,\dots,g
, so that
A_{(x,y)}=A_{j}
. The curves
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}
and
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}
divide
\Sigma
into four parts. The four zeros of
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
on
A_{j}
alternate between local minima and local maxima. By Lemma
7.6
,
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}
and
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}
do not intersect, and so the same lemma implies that
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}
intersects
A_{0}
once in each component of
A_{R,+}
, and
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}
intersects
A_{0}
once in each component of
A_{R,-}
.

2.

If
(x,y)\in\mathcal{L}
, so that
\Omega(x,y)\in\Sigma_{+}^{\circ}
, a similar argument shows that
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}
intersects
A_{0}
once in each component of
A_{R,+}
, and
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}
intersects
A_{0}
once in each component of
A_{R,-}
.

3.

Finally, if
(x,y)\in\mathcal{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
or
(x,y)\in\mathcal{Q}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
for some
(\alpha,\beta)\subset A_{0}
, there are two possibilities:

(a)

If
A_{(x,y)}\subset A_{R,+}
(resp.
A_{(x,y)}\subset A_{R,-}
), then there is one local minimum (resp. local maximum) and two local maxima (resp. local minima) in
A_{(x,y)}
, and the types of extrema alternate along
A_{(x,y)}
. By the same argument as above, the curve
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}
(resp.
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}
) goes between
A_{(x,y)}
and the opposite component of
A_{R,+}
(resp.
A_{R,-}
), and
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}
(resp.
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}
) consists of two components, going between the two components of
A_{R,-}
(resp.
A_{R,+}
) and
A_{(x,y)}
.

(b)

If
A_{(x,y)}
is contained in neither
A_{R,+}
nor
A_{R,-}
, then there is one local minimum and one local maximum in
A_{(x,y)}
. By the same argument as above,
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}
(resp.
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}
) joins
A_{(x,y)}
and the opposite component of
A_{R,+}
(resp.
A_{R,-}
).

In each case, the description of the curves given above together with Lemma
7.5
implies that, if we choose the orientation correctly,
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}
(resp.
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}
) can be deformed to
C(I_{\mathsf{b}_{n}})
(resp.
C(I_{\mathsf{w}_{n}})
) in
\Sigma\backslash\cup_{e\in I_{{\mathsf{b}_{n}},-}}\bm{\alpha}_{e}
(resp.
\Sigma\backslash\cup_{e\in I_{{\mathsf{w}_{n}},+}}\bm{\alpha}_{e}
); see Figure
28
.

∎

Remark 7.8
.

Note that if
(x,y)
lies in a frozen region
\mathcal{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
at a concave vertex as in Figure
27
(B), the steepest descent and ascent curves are fundamentally different depending on which side of the dashed line the point
(x,y)
lies on; see Figure
28
(B)–(C). When
(x,y)\in\mathcal{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}\cap\left(\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}\cup\bm{l}_{e(\beta)}\right)
, the analysis therefore becomes sensitive to the precise locations of
\mathsf{b}_{n}
and
\mathsf{w}_{n}
. This is the reason that we exclude this case in our analysis.

Proof of Theorem
7.2
.

We use the convention in Remark
4.2
, so that

\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}^{-1}=\iint_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}_{n}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{b}_{n}})}\omega_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}-\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}_{n}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}\rangle.

(7.3)

Let
I_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}=[\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{n}},r_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}]
. Then, for sufficiently large
n
,

-\langle\partial I_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}\cap I_{\mathsf{w}_{n}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}\rangle=\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}\in I_{\mathsf{w}_{n}}\}}\mathsf{A}^{\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}}_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{r_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}\in I_{\mathsf{w}_{n}}\}}\mathsf{A}^{r_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}}_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}=\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}^{\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}}.

(7.4)

Indeed, by the choice of
I_{\mathsf{w}_{n}}
and
I_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}
in Remark
4.2
, and by Lemma
7.5
,
r_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}\notin I_{\mathsf{w}_{n}}
. If
\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}\notin I_{\mathsf{w}_{n}}
,
\operatorname{sign}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}}(e)=+
and
\operatorname{sign}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{w}_{n}}}(e)=-
, where
e
is the edge immediately to the left of
\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}
, so
\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}\in\mathfrak{N}_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}
by Lemma
4.8
, and, hence,
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}^{\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}}=0
.

By Lemma
7.7
, we may deform
C(I_{\mathsf{w}_{n}})
to
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}
and
C(I_{\mathsf{b}_{n}})
to
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}
in the double contour integral in (
7.3
) without crossing any poles of
\omega_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}
other than the diagonal
\zeta=\eta
. Thus, the contribution of this deformation is precisely the diagonal residue along the curve
C_{\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}}^{\zeta_{*}}
defined in Definition
2.15
. Therefore,

\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}^{-1}=\int_{C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}}\int_{C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}}\omega_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}}^{\zeta_{*}}}g_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}+\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}^{\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}}.

(7.5)

Since

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}}^{\zeta_{*}}}g_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}+\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}^{\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{n}}}=\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}^{\zeta_{*}}=\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{\zeta_{*}},

(7.6)

it remains to prove that the double contour integral tends to zero as
n\to\infty
.

Recall the action function
\Lambda_{c}(\cdot;x,y)
from Definition
6.2
. Expanding all the factors, we write the kernel (
4.1
) as

\omega_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\frac{\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}+\eta-\zeta)\theta(t+\eta+\bm{d}(\mathsf{w}_{n}))\theta(-t+\zeta-\bm{d}(\mathsf{b}_{n}))}{E(\zeta,\eta)\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})}\frac{\prod_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}E(\alpha,\eta)^{\left(\bm{d}(\mathsf{w}_{n})+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}\right)_{\alpha}}}{\prod_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}E(\alpha,\zeta)^{\left(\bm{d}(\mathsf{b}_{n})+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}\right)_{\alpha}}}.

(7.7)

By (
6.4
), the prime-form factor in (
7.7
) is

\exp\Bigl(n\bigl(\Lambda_{c}(\eta;x,y)-\Lambda_{c}(\zeta;x,y)\bigr)+O(1)\Bigr).

Since
d\Lambda_{c}(\cdot;x,y)=\lambda_{c}(x,y)
and
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
is imaginary-normalized, the real part
\Re\Lambda_{c}(\cdot;x,y)
is well-defined on
\Sigma
, and

\Re\Lambda_{c}(\zeta;x,y)-\Re\Lambda_{c}(\eta;x,y)=\Re\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\zeta}\lambda_{c}(x,y)-\Re\int_{\zeta_{0}}^{\eta}\lambda_{c}(x,y).

(7.8)

It follows that the curves
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}
and
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}
are steepest ascent and descent contours for the action function
\Lambda_{c}(\cdot;x,y)
. Standard arguments (that go back to
[
97
]
), using that the first factor in (
7.7
) is bounded in
n
since the ovals are compact, show that

\int_{C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}}\int_{C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}}\omega_{\mathsf{b}_{n},\mathsf{w}_{n}}\to 0

(7.9)

as
n\to\infty
, which proves the theorem.
∎

7.2
The limit shape

Let
h_{M}
be the height function corresponding to a dimer cover
M
defined on
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}
with the extremal dimer cover
M_{0}:=M_{u_{0}}
corresponding to
u_{0}\in V(N)
as reference dimer cover. We choose the reference face
\mathsf{f}_{0}\in F({\mathsf{G}})\backslash F(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})
to be adjacent to
\mathsf{e}_{u_{0}}\in E(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})
, where
\mathsf{e}_{u_{0}}
is the intersection of two boundary zig-zag paths as in Section
3.1
. The limit shape is the appropriate scaled large
n
limit of the height function: Let
(j_{n},k_{n})=n(x,y)+O(1)
for some
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
and set
\mathsf{f}_{n}=\mathsf{f}_{0}+(j_{n},k_{n})\in F(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})
. The limit shape
\mathfrak{h}
is defined as

\mathfrak{h}(x,y):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{n}h_{M}(\mathsf{f}_{n})\right].

(7.10)

For dimer models with periodic edge weights, it is well known that the limit shape exists, is the solution to a variational problem, and that
\frac{1}{n}h_{M}\to\mathfrak{h}
in probability as
n\to\infty

[
38
,
64
,
79
,
86
]
. This is also expected to be true for Fock’s weights with periodic angles
[
18
]
.

We now give an integral formula for the limit shape.

Theorem 7.9
.

Assume that the angle function is periodic.
For
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}\penalty\
,

\mathfrak{h}(x,y)=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta_{*}}}\lambda_{c}(x,y),

(7.11)

where
C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta_{*}}
is the curve going from
\sigma(\zeta_{*})
to
\zeta_{*}
crossing
A_{0}
only once in the component of
A_{0}\backslash\bm{\alpha}
corresponding to
u_{0}
as in Definition
2.15
. The point
\zeta_{*}\in\Sigma_{+}
is defined as in Definition
7.1
if
(x,y)
lies in a phase region and defined by continuity otherwise.

The proof starts by rewriting the expectation of the height function in a form that is suitable for steepest descent analysis, similar to the one carried out in Section
7.1
.
The key identity that allows for this simplification in our setting is given in the following proposition. This identity was proved in
[
12
]
in the study of perfect t-embeddings of Aztec diamonds with Fock’s weights. For the convenience of the reader, we include the proof from
[
12
]
in Appendix
B
.

Proposition 7.10

(
[
12
]
)
.

Let
\mathsf{f}_{+},\mathsf{f}_{-}\in F(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})
be adjacent faces with
\mathsf{w}\mathsf{b}\in E(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})
as their common adjacent edge, and let
\alpha
and
\beta
be the strands containing
\mathsf{w}\mathsf{b}
, as in Figure
8
. Then

E(\zeta,\eta)\frac{g_{\mathsf{w}}(\eta)}{g_{\mathsf{b}}(\zeta)}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}=G_{\mathsf{f}_{+}}(\zeta,\eta)-G_{\mathsf{f}_{-}}(\zeta,\eta),

(7.12)

where

G_{\mathsf{f}}(\zeta,\eta):=\frac{\theta(-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f})+\zeta-\eta)}{\theta(t+\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}))}\frac{g_{\mathsf{f}}(\eta)}{g_{\mathsf{f}}(\zeta)},

(7.13)

for all faces
\mathsf{f}\in F(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})
and all
\zeta,\eta\in\Sigma
.

Proof of Theorem
7.9
.

We will consider points
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
satisfying the following assumption.

Assumption 7.11
.

Let
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
be a point in a phase region. If
(x,y)\in\mathcal{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
, assume also that
(x,y)\notin\bm{l}_{e(\alpha)}\cup\bm{l}_{e(\beta)}.

In particular, we will prove the limit

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{n}h_{M}(\mathsf{f}_{n})\right]=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta_{*}}}\lambda_{c}(x,y),

(7.14)

for
(x,y)
satisfying Assumption
7.11
. Since
\mathfrak{h}
is a continuous function–indeed, Lipschitz continuous–the statement follows.

By definition of the height function; see Section
2.2.1
, and by Theorems
2.2
and
2.18
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[h_{M}(\mathsf{f}_{n})\right]=\sum_{\mathsf{e}\in E(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})}(\mathsf{e}\wedge\gamma_{n}^{*})\mathbb{E}[\one_{\mathsf{e}\in M}-\one_{\mathsf{e}\in M_{0}}]=\sum_{\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}\in E(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})}(\mathsf{e}\wedge\gamma_{n}^{*})\left(\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}-\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{u_{0}}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}\right),

(7.15)

where
\gamma_{n}^{*}
is a dual path in
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}^{*}
from
\mathsf{f}_{0}
to
\mathsf{f}_{n}
, and
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{u_{0}}
is the whole-plane inverse Kasteleyn matrix defining the Gibbs measure associated with
u_{0}
.

We will first compute the limit of the expected height difference between two faces in the same chamber.
We use the convention for
\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}^{-1}
from Remark
4.2
. Let
\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}
and let
I_{\mathsf{b}}=[\ell_{\mathsf{b}},r_{\mathsf{b}}]
. By Proposition
7.10
and definition of
\mathsf{A}^{\zeta}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}
,

\mathbb{E}[\one_{\mathsf{e}\in M}-\one_{\mathsf{e}\in M_{0}}]=\iint_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{b}})}\omega_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}+\left(\mathsf{A}^{\ell_{\mathsf{b}}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}-\mathsf{A}^{u_{0}}_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\right)\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C([\ell_{\mathsf{b}},u_{0}])}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}\\
+\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\iint_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{b}})}\frac{\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}+\eta-\zeta)}{\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})E(\zeta,\eta)^{2}}\left(G_{\mathsf{f}_{+}}(\zeta,\eta)-G_{\mathsf{f}_{-}}(\zeta,\eta)\right)\frac{\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\eta)}{\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\zeta)},

(7.16)

where
\mathsf{f}_{-},\mathsf{f}_{+}\in F(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})
are as in Figure
8
.

More generally, let
\mathsf{f}_{-},\mathsf{f}_{+}\in F(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})
be any two faces in the same chamber
\mathsf{R}
, and let
\gamma^{*}
be a dual path in
\mathsf{R}
going from
\mathsf{f}_{-}
to
\mathsf{f}_{+}
keeping white vertices to the left. Then

\mathbb{E}\left[h_{M}(\mathsf{f}_{+})-h_{M}(\mathsf{f}_{-})\right]=\sum_{\mathsf{e}\in E(\mathsf{R})}(\mathsf{e}\wedge\gamma^{*})\mathbb{E}[\one_{\mathsf{e}\in M}-\one_{\mathsf{e}\in M_{0}}]=\sum_{\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}\in E(\mathsf{R})}\frac{(\mathsf{e}\wedge\gamma^{*})}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C([\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{\mathsf{R}}},u_{0}])}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}\\
+\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\iint_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}_{\mathsf{R}}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{b}_{\mathsf{R}}})}\frac{\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}+\eta-\zeta)}{\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})E(\zeta,\eta)^{2}}\left(G_{\mathsf{f}_{+}}(\zeta,\eta)-G_{\mathsf{f}_{-}}(\zeta,\eta)\right)\frac{\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\eta)}{\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\zeta)},

(7.17)

where
\mathsf{b}_{\mathsf{R}}\in B(\mathsf{R})
and
\mathsf{w}_{\mathsf{R}}\in W(\mathsf{R})
are fixed vertices, and we use that the
G_{\mathsf{f}}(\zeta,\eta)
-terms telescope.

We consider first the single integral. If
\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}\in E(\mathsf{R})
, then Lemma
2.10
implies that

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C([\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{\mathsf{R}}},u_{0}])}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C([\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{\mathsf{R}}},u_{0}])}\omega_{\beta-\alpha},

(7.18)

where
\alpha
and
\beta
are the two strands containing
\mathsf{e}
as in Figure
8
. Summing over
\gamma^{*}
yields

\sum_{\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{b}\mathsf{w}\in E(\mathsf{R})}\frac{(\mathsf{e}\wedge\gamma^{*})}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C([\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{\mathsf{R}}},u_{0}])}g_{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C([\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{\mathsf{R}}},u_{0}])}\omega_{\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{+})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{-})}.

(7.19)

This is the height difference between
\mathsf{f}_{+}
and
\mathsf{f}_{-}
for the periodic dimer cover
M_{\ell_{\mathsf{b}_{\mathsf{R}}}}
of
{\mathsf{G}}
with reference dimer cover
M_{0}
. We note that

\omega_{\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{+})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{-})}=\omega_{\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{+})+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{-})-\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}}=\omega_{\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{+})+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}}-\omega_{\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{-})+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}}.

(7.20)

Now we turn to the double contour integral in (
7.17
). For
\mathsf{f}\in F(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})
, let

\omega_{\mathsf{f}}:=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\frac{1}{E(\zeta,\eta)^{2}}\frac{\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}+\eta-\zeta)}{\theta(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})}\frac{\theta(-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f})+\zeta-\eta)}{\theta(t+\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}))}\frac{\prod_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}E(\alpha,\eta)^{\left(\bm{d}(\mathsf{f})+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}\right)_{\alpha}}}{\prod_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}E(\alpha,\zeta)^{\left(\bm{d}(\mathsf{f})+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}\right)_{\alpha}}},

(7.21)

so that the double integral may be written as

\iint_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}_{\mathsf{R}}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{b}_{\mathsf{R}}})}(\omega_{\mathsf{f}_{+}}-\omega_{\mathsf{f}_{-}}).

For
n=1,2,\dots
, let
\mathsf{R}_{n}\subset\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}
be a chamber and assume that
\operatorname{sign}_{\mathsf{R}_{n}}(e)
is independent of
n
if
n
is large enough. Let
\mathsf{f}_{n}=\mathsf{f}_{0}+(j_{n},k_{n})\in F(\mathsf{R}_{n})
with
(j_{n},k_{n})=n(x,y)+O(1)
as
n\to\infty
, where
(x,y)
satisfy Assumption
7.11
. Let
\zeta_{*}=\zeta_{*}(x,y)
be defined by (
7.1
).

Comparing (
7.21
) with (
7.7
) makes it clear that we can reuse the steepest descent analysis performed in Section
7.1
. We deform
C(I_{\mathsf{w}})
to
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}
and
C(I_{\mathsf{b}})
to
C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}
, where the curves are the steepest ascent and descent curves as in Section
7.1
, and
\mathsf{w}=\mathsf{w}_{\mathsf{R}_{n}}
,
\mathsf{b}=\mathsf{b}_{\mathsf{R}_{n}}
. Similarly to the proof of Theorem
7.2
, Lemma
7.7
implies that the only contribution from such deformation comes from the pole at
\zeta=\eta
along the curve
C^{\zeta_{*}}_{\ell_{\mathsf{b}}}
. The pole, however, is now a double pole instead of a simple pole, which leads to a very different contribution along this curve:

\iint_{C(I_{\mathsf{w}})\prec C(I_{\mathsf{b}})}\omega_{\mathsf{f}_{n}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{\ell_{\mathsf{b}}}^{\zeta_{*}}}\omega_{\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{n})+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}}+\int_{C_{(x,y),\mathrm{asc}}}\int_{C_{(x,y),\mathrm{desc}}}\omega_{\mathsf{f}_{n}}\\
+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{k=1}^{g}\left(\frac{\partial\log\theta}{\partial z_{k}}(-t+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})-\frac{\partial\log\theta}{\partial z_{k}}(-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{n}))\right)\int_{C_{\ell_{\mathsf{b}}}^{\zeta_{*}}}\omega_{k}.

(7.22)

Indeed, the residue at the double pole is computed by differentiating the regular part of the integrand in (
7.21
) and then setting
\zeta=\eta
. The derivative of the factor involving the prime forms gives the first term in (
7.22
) by (
2.2
), whereas the derivative of the theta-function factor gives the third term. By the periodicity of
\theta(z)
for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{g}
, the third term is bounded in
n
. The second term on the right-hand side of (
7.22
) is likewise bounded in
n
, by standard steepest descent arguments. Therefore, as
n\to\infty
, the main contribution to (
7.22
) comes from the first term on the right-hand side.

Let
\mathsf{f}_{n,+}=\mathsf{f}_{0}+(i_{n,+},j_{n,+})\in F(\mathsf{R}_{n})
and
\mathsf{f}_{n,-}=\mathsf{f}_{0}+(i_{n,-},j_{n,-})\in F(\mathsf{R}_{n})
with
(i_{n,+},j_{n,+})=n(x_{+},y_{+})+O(1)
and
(i_{n,-},j_{n,-})=n(x_{-},y_{-})+O(1)
as
n\to\infty
, for some
(x_{+},y_{+}),(x_{-},y_{-})\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
satisfying Assumption
7.11
. The assumption on the limiting points is a technical condition ensuring that we can deform the integration contours to the corresponding steepest descent and ascent curves; see Remark
7.8
.
Let
\zeta_{+}=\zeta_{*}(x_{+},y_{+})
be defined by (
7.1
), and similarly
\zeta_{-}=\zeta_{*}(x_{-},y_{-})
. Then, by (
7.17
), (
7.19
), (
7.20
), and (
7.22
),

\mathbb{E}\left[h_{M}(\mathsf{f}_{n,+})-h_{M}(\mathsf{f}_{n,-})\right]=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C([\ell_{\mathsf{b}},u_{0}])}\omega_{\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{n,+})-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{n,-})}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{\ell_{\mathsf{b}}}^{\zeta_{+}}}\omega_{\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{n,+})+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}}\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{\ell_{\mathsf{b}}}^{\zeta_{-}}}\omega_{\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{n,-})+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}}+O(1)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta_{+}}}\omega_{\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{n,+})+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}}-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta_{-}}}\omega_{\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{n,-})+\bm{D}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}}+O(1),

(7.23)

as
n\to\infty
. By Lemma
6.3
we conclude that, as
n\to\infty
,

\mathbb{E}\left[h_{M}(\mathsf{f}_{n,+})-h_{M}(\mathsf{f}_{n,-})\right]=-n\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta_{+}}}\lambda_{c}(x_{+},y_{+})+n\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta_{-}}}\lambda_{c}(x_{-},y_{-})+O(1).

(7.24)

Thus, the height difference is consistent with (
7.11
) when
(x_{+},y_{+})
and
(x_{-},y_{-})
lie in the same chamber. To treat the general case, we connect the two points by a curve and decompose the height difference into a sum of contributions from the chambers crossed by the curve. More precisely, we split the curve into long segments lying inside single chambers and short segments crossing the separating lines between chambers. The long segments are handled by (
7.24
), and we will show that the short segments contribute negligibly.

Let
(x,y)
satisfy Assumption
7.11
and let
\mathsf{f}_{n}=\mathsf{f}_{0}+(j_{n},k_{n})\in F(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})
be such that
(j_{n},k_{n})=n(x,y)+O(1)
as
n\to\infty
. Pick a curve
\Gamma
in
\mathcal{D}_{c}
from
(0,0)
to
(x,y)
, intersecting the lines
\bm{l}_{e}
,
e\in E(N)
, at a finite number of locations
(x_{k},y_{k})
,
k=1,\dots,m-1
. We assume that these intersections are transverse, that each point
(x_{k},y_{k})
lies on exactly one line
\bm{l}_{e}
with
e\in E(N)
, and that the points
(x_{k},y_{k})
satisfy Assumption
7.11
. The last assumption is possible because the rough region is locally convex, so
\mathscr{A}
intersects each line
\bm{l}_{e}
only finitely many times.

If
(x_{k},y_{k})\in\bm{l}_{e}
, let
R_{k}
and
R_{k+1}
be the 2-cells adjacent to the part of
\bm{l}_{e}
containing
(x_{k},y_{k})
, so that
\Gamma
enters
(x_{k},y_{k})
from
R_{k}
and exits into
R_{k+1}
. For large enough
n
, Lemma
7.5
implies that, for
k=1,\dots,{m}
, there is a chamber
\mathsf{R}_{n,k}
in
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}
such that

\operatorname{sign}_{\mathsf{R}_{n,k}}(e)=\operatorname{sign}_{R_{k}}(e),

(7.25)

for all
e\in E(N)
. Let

\mathsf{f}_{n,k,+}=\mathsf{f}_{0}+(i_{n,k,+},j_{n,k,+})\in F(\mathsf{R}_{n,k})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{f}_{n,k,-}=\mathsf{f}_{0}+(i_{n,k,-},j_{n,k,-})\in F(\mathsf{R}_{n,k+1}),

(7.26)

where

(i_{n,k,+},j_{n,k,+})=n(x_{k},y_{k})+O(1),\quad(i_{n,k,-},j_{n,k,-})=n(x_{k},y_{k})+O(1)\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty.

(7.27)

We also set
\mathsf{f}_{n,0,+}=\mathsf{f}_{0}
,
\mathsf{f}_{n,m,-}=\mathsf{f}_{n}
,

\mathsf{f}_{n,0,-}=\mathsf{f}_{0}+(i_{n,0,-},j_{n,0,-})\in F(\mathsf{R}_{n,1})\quad\text{with}\quad(i_{n,0,-},j_{n,0,-})=O(1)\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty,

(7.28)

and

\mathsf{f}_{n,m,+}=\mathsf{f}_{0}+(i_{n,m,+},j_{n,m,+})\in F(\mathsf{R}_{n,m})\quad\text{with}\quad(i_{n,m,+},j_{n,m,+})=n(x,y)+O(1)\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty.

(7.29)

For
k=1,\dots,m
, we define the dual path
\gamma_{n,k,\mathrm{l}}^{*}
contained in
\mathsf{R}_{n,k}
going from
\mathsf{f}_{n,k-1,-}
to
\mathsf{f}_{n,k,+}
, and for
k=0,\dots,m
, we define the dual path
\gamma_{n,k,\mathrm{s}}^{*}
going from
\mathsf{f}_{n,k,+}
to
\mathsf{f}_{n,k,-}
. Here
\mathrm{l}
and
\mathrm{s}
stand for long and short respectively. The concatenation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{f}_{0}=\mathsf{f}_{n,0,+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{f}_{n,0,-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{f}_{n,1,+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{f}_{n,1,-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{f}_{n,m,+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{f}_{n,m,-}=\mathsf{f}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x_{1},y_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x,y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{n,0,\mathrm{s}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{n,1,\mathrm{l}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{n,1,\mathrm{s}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{n,2,\mathrm{l}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{n,m,\mathrm{l}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{n,m,\mathrm{s}}^{*}}

of these paths defines a dual path
\gamma_{n}^{*}
from
\mathsf{f}_{0}
to
\mathsf{f}_{n}
, where the downward arrows indicate the points to which the faces converge in the scaling limit.

We first bound the part of (
7.15
) coming from the curves
\gamma_{n,k,\mathrm{s}}^{*}
. By (
7.27
), we may pick
\gamma_{n,k,\mathrm{s}}^{*}
so that its length is uniformly bounded for all
n
. We obtain

\sum_{\mathsf{e}\in E(\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}})}(\mathsf{e}\wedge\gamma_{n,k,\mathrm{s}}^{*})\mathbb{E}[\one_{\mathsf{e}\in M}-\one_{\mathsf{e}\in M_{0}}]=O(1)

(7.30)

as
n\to\infty
, for
k=0,\dots,m
.

We continue by summing over the curves
\gamma_{n,k,\mathrm{l}}^{*}
. For
k=1,\dots,m-1
, let
\zeta_{k}=\zeta_{*}(x_{k},y_{k})
be given by (
7.1
). Let
{\zeta_{0}}\in A_{0}\backslash\bm{\alpha}
be any point in the component corresponding to
u_{0}
. Then, by (
7.24
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[h_{M}(\mathsf{f}_{n,k,+})-h_{M}(\mathsf{f}_{n,k-1,-})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta_{k}}}\lambda_{c}(x_{k},y_{k})+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta_{k-1}}}\lambda_{c}(x_{k-1},y_{k-1})+O(\frac{1}{n})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta_{0}}}\lambda_{c}(0,0)-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta_{*}}}\lambda_{c}(x,y)+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right),

as
n\to\infty
. Since the curve
C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta_{0}}
does not enclose any angles, the above equality proves Theorem
7.9
.
∎

Using the formulation (
6.2
) for
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
, we see that the slope of
\mathfrak{h}
is

\nabla\mathfrak{h}(x,y)=\left(-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta_{*}}}d\log z,-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C_{u_{0}}^{\zeta_{*}}}d\log w\right).

(7.31)

The right-hand side agrees with the slope (
2.11
) of the Gibbs measure
\mathbb{P}^{\zeta_{*}}
. We are led to the following corollary of Theorems
7.2
and
7.9
.

Corollary 7.12
.

Assume that the angle function is periodic, and
suppose
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\circ}
lies in a phase region and satisfies Assumption
7.11
. The local correlations in a neighborhood of
(x,y)
of the dimer model on
\mathsf{G}_{\bm{\beta}^{n}}
defined by the Kasteleyn matrix (
2.5
) converge to those of the Gibbs measure defined by the corresponding whole-plane Kasteleyn matrix with slope
\nabla\mathfrak{h}(x,y)
.

Remark 7.13
.

In the case of the Aztec diamond, for
(x,y)\in\mathcal{G}_{j}
,
j=1,\dots,g
, the part of the integral (
7.11
) coming from the third term in expression (
6.2
) for
\lambda_{c}(x,y)
, namely

-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{B_{j}}\omega_{\bm{D}_{c}},

(7.32)

with
\bm{D}_{c}
as in (
6.1
), turned out to be important in the description of the global height fluctuations
[
16
, Equation (6)]
. It is therefore natural to expect that the same quantity will play a similar role in the global height fluctuations of AZ graphs.

Appendix A
Tropical limits and simulations

In this appendix, we explain how certain astroidal domains arise as local pieces of the tropical limit of the Aztec diamond with periodic weights. This allows us to use domino shuffling on the Aztec diamond
[
102
]
to simulate limit shapes in more general astroidal domains.

Let
{\mathsf{G}}
be a minimal periodic bipartite graph and let
p:{\mathsf{G}}\to\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}
denote the quotient map to the corresponding graph
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}
on the torus. There is a construction, called the
spectral transform

[
80
]
, which allows one to view any periodic choice of edge weights on
{\mathsf{G}}
(equivalently, edge weights on
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}
) as gauge-equivalent to Fock’s dimer model for an M-curve
\Sigma
,
t\in\operatorname{Jac}(\Sigma)
, and angle function
\nu

[
53
,
27
]
. We apply this to the family of edge weights on
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}
of the form

\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{e};\tau)=w(\mathsf{e})\exp(\tau\mathcal{E}(\mathsf{e})),

where

w:E(\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}})\to\mathbb{R}_{>0},\qquad\mathcal{E}:E(\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}})\to\mathbb{R},\qquad\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

The corresponding M-curve
\Sigma_{\tau}
is an explicit algebraic curve given by the vanishing locus of the
characteristic polynomial

\mathsf{P}(z,w;\tau):=\sum_{(j,k)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\pm\left(\sum_{\text{dimer covers $M$ on $\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}$}:(h^{y}_{M},-h^{x}_{M})=(j,k)}\left(\prod_{\mathsf{e}\in M}\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{e};\tau)\right)z^{j}w^{k}\right),

which is a Laurent polynomial in the variables
z,w
with Newton polygon equal to
N

[
79
]
. Here
\pm
denotes a sign that depends only on
(j,k)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
h_{M}^{x}
and
h_{M}^{y}
are the height changes of
M
along dual paths with homology classes
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
respectively.

The limit as
\tau\to\infty
is called the tropical limit; see
[
69
,
91
]
for a general introduction to tropical geometry. The
tropical characteristic polynomial
is defined as

\mathsf{P}^{t}(x,y):=\max_{(j,k)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\left(\max_{\text{dimer covers $M$ on $\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{T}}$}:(h^{y}_{M},-h^{x}_{M})=(j,k)}\left(\sum_{\mathsf{e}\in M}\mathcal{E}(\mathsf{e})\right)+jx+ky\right).

(A.1)

The fundamental theorem of tropical geometry
[
50
]
states that, as
\tau\to\infty
, the rescaled
amoeba

\frac{1}{\tau}\{(\log|z|,\log|w|)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\mathsf{P}(z,w;\tau)=0\}

converges in the Hausdorff metric to the
tropical spectral curve

\Sigma^{t}:=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\text{the maximum over $(j,k)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}$ in\penalty\ \eqref{eq:trop_char_poly} is achieved at least twice}\}.

The graph of the tropical characteristic polynomial induces a regular subdivision of the Newton polygon
N
which is dual to
\Sigma^{t}
(see Figure
31
). The tropical curve is called
smooth
if all
2
-cells of the subdivision are unimodular triangles. We illustrate, by means of an example, how the arctic curve can be computed in the tropical limit by a generalization of the results of
[
10
]
. Their setting was for smooth tropical curves, whereas here we consider a non-smooth tropical curve.

\exp({-\tau})

:
A
(2\times 3)
-fundamental domain of the square lattice with a family of edge weights (left), and the corresponding limiting graph obtained by deleting the edges whose weights vanish in the tropical limit (right). This particular choice of weights was obtained via the tropical inverse spectral transform developed in the forthcoming work
[

]
.

(left). The Newton polygon

and the function

for the scaling limit of the Aztec diamond, are given by

:
The tropical spectral curve with the integral lengths of its edges (left), and the dual regular subdivision of the Newton polygon with a shaded pentagonal
2
-cell (right).

The tropical curve for this family of edge weights is shown in Figure
31
(left).

Figure 32
:
The harmonic
1
-form on
\Sigma^{t}
(left), and the image of
(\Omega^{-1})^{t}
in
\mathcal{D}_{c}
(right).

A
discrete
1
-form

\omega
on
\Sigma^{t}
is an antisymmetric function on the oriented edges of
\Sigma^{t}
. It is called
harmonic
if it is

•

closed
,
i.e.
for every face
f
of
\Sigma^{t}
,

\sum_{e\subset\partial f}|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}\omega(e)=0,

where the edges in
\partial f
are oriented counterclockwise, and

•

co-closed
,
i.e.
for every vertex
u\in V(\Sigma^{t})
,

\sum_{e=uv}\omega(e)=0,

where the sum is over all edges incident to
u
, each oriented from
u
to
v
.

Given any outflows on the unbounded edges of
\Sigma^{t}
that sum to
0
, there is a unique harmonic
1
-form with those boundary values. Taking the boundary values given by the function
c
, we obtain the harmonic
1
-form shown in Figure
32
(left).

The map
\Omega^{-1}
tropicalizes, in the limit
\tau\to\infty
, to the map

(\Omega^{-1})^{t}:\Sigma^{t}\to\mathcal{D}_{c}

sending
v\in V(\Sigma^{t})
to the point
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that

\frac{1}{|e|_{\mathbb{Z}}}\langle(x,y),e\rangle+\omega(e)=0

(A.2)

for every edge
e
of
\Sigma^{t}
incident to
v
, and sending non-boundary edges of
\Sigma^{t}
to the corresponding line segments between the images of their endpoints. Its image is shown in Figure
32
(right). Note that the degree-
5
vertex
O
maps to a pentagonal astroidal domain
\mathcal{D}_{O}
bounded by the lines defined by (
A.2
). The condition that
\omega
is co-closed at
O
is equivalent to the admissibility condition for
\mathcal{D}_{O}
.

Letting
\tau\to\infty
and deleting edges with zero weight, we obtain the graph shown on the right-hand side of Figure
30
, which is a minimal graph for the pentagonal Newton polygon shown shaded in Figure
31
(right). Its M-curve
\Sigma_{\infty}
is given by the vanishing of the
initial polynomial

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\mathsf{P}(z,w;\tau).

Our choice of weights is such that
\Sigma_{\infty}
has genus
0
, and the weights
\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{e};\infty)
are isoradial weights as in Section
4.3
. As in that section, the prime form is given by

E(\zeta,\eta)=\frac{\eta-\zeta}{\sqrt{d\zeta}\sqrt{d\eta}}.

Thus, the functions in (
6.7
) can be written explicitly and globally as

f_{1}(\zeta)=-\sum_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}b_{e(\alpha)}\log(\zeta-\alpha),\qquad f_{2}(\zeta)=\sum_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}a_{e(\alpha)}\log(\zeta-\alpha),\qquad f_{3}(\zeta)=\sum_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}c{(e(\alpha))}\log(\zeta-\alpha).

Choosing the angles

\alpha=1,\qquad\beta=e^{\pi\mathrm{i}/25},\qquad\gamma=e^{4\pi\mathrm{i}/5},\qquad\delta=e^{6\pi\mathrm{i}/5},\qquad\epsilon=e^{8\pi\mathrm{i}/5},

and using the parametrization (
6.12
), we obtain the arctic curve in
\mathcal{D}_{O}
shown in Figure
6
(right). For comparison, a simulation of the Aztec diamond is shown in Figure
6
(left).

Appendix B
Proof of Proposition
7.10

The following proof is reproduced from
[
12
]
.
By the product form (
2.6
) of
g
, we have

g_{\mathsf{x}}=g_{\mathsf{f}_{+}}\,g_{\mathsf{f}_{+},\mathsf{x}},\qquad\mathsf{x}\in\{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{w},\mathsf{f}_{-},\mathsf{f}_{+}\}.

Canceling the
g_{\mathsf{f}_{+}}
-factors, it suffices to prove that

\displaystyle E(\zeta,\eta){\frac{g_{\mathsf{f}_{+},\mathsf{w}}(\eta)}{g_{\mathsf{f}_{+},\mathsf{b}}(\zeta)}}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta(-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{+})+\zeta-\eta)}{\theta(t+\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{+}))}

(B.1)

\displaystyle-\frac{\theta(-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{-})+\zeta-\eta)}{\theta(t+\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{-}))}\frac{E(\beta,\zeta)}{E(\alpha,\zeta)}\frac{E(\alpha,\eta)}{E(\beta,\eta)}.

By definition,

\displaystyle E(\zeta,\eta){\frac{g_{\mathsf{f}_{+},\mathsf{w}}(\eta)}{g_{\mathsf{f}_{+},\mathsf{b}}(\zeta)}}\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta(-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{b})+\zeta)\,\theta(t+\bm{d}(\mathsf{w})+\eta)\,E(\alpha,\beta)\,E(\zeta,\eta)}{\theta(t+\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{+}))\,\theta(t+\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{-}))\,E(\alpha,\zeta)\,E(\beta,\eta)}.

We now apply Fay’s trisecant identity
[
51
]
(see also
[
27
, Theorem 27]
): for
z\in\mathbb{C}^{g}
and
a,b,c,d\in\Sigma
,

\displaystyle\theta(z+c-a)\theta(z+d-b)E(c,b)E(a,d)+\theta(z+c-b)\theta(z+d-a)E(c,a)E(d,b)

\displaystyle=\theta(z+c+d-a-b)\theta(z)E(c,d)E(a,b).

Dividing by
\theta(z)\theta(z+d-a)E(c,a)E(d,b)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\theta(z+c-a)\theta(z+d-b)E(c,b)E(a,d)}{\theta(z)\theta(z+d-a)E(c,a)E(d,b)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta(z+c+d-a-b)E(c,d)E(a,b)}{\theta(z+d-a)E(c,a)E(d,b)}-\frac{\theta(z+c-b)}{\theta(z)}.

Now set

z=-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{+}),\qquad a=\alpha,\qquad b=\eta,\qquad c=\zeta,\qquad d=\beta.

Using

z-a=-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{b}),\qquad z+d=-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{w}),\qquad z+d-a=-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{-}),

we get

\displaystyle\frac{\theta(-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{b})+\zeta)\,\theta(-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{w})-\eta)\,E(\zeta,\eta)\,E(\alpha,\beta)}{\theta(-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{+}))\,\theta(-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{-}))\,E(\zeta,\alpha)\,E(\beta,\eta)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta(-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{-})+\zeta-\eta)\,E(\zeta,\beta)\,E(\alpha,\eta)}{\theta(-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{-}))\,E(\zeta,\alpha)\,E(\beta,\eta)}-\frac{\theta(-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{+})+\zeta-\eta)}{\theta(-t-\bm{d}(\mathsf{f}_{+}))}.

Finally, using that the Riemann theta function is even and that the prime form is antisymmetric, the previous identity simplifies to (
B.1
). This proves the proposition.
∎
